# Varṇāśrama Compendium

## Preface

This book, like the others in the Varṇāśrama Compendium series to follow, is written specifically for the audience of the Hungarian yatra. Its authors are the Kozgyules (Hungarian National Council), and its application is in the Magyarorszagi Krisna-tudatu Hivok Kozossege (MKTHK), or the Hungarian yatra. I originally wrote the book in English and purposefully did not de-Hungarianise it so as to maintain the flavour of practically applying the principles of varṇāśrama in a geographical locale, a yatra. I thought that the benefit to the readers of seeing this practical application outweighed the drawback of foreign reference.

The principles of varṇāśrama are eternal, whereas its application is variable according to time, place, and circumstance. Therefore the form of varṇāśrama today will be different from that of Yudhisthira Maharaja's time. Similarly, the varṇāśrama structure that we are developing in Hungary may differ from what others set in motion in China or Canada, for example. After all, the places and circumstances differ. However, the eternal principles of varṇāśrama will be the same and thus in time ISKCON will have fulfilled its worldwide remit to organise itself along the lines of daiva-varṇāśrama. Having done that, our movement can then inspire world leaders to follow our model.

What a challenge! I hope that the form of this book will help devotees to apply the principles of varna and asrama in their yatra.

Sivarama Swami  
January 18, 2016

## Introduction. How This Book Came To Be

In January 2015, at their annual meeting, the members of the Hungarian National Council (Kozgyules) discussed how to implement Srila Prabhupada's desire to establish varṇāśrama-dharma in both the devotee and the non-devotee societies of Hungary.

Thirty-five devotees met for three full days, hearing Srila Prabhupada's recorded instructions, reading Srimad-Bhagavatam, and discussing what varṇāśrama and its implementation actually meant. We realised it was no small task!

But we understood that Srila Prabhupada had said that it was the unfinished fifty-per cent of his mission. While some devotees in ISKCON question the truth of that understanding, there is ample first-hand evidence to substantiate it. For our readers we present two such pieces of evidence:

Describing an early-morning encounter with Srila Prabhupada on August 10th 1977, Bhakti Caru Maharaja narrates:

**"One night Srila Prabhupada could not sleep, and I was massaging his feet. At that time he was lamenting that his health was so bad and he had so much to do. I tried to console him saying, 'Srila Prabhupada, what you have done is beyond anyone s imagination. You should not feel that there is anything more to be done. Now you should just relax.’**

**"Then Srila Prabhupada told me, 'What I have done is fifty-percent; the other fifty-percent is to establish varṇāśrama. Not that everyone will become a devotee. Those who won't become devotees, for them we need varṇāśrama.'"**

Abhirama Prabhu, who was Srila Prabhupada's nurse in 1977, narrates two occasions when he heard Srila Prabhupada say a similar thing:

**"The first time, several devotees were with Srila Prabhupada, and we knew that his health was getting weaker. He was talking to the devotees about his imminent departure. ‘I have no lamentation,’ Prabhupada said, then paused for a few seconds and added, 'No, I have one lamentation.'**

**"Tamal Kṛṣṇa Goswami asked, 'That you have not finished translating Srimad-Bhagavatam?'**

**"Srila Prabhupada replied, 'No, that I have not established varṇāśrama.'**

**On the next occasion, some time later, I was with Srila Prabhupada in his room when he made the statement, 'Fifty-percent of my work is not complete because I have not established varṇāśrama.'"**

While some devotees in ISKCON may remain sceptical of the importance of varṇāśrama, the majority are increasingly convinced of the need to realise Srila Prabhupada's mission. In Hungary, devotees and their leaders unitedly share this majority concern. And so it was with the Kozgyules.

We are Srila Prabhupada's followers and servants, and so we are duty-bound to make an attempt to fulfil his desires. From all evidence it is clear that His Divine Grace wanted his followers to establish varṇāśrama-dharma—in ISKCON and then in the world.

But where would we start? For the past decade the members of the Hungarian yatra had dedicated themselves to fulfilling the first 50% of Srila Prabhupada's mission, and by his own admission Srila Prabhupada had not given us a varṇāśrama template to follow. In fact there is some indication that even Srila Prabhupada felt he needed to give thought to what the second half of his mission was. In this regard Abhirama Prabhu wrote,

**"I said, 'Srila Prabhupada, you mentioned that you had wanted to establish varṇāśrama. How will you establish varṇāśrama?' and he responded, 'I will go to Gita-nagari, sit down and teach you how to live off the land.'**

**"Being present on this occasion and the words being directly aimed at me, I understood this in a certain way... I gained some grasp of his way of speaking in these informal and practical settings.**

**"Reflecting on his words, I believe that one part of the phrase may be a little hidden and interesting. By His Divine Grace saying 'I will sit down' it is my personal speculation that this meant he did not yet know everything he would do or instruct. That it was something he would need to 'sit down' and observe, reflect on and gain insights from Paramatma, as to how to 'establish varṇāśrama.' He had not yet done this, so far as we know, since not that much detail is given in his writing. Thus, it is now for us to 'sit down' and learn how we can 'live off the land'."**

Following Abhirama Prabhu's reasoning, if Srila Prabhupada had to work out a game plan for varṇāśrama, we should not expect to—or be expected to—work out a master plan overnight. We, too, would have to "sit down" and reflect, and probably quite a lot. While even collectively we did not equal a fraction of Srila Prabhupada's Krsna conscious realisation, we were convinced that we had his order and the empowerment that came along with that order. And to satisfy that order we had to start somewhere.

Discussing the emphasis of what the Kozgyules had heard and read at their meeting, we concluded that varṇāśrama began with two things. The first was acquiring the qualities and following the duties incumbent upon every member of the Society, and the second was identifying which was the varna and asrama of each member. In regards to the latter it was also essential that the distinctive qualities and duties of the varnas and asramas were clearly delineated, and guidelines prepared for cultivating those qualities and learning those duties.

Defining and introducing that first step would take place in 2015, and education and implementation would begin the following year.

Since regulating sense gratification is one of the aims of varṇāśrama, the National Council also decided that the relationship and conduct among men and women would be the next step. Studying and introducing that topic would take place in 2016, and education and implementation the following year. In this way we envisaged a step-by-step, five-year plan as a strategic beginning to our varṇāśrama mission.

We did not know what the further steps of the plan would be, what to speak of what lay beyond them, but we were confident that if we moved forward, Srila Prabhupada and Radha-Syama would guide us.

To this end the National Council also established a Varṇāśrama-dharma Committee (vad).That committee was deputed with the task of working out introductory material, presenting that to all the centres, and setting up a network of varṇāśrama representatives throughout the country. And by the end of the year it was meant to bring to the National Council a manuscript that fulfilled its first year's remit: a handbook that outlined the qualities and conduct of devotees in each of the four varnas.

This is that handbook—the first in the Varṇāśrama Compendium series—and its title is The Varnas. It's central theme is about being a good person and being a good devotee. Although being a good person is not a prerequesite to becoming a devotee, if one is still a conditioned soul, being a good person helps one to be a good devotee.

Why?

Because devotional service is most easy and most effective when practitioners are situated in the mode of goodness. By practising bhakti in goodness, devotees can quickly rise to the transcendental platform, where the good qualities of demigods spontaneously manifest. Varṇāśrama for devotees is about helping them come to the mode of goodness. When we cultivate the qualities and follow the duties of our varnas and asramas, then we are graced by the mode of goodness and the benefits it bestows upon us.

The contents of this book will be systematically taught to members of Magyarorszagi Krisna-tudatu Hivok Kozossege (MKTHK), in the Bhaktivedanta College, bhakta programs, gurukulas, and counsellor groups—and hopefully in devotees' homes. We envisage that this will be the basis upon which devotees are guided in their service, work, and asramas. Moreover, it should remove any subjectivity about what it means to be a good, responsible person and devotee.

Our hope is that members of MKTHK will themselves take seriously the mission left to us by Srila Prabhupada and begin their journey in varṇāśrama by reading, personally applying, and teaching to others the contents of this book.

Thank you.

Sivarama Swami  
On behalf of the Hungarian National Council  
Indira Ekadasi, October 8, 2015

## Part One. About This Book

## Chapter One. The Compendium and This Volume

**The Compendium**

This handbook is the first instalment of publications that will define and regulate the implementation of varṇāśrama for members of MKTHK. It is not for the general public but for practising devotees.

The subject matters of this first volume are the qualities and duties of devotees in general, as well as those specific to each varna. By developing the qualities and duties of their varna, devotees can feel confident that they are living in the world in a way that is pleasing to Krsna and which facilitates their Krsna consciousness.

Varṇāśrama-dharma is not about pigeon-holing devotees according to their qualities and work. It is about helping devotees cultivate those qualities, perfect their service to Krsna, and practise their profession in the most dharmic way. Since this handbook is meant to be concise, these practices are described succinctly, the in-depth explanations being left to the education process that will follow this volume.

Devotees who have or have not embraced a varna may use the online varna-finder to see what kind of work is most suited to their nature. The link to the varna-finder can be found in chapter 30. For those who are still either volunteers or students, this will be a helpful guide to their future. Moreover, devotees who want to see their suitability for brahmacari, sannyasa, or grhastha life can do so with a similar asrama-finder.

Codes of conduct are generally accompanied by consequences for their breach. That is not the case with this compendium, as we had neither the time for such an undertaking at present, nor a way to comprehensively implement sanctions. The subject of sanctions and their enforcement, like other subjects, may be added to revised editions of this handbook at later dates.

In conclusion, readers should note that this handbook is not meant to be an exhaustive exposition of its subjects: it is an introduction. And while not complete, it is the standard for our devotee society until it is revised. This is the same as the process of law-making in our country. Whatever form a law is in, it is incumbent on all citizens to follow it, and the current form of a law is the basis by which citizens are judged. That law may be repeatedly amended or even repealed, but whatever form it stands in at a given time, that is the law.

Devotees may question the statements in this handbook or fault its delivery. Such issues can be discussed with the local varṇāśrama representative, who will then bring that information to the varṇāśrama council for deliberation and response.

Krsna consciousness is based on the eternal values found in the Bhagavad-gita and Srimad-Bhagavatam as taught to us by Srila Prabhupada. But because both texts teach the essence of the Vedic literature, they do not deal with themes of religiosity, economic development, and sense gratification. However, devotees—especially householder devotees—come in contact with these aspects of life daily.

Yet we find that there is no systematic body of information that tells devotees, for example, what principles they should work by in business, how much profit should they make, or how they should deal with employees. Nor are devotee business owners or managers clear what conduct and work codes are expected of them as Vaisnavas.

While answering every individual's question requires personal guidance, such guidance must be based on eternal principles, and it is these principles that this compendium presents.

In short, this handbook hopes to extend and strengthen the values by which devotees live, work, play, and worship, as well as offering principles to resolve leading ethical and behavioural issues.

**Scriptural Basis**

The subject of varṇāśrama is dealt with in the dharma-sastra branch of the Vedas. Srila Prabhupada mentions twenty dharma-sastra, of which Manu-samhita is the foremost and likely the most well known.

The study of the Vedas, however, is difficult, and so conclusions drawn by its students may not always be clear or correct. For pure Vaisnavas, therefore, Srimad-Bhagavatam is the preferred first port of call for scriptural guidance. Fortunately, both the Seventh and Eleventh Cantos of Srimad-Bhagavatam deal extensively with varṇāśrama. And for the former we have extensive commentaries by Srila Prabhupada.

Considering the wide scope of Srimad-Bhagavatam's treatment of the qualities and duties of both varnas and asramas, we considered its guidance eminently sufficient for inaugurating this first step of varṇāśrama in the yatra.

The first two volumes of Varṇāśrama Compendium are current dharma-sastra. They deal with the first of the genre's three topics, acara—the appropriate duties and the conduct of the varnas (Volume 1) and asramas (Volume 2). The other two, not dealt with in this book, are vyavahara, judging misconduct, and prayascitta, sanctions and atonements for misconduct.

Both sections of Srimad-Bhagavatam describing varṇāśrama-dharma deal almost exclusively with acara, and so this volume is more or less a synthesis of Narada Muni's and Krsna's teachings on the subject. What this book brings to the fore is the context of Srimad-Bhagavatam's teachings. Whereas Srimad-Bhagavatam speaks to a culture five thousand years ago, this handbook tries to speak to devotees in twenty-first century Hungary.

In addition to Srimad-Bhagavatam, we have referred to other books and statements of Srila Prabhupada and of our acaryas. Nonetheless, Srimad-Bhagavatam remains the primary reference.

We consider ourselves very fortunate that the primary scriptural basis of this book is Srimad-Bhagavatam. Our hope is that we have done justice to our spiritual lineage's greatest scripture.

**Scope**

Laws or guidelines are addressed to an audience. While this handbook addresses all devotees in the Hungarian yatra, not all of them may immediately appreciate that this compendium has a say in almost every aspect of their lives. But varṇāśrama-dharma is all-encompassing. It sets out everyone's conduct and duties in all times, places, and circumstances. So, yes, it has a say in every aspect of a follower's life.

Do we like that?

Like it or not, as conditioned souls who are products of a materialistic culture, our entire lives have already been moulded by Kali-yuga. If we want freedom from Kali, then we need to revise every aspect of our lives based on Krsna's advice. And it will be for readers to decide whether they are ready to do so.

After instructing Arjuna, Krsna left it to him to decide for himself whether he wanted to follow the Lord's teachings: vimrsyaitad asesena yathecchasi tatha kuru, "Deliberate on this fully, and then do what you wish to do."

Reading this book the reader may ask, "Am I ready to follow varṇāśrama-dharma?"

For those who are, this book is for them. And those who are not must accept that, until they are, they will not derive the same benefits from life as followers. These benefits are described in the subsequent two chapters, and the societal benefits are hinted at below.

A devotee who does not follow the tenets of varṇāśrama cannot expect the same rights from the devotee society as those who do. Arjuna could not expect to be part of the future governance of the world if he did not fight the war. Everyone would have considered him a coward and thus unwilling to follow him.

The mission and social structure of MKTHK is varṇāśrama. Every society in the world offers rights only to the degree that their members fulfil their obligations. A person cannot expect running water in his house if he is not willing to pay the municipal water bill. Similarly a devotee may not expect, for example, the Society's representatives to perform family samskaras if their life is not in accord with those samskaras, and with the principles of the Society.

It is our hope, however, that devotees—especially initiated devotees—see it as their duty to fulfil Srila Prabhupada's expressed desire, and thus adopt the mission of varṇāśrama by observing and adhering to its principles.

We have spent many years discussing the concept of membership in MKTHK. While we viewed our Society as a church, membership was based solely on belief in Krsna. However, as we make the transition to a broader social structure, we may have to review and redefine membership in terms of those who accept and put into practice the tenets of varṇāśrama. Membership is not the subject of this handbook, and its implementation is some way off, but it is inevitably an important agenda item.

Over and above the need to follow institutional norms is Krsna's desire. What does Krsna want of His devotees? In creating the material world, Krsna also gave us the social system and accompanying rules of conduct by which to achieve liberation. A lifestyle that deviates from Krsna's plan does not serve that end, but following the principles of varṇāśrama does. Thus Narada says, "Simply by acquiring these qualifications, one can satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead."

Finally, all devotees are interested in their spiritual advancement. We follow Krsna and Srila Prabhupada, being convinced that they know what is best for us. And according to them, our spiritual lives will be best served in a varṇāśrama-based society. The concept of "outcast" refers to conditioned souls who choose to stay outside of the guidance of varṇāśrama. Defining an outcast, Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes,

**"Those who do not distinguish between proper and improper action, who have no inclination for righteousness, who are fond of argument, extremely selfish, think only of food, and who are devoid of marriage rules, are called antyaja or outcast. Without giving up such a nature, the real nature of a human being does not manifest."**

**Implementing Guidelines**

Laws only have worth if they are enforced. In an ideal society of transcendentalists we would not need laws, or this compendium. Being directly in touch with Krsna, perfect devotees would know what to do, what is right and what is wrong. But that is Satya-yuga and we live in Kali-yuga, and we may not all be transcendentalists.

In this age there is a need for laws, which Plato described in this way:

**"Laws are made to instruct the good, and in the hope that there may be no need of them; also to control the bad, whose hardness of heart will not be hindered from crime."**

Because as a Society the Hungarian yatra does not have comprehensive means to enforce laws, and also because in some cases there is no need for formal enforcement, this compendium is a book of guidelines. Guidelines are more advice than command.

Let us briefly consider the two reasons why this compendium is underpinned by minimal enforcement: we have little means for it and it is not always needed.

Our Society has not appointed and recognised ksatriyas to identify and apprehend transgressors, judge them, and then mete out punishment. We don't have a functioning constabulary or judiciary. To the contrary, the same devotees who guide others' lives, brahmanas, often have to be both policeman and judge—hardly a desirable mix, and hardly a proper division of work.

We do have and enact sanctions for serious offenses, such as a spouse abandoning his or her family. But even these sanctions are limited to the sphere of influence of individual temples and centres, and not necessarily to the wider devotee society. In other words, while temple management may deal one way with an offender based on one set of values, devotees outside the temple may deal another way with that person based on another set of values. The result is limited enforcement and a varied approach to those who transgress principles of dharma.

These are the very reasons that we need varṇāśrama. Were we a real dharmic community—people living together based on common ideals—, the response to transgressing codes of conduct would be uniform throughout the Society and based on common values. Varṇāśrama establishes those eternal values called sanatana-dharma.

Therefore, before any efficient enforcement can be established, our Society needs to be able to delegate different roles to different devotees. We are in the process of establishing a judiciary; an independent department for enforcement will follow.

As for the need to enforce guidelines:

Law enforcement and judiciary bodies are generally for major transgressions, those that seriously obstruct the functioning of the Society. For the most part the qualities and duties, and their infringement, are an individual or family affair. In such cases it will be the devotees' inspiration to better themselves—as well as the guidance they receive from peers and superiors—that will help to them to correct and elevate themselves.

In such cases it may be said that there is little need for enforcement. That said, when devotees choose a lifestyle or conduct that contradicts the criteria of their membership in, or their community service to, MKTHK, they may deprive themselves of certain rights and services. These rights and services are comprehensively explained in the MKTHK publication entitled Kozossegi Szerepek (Social Roles).

Thus, at the present time, this volume remains a book of guidelines—advice which the aspirants for pure devotion voluntarily embrace as a template for both elevating themselves and humbly correcting others. As the ksatriya wing of varṇāśrama is further established in Hungary, this compendium will become a law book.

**Dissemination and Education**

This handbook is published by the MKTHK and is sold to all devotees and sympathisers of the movement. It will be available at our centres throughout the country and we urge all devotees to read and study its contents. We believe that the challenge of transforming our church to a real society is both a serious and plausible one. However, it will require participation by all involved: in many cases it means a different way of thinking, a change of lifestyle, and above all, education.

Starting in 2016, the contents of this handbook will be taught as widely as possible: wherever our Society has the capacity to educate and train it will do so. That means this booklet will become mandatory curriculum for all new volunteers, the counsellor system, our centres, Bhaktivedanta College, and our schools. Additionally we would hope that the family tradition will embrace the values of this book and family members will assist each other to imbibe them.

As other handbooks on varṇāśrama topics become available in successive years—the next one dealing with asramas and the interaction between men and women—their contents will be added to the devotee-training curriculum.

A teaching guide will be produced for this handbook, and that will be the standard teaching format, as well as the guide for home education. The very key to establishing varṇāśrama is education. That means accepting the need for it, studying, assimilating, and practising it. Then the different sections of our Society will systematically evolve from strength to strength, because its members will have become duly qualified, in Narada Muni's words, as human beings.

Srila Prabhupada said,

**"So we are stressing on the point of education. You educate certain section as brahmana, certain section as ksatriya, certain section as vaisya. In that education we don't discriminate because he's coming of a sudra family. Take education. Be qualified."**

## Chapter Two. Why Varṇāśrama is Special

The number of societies worldwide and the purposes they all serve are too many to list. They may go under different political and social aliases, but in effect they offer the same goal—sense gratification, economic development, and religiosity. There is no true concept of liberation. And while these societies may promote religious and moral values, both those goals are in a free-fall of qualitative degradation that is underscored by the world's current economic collapse.

The net result is that people the world over are destined to lose their valuable human form of life. In other words, the very social fabric that is meant to elevate people instead condemns them. And what else would one expect, when unqualified citizens vote for unqualified leaders, neither knowing the goal of life, how to achieve it, nor any willingness to do so even if they did?

But Krsna created this world and the men and women that inhabit it, and He also created a social system to facilitate the goal of life, which is going back to Godhead. That social system is varṇāśrama-dharma. Krsna says, *tasya kartaram api mam*, "I am the creator, the father, of this social system \[varṇāśrama\]."

Without following the laws of God, Krsna’s laws, people are like irresponsible children playing games with their valuable human form of life. But if they follow Krsna's direction on the four varnas and four asramas, then they will fulfil their mandate on earth.

That is what makes varṇāśrama special: it is created for the purpose of liberation. It is conducive to and elevates its followers to spiritual perfection. And it does so by engaging everyone in the social order, varna, and spiritual order, asrama, that corresponds to their conditioned nature. Being thus properly situated, people are further connected to the devotional service of the Lord, the practice of which is compulsory for everyone.

The qualities and duties of the varnas and asramas can be summarised in the following way:

The quality of a brahmacari is studiousness, of a grhastha religious responsibility to his family and society, of a vanaprastha detachment, and of a sannyasi renunciation. Similarly, the main quality of a brahmana is the search for the absolute truth, of a ksatriya protection of dependents and obedience to brahmanas, of the vaisyas independent industriousness, and of sudras dependence on the higher castes.

Krsna summarises the duties of the four asramas in this way:

**“The main religious duties of a sannyasi are equanimity and non-violence, whereas for the vanaprastha, austerity and philosophical understanding of the difference between the body and soul are prominent. The main duties of a householder are to give shelter to all living entities and perform sacrifices, and the brahmacari is mainly engaged in serving the spiritual master."**

Similarly, the four varnas have their respective duties: the main duty of a sudra is service to superiors, of a vaisya to engage in religious economic development, of a ksatriya to give protection to others, and of a brahmana to teach transcendental knowledge.

And yet there will be some people who fall foul of the varṇāśrama system. Krsna indicates the qualities by which they can be recognised:

**"Dirtiness, dishonesty, thievery, faithlessness, useless quarrel, lust, anger, and hankering constitute the nature of those in the lowest position outside the varṇāśrama system."**

When devotees engage in duties in both the social and spiritual fields—varnas and asramas—that correspond to their natures, then they are properly situated. Under such circumstances they will be most productive to society and best aligned for practising Krsna consciousness. This is the substance and purpose of varṇāśrama-dharma. Narada mentions one benefit of faithfully following the Vedic codes of conduct:

*vrttya sva-bhava-krtaya*  
*vartamanah sva-karma-krt*  
*hitva sva-bhava-jam karma*  
*sanair nirgunatam iyat*

**"If one acts in his profession according to his position in the modes of nature and gradually gives up these activities, he attains the niskama stage."**

What this verse says is that the freedom from material desires, niskama, for which yogis perform austerities and jnanis study endlessly, can be attained by devotees who give up hereditary customs and serve Krsna according to their individual natures. Being rightly situated, devotees become free from the accoutrements of their varna and asrama, and from material desire.

In conclusion, we may profit by noting well the famous statement of Manu-samhita: *dharmo raksati raksitah*, “dharma protects those who protect dharma."

Having been conditioned by modern concepts of democracy, egalitarianism, libertarianism, and the idealism that one can be anyone and do anything, even we devotees are often reluctant to accept a social system that is authoritarian, hierarchical, and which predestines our work according to our natures. However, this is the social system Krsna established as being the most suitable material basis for elevating conditioned souls to perfection through Krsna consciousness.

The phrase dharmo raksati raksitah assures us that if we follow Krsna's system we will benefit individually and collectively: do the right thing and you will get the right result—both materially and spiritually. It's a matter of faith in Krsna and in the process of dharma.

## Chapter Three. Varṇāśrama: Neither Outdated Nor Dangerous

Since the time of Srila Prabhupada's departure from this world, there have been controversies in ISKCON on the relevance today of varṇāśrama to ISKCON and to greater society. Those who doubt or deny that relevance consider varṇāśrama to be an outdated social structure that has been supplanted both for Vaisnavas and for the world by nama-sankirtana. They say it cannot be practised today. Moreover, continue sceptics, the corrupted, prejudicial caste system of India shows the dangers associated with varṇāśrama. And the global notoriety that casteism has garnered precludes any thinking person from accepting it.

However, if we study the chronology of Srila Prabhupada's teachings, we see that as years passed, His Divine Grace gave increasing importance to varṇāśrama both for ISKCON and for the whole world. The twelve years of ISKCON's history under Srila Prabhupada's direction is evidence of that. Indeed it was a theme of his last instructions to his followers. Srila Prabhupada wanted ISKCON to be a pilot project that could show the world the perfect social structure designed for man by God. And if thinking people free of prejudice truly examine varṇāśrama, they will agree with Srila Prabhupada's assessment of its need and unique status as the template for a perfect society.

**Prabhupada's Instructions on Varṇāśrama**

In introducing varṇāśrama while touring the Hungarian yatra in 2015, Sivarama Swami presented in detail the increased emphasis Srila Prabhupada gave to this part of his mission. To summarise Maharaja's presentation, we quote some passages from one lengthy conversation between Srila Prabhupada and senior devotees in Mayapur on February 14, 1977. We have put topic headings above the passages, which are not necessarily in the order Srila Prabhupada spoke them.

**We Do Not Reject Varṇāśrama:**

**Satsvarupa**: Lord Caitanya, when Ramananda Raya brought this up He said it was not possible in this age to introduce this.  
**Prabhupada**: Yes. Not... He did not say possible. Iha bahya. Caitanya Mahaprabhu was interested only on the spiritual platform. He had no idea of material side. He rejected material side.  
**Satsvarupa**: But don't we do that also?  
**Prabhupada**: No. Our position is different. We are trying to implement Krsna consciousness in everything. And Caitanya Mahaprabhu personally took sannyasa. He rejected completely material. Niskincana. But we are not going to be niskincana. We are trying to cement the troubled position of the... That is also in the prescription of Bhagavad-gita. We are not rejecting the whole society.

**Chanting and Varṇāśrama Complement Each Other:**

**Hari-sauri**: But in Caitanya Mahaprabhu's practical preaching He only induced them to chant.  
**Prabhupada**: That is not possible for ordinary man.  
**Hari-sauri**: What, to simply induce people to chant?  
**Prabhupada**: Hm?  
**Hari-sauri**: He only introduced just the chanting.  
**Prabhupada**: But who will chant? Who'll chant?  
**Satsvarupa**: But if they won't chant, then neither will they train up in the varṇāśrama. That's the easiest.  
**Prabhupada**: The chanting will be there, but you cannot expect that people will chant like Caitanya Mahaprabhu. They cannot even chant sixteen rounds. (And) these rascals are going to be Caitanya Mahaprabhu.  
**Satsvarupa**: No. But if they at least will chant and take some prasada...  
**Prabhupada**: Chanting will go on. That is not stopped. But at the same time the varṇāśrama-dharma must be established to make the way easy.  
**Hari-sauri**: Well, at least my own understanding was that the chanting was introduced in the age of Kali because varṇāśrama is not possible.  
**Prabhupada**: Because it will cleanse the mind. Chanting will not stop.  
**Hari-sauri**: So therefore the chanting was introduced to replace all of the systems of varṇāśrama and like that.  
**Prabhupada**: Yes, it can replace, but who is going to replace it? The... People are not so advanced. If you imitate Haridasa Thakura to chant, it is not possible.

**We in ISKCON Must Establish Varṇāśrama:**

**Satsvarupa**: Varṇāśrama is not required.  
**Prabhupada**: Not required. Caitanya Mahaprabhu denied, "I am not brahmana, I am not ksatriya, I am not this, I am not this." He rejected. But in the Bhagavad-gita, the catur-varnyam ḍaóṁ srstam \[Bhg. 4.13\]. So we are Krsna..., preaching Krsna consciousness. It must be done.  
**Prabhupada**: Just like the sahajiyas also have got the beads and..., but they have got three dozen women. This kind of chanting will go on. Just like our (name withheld). He was not fit for sannyasa but he was given sannyasa. And five women he was attached, and he disclosed. Therefore varṇāśrama-dharma is required. Simply show-bottle will not do. So the varṇāśrama-dharma should be introduced all over the world...  
**Prabhupada**: To save the mass people from falling down to the repetition of birth and death, that is welfare activity. To save them by Krsna consciousness, we have to keep this ideal at least, varṇāśrama, that "Here is the position."  
**Hari-sauri**: At this time should we try to introduce it in our centres or not?  
**Prabhupada**: Always we shall try. Human society will be always there. We have to serve them, para-upakara. We have to keep them in the right position.  
**Hari-sauri**: I just remember two or three years ago there was a thing... A pamphlet came out about introducing the varṇāśrama system in the society, but actually nothing came of it.  
**Prabhupada**: Yes. That time was not right. Now you can do something.

**ISKCON Should be a Varṇāśrama Pilot Project for the World:**

**Satsvarupa**: Introduced starting with ISKCON community?  
**Prabhupada**: Yes. Yes. Brahmana, ksatriyas. There must be regular education.  
**Hari-sauri**: But in our community, if the..., being as we're training up as Vaisnavas...  
**Prabhupada**: Yes.  
**Hari-sauri**: Where will we introduce the varṇāśrama system, then?  
**Prabhupada**: In our Society, amongst our members.  
**Hari-sauri**: But then if everybody's being raised to the brahminical platform...  
**Prabhupada**: Not everybody. Why you are misunderstanding? Varṇāśrama, not everybody brahmana.  
**Hari-sauri**: No, but in our Society practically everyone is being raised to that platform. So then one might ask what is...  
**Prabhupada**: That is... Everybody is being raised, but they're falling down.  
**Hari-sauri**: The principle we follow. We're just thinking how it can be implemented. You were saying that it should be started in our Society.  
**Prabhupada**: Yes, that is a very broad idea. Now we are speaking of some of them, training them. That is another thing. That is small scale.  
**Hari-sauri**: The principle we're following.  
**Prabhupada**: Yes. In the... For the big scale, this is the required. In big scale you cannot make all of them as brahmanas or sannyasis. No. That is not possible. This is a small scale. How many percentage of people of the world we are controlling? Very insignificant. But if you want to make the whole human society perfect, then this Krsna consciousness movement should be introduced according to the Krsna's instruction, if you want to do it in a large scale for the benefit of the whole human society.  
**Prabhupada**: That "We said" means not we are going to take them, but we are simply giving the ideas. We are not going to be a sudra. But to show the... Just like you play in a drama. You are playing the part of a king. You are not a king.  
**Hari-sauri**: No.  
**Prabhupada**: So similarly, just to give them idea, we have to play like that.  
**Hari-sauri**: Well, again, that's...  
**Prabhupada**: Not necessarily that we are going to be sudra. So that is it. That is the thing. We are servant of Krsna. That's all. And as servant of Krsna, we have to execute the order of Krsna.  
**Satsvarupa**: So we can ideally organise ourselves and then for the rest of the people all we can do is hope that they'll follow it.  
**Prabhupada**: Yes.  
**Bhavananda**: Set the example.  
**Prabhupada**: In order to serve the mass of people, to bring them to the ideal position, we should try to introduce this varṇāśrama, not that we are going to be candidates of varṇāśrama. It is not our business. But to teach them how the world will be in peaceful position we have to introduce.

**It Is Possible:**

**Prabhupada**: In this way, gradual step... So it is very difficult to bring, introduce varṇāśrama, but at least there must be some idea.  
**Prabhupada**: Then we have to introduce this varṇāśrama-dharma. It must be done perfectly, and it is possible and people will be happy.  
**Prabhupada**: It must be done.

To conclude on this theme we quote a statement from Thakura Bhaktivinoda emphasising the importance of varṇāśrama for the practising devotee and for society at large:

**"In the assembly of the wise, everyone can understand the meaning of the scriptures. Therefore I shall not give any comment on these words (Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.17.15-21). I will only say this: the duties of varnṁ and asrama are the root of following the scriptural rules and regulations (vaidha). A country is impious to the degree it does not follow varṇāśrama.”**

**The Risk of Casteism**

Srila Prabhupada's statements above answer many objections of devotees who doubt the relevance of varṇāśrama to ISKCON and its mission today. There is no doubt, however, that the risk of varṇāśrama degrading into a materialistic caste system exists.

But that does not mean we should not establish varṇāśrama.

In the past there have been deviations from the practice of pure devotional service, deviations that have opened the floodgates to degraded practices and erroneous philosophies, which the acaryas group under the epithet of sahajiyaism. No doubt such sahajiyas also thrive at present and will do so in the future. But that did not, does not, and will not stop us from taking up or practising Krsna consciousness. It should be the same with varṇāśrama.

The antidote to material casteism is to clearly imbibe Krsna's teachings in which social and spiritual classification is not primarily determined by birth, but by qualities and activities.The Lord says,

*catur-varnyam ...ṁ.srstam*  
*guna-karma-vibhagasah*

**"According to the three modes of material nature and the work associated with them, the four divisions of human society are created by Me."**

In Narada Muni's words,

*yasya yal laksanam proktam*  
*pumso varnabhivyanjakam*  
*yad anyatrapi drsyeta*  
*tat tenaiva vinirdiset*

**"If one shows the symptoms of being a brahmana, ksatriya, vaisya or sudra, as described above, even if he has appeared in a different class, he should be accepted according to those symptoms of classification."**

**Vaisnavas are Beyond Their Varna and Asrama**

Another distortion of varṇāśrama is to consider that Vaisnavas belong to a particular varna or asrama.

The role of varṇāśrama in greater society is to bring people to the stage of surrender to Krsna, to inspire people to practise pure bhakti. When someone is already a Vaisnava, then that is his primary identity and it is offensive to call him, for example, either a vaisya or a grhastha. Srila Prabhupada would quote the verse *arcye visnau sila-dhir gurusu nara-matir vaisnave jati-buddhih*, "One who thinks the Deity in the temple to be made of wood or stone, who thinks of the spiritual master in the disciplic succession as an ordinary man, who thinks the Vaisnava in the Acyuta-gotra to belong to a certain caste or creed or who thinks of caranamrta or Ganges water as ordinary water is taken to be a resident of hell."

Prior to becoming a Vaisnava, social identity is based on varna and asrama, "here is a grhastha-brahmana." Since one has not realised his spiritual identity as servant of Krsna, identification is on the basis of the body.

But once one takes to Krsna consciousness, one has embraced the spiritual identity, "I am a servant of Krsna." At that time the material identity is secondary and the spiritual one both primary and dominant. As long as that understanding and nomenclature is kept in the forefront, offenses will be avoided. Neglecting to do so will result in offense, and the pilot project Srila Prabhupada envisaged in which ISKCON exemplifies a model society, finished.

**Devotees Are Not Transcendental to Varṇāśrama**

That the identity of devotees transcends the material identity based on varna and asrama does not mean that devotees themselves are automatically transcendentally situated, or transcendental to varṇāśrama. Transcendence comes not by proclamation but by purification of the heart. Neither does it mean that varṇāśrama for the devotee community is a show.

In the conversation quoted previously, Srila Prabhupada explains that as long as devotees are conditioned—as long as they are below the stage of ecstasy, bhava—their Krsna consciousness is dependent on material conditions. The most favourable material condition in which to practise Krsna consciousness is goodness, and the principles of varṇāśrama raise and keep devotees in that mode.

For this reason, practising devotees observe the principles of varṇāśrama because, in Srila Prabhupada's words, it "makes the way easy for chanting" and for a life based on good sadhana. It may be noted that even devotees who are perfected continue to follow varṇāśrama to set the example for others, and because it is also part of the eternal social structure in Vaikuntha.

**Varṇāśrama in Vaikuntha**

The eternality of varṇāśrama is confirmed in Brhad-bhagavatamrta when, in celestial Dvaraka, Uddhava hesitates to instruct Gopa-kumara in Narada's presence. Krsna's cousin says that as a ksatriya he is inferior and so should not speak in the presence of a brahmana, Narada. The sage responds by proposing that varṇāśrama designations belong to conditioned souls in the material world, to which Uddhava replies, "What can I say? How can someone like me stop thinking himself a ksatriya when even our Lord does not?"

*yatha tatra tathatrapi*  
*sad-dharma-paripalanam*  
*garhasthyari-jaya-jyestha-*  
*vipra-sammananadikam*

**"Here in Vaikuntha the Lord maintains the religious principles of civilised people, just as He does on earth. He behaves like a proper family man, conquers His enemies, shows respect to elders and brahmanas, and so on."**

In other words, varṇāśrama is the social structure in which the Lord, in His unlimited forms on unlimited Vaikuntha planets, displays pastimes.

Furthermore, readers may also consider Krsna's pastimes in Goloka-Vraja. There the Lord is a cowherd boy, part of a vaisya community which, although they are the most exalted of transcendentalists, follows the strictures of varṇāśrama.

Indeed Krsna's conjugal pastimes with the gopis, His parakiya-rasa, are contingent on the norms of the varṇāśrama that everyone else follows. The supreme taste of that forbidden relationship is dependent on breaking the rules of varṇāśrama. In this way varṇāśrama serves Krsna's identity and pastimes in the spiritual world, as it does when He descends to earth.

Obviously, if varṇāśrama is relevant to Krsna and His associates in Vaikuntha, it is relevant to us practitioners in ISKCON.

**Varṇāśrama is Relevant Today**

According to Jiva Gosvami, the varṇāśrama system naturally arises in the world at the time of creation, and therefore should be accepted by all intelligent human beings.About varṇāśrama, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura says that while varnas and asramas are external designations, conditioned souls should follow these scientific divisions until they reach the stage of liberation.Krsna says the same thing to Uddhava.

The ideal Krsna conscious society that varṇāśrama sceptics sometimes speak about is to be found in Satya-yuga when everyone is a pure devotee from birth, and thus religious duties are followed spontaneously and perfectly. In that age there is no lower and higher in terms of social or spiritual divisions, and the mode of goodness is dominant.

Varṇāśrama became an organisational necessity in Treta-yuga when society was driven predominantly by the mode of passion. At that time the social divisions were clearly recognised by their unique duties and qualities, being very clearly manifest. Indeed, birth was an indication of varnas. In other words, a ksatriya was almost always born in the family of ksatriyas, and it was the same with the other varnas.

In Dvapara-yuga the mixed mode of passion and ignorance is dominant, and the ksatriyas and brahmanas are most numerous, both being devoted to study of the Vedas. In this age varṇāśrama-dharma remains the structure of society, but because people are not as qualified as in Treta-yuga, their duties are adjusted to facilitate the dominant modes of nature.

Finally, in Kali-yuga, irreligion is dominant and ignorance is the presiding mode. Suta Gosvami says,

**"In the Kali age people tend to be greedy, ill-behaved and merciless, and they fight one another without good reason. Unfortunate and obsessed with material desires, the people of Kali-yuga are almost all sudras and barbarians."**

We may well ask, "Under these conditions in Kali-yuga, is varṇāśrama really possible?"

The answer of course is "Yes!"

It is possible by Srila Prabhupada's grace in the form of this Krsna consciousness movement and through his sincere followers. Although intrinsically conditions in Kali-yuga seem hopeless, there is hope, great hope.

The three modes of nature are always present everywhere, and so even within Kali-yuga, although ignorance dominates, passion and goodness are also present. Similarly, the other three ages are present as sub-factors in every age. Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura explains that these concealed ages may occasionally manifest as sub-ages in any yuga. So even in Satya-yuga there may be a brief Kali-yuga.That means that a golden age—as is well-known by devotees—lies dormant in Kali-yuga.That dormant age can be awakened by Krsna consciousness, because goodness is invoked when its qualities are cultivated. This is described in Srimad-Bhagavatam:

**"When the mind, intelligence and senses are solidly fixed in the mode of goodness, that time should be understood as Satya-yuga, the age of truth. People then take pleasure in knowledge and austerity."**

In other words, Satya-yuga is not just a period in time, it is a condition that can appear at any time, anywhere, when devotees cultivate transcendental knowledge and engage in nama-sankirtana. The same can be said of Treta- and Dvapara-yugas. The former appears when devotees perform their duties but have ulterior motive, and the latter when devotees are dutiful but also greedy, proud, and selfish. Since Lord Caitanya's appearance is meant to usher in a golden age, a Satya-yuga, devotees should exhibit those qualities, and then varṇāśrama will be very easy to realise and to follow.

Narada Muni explains that in each age, adjustments congruous with the prevailing mode of nature must be made in the duties of the varnas and asramas. Thus, while followers of Gauranga may live in Satya-yuga, they must establish and follow a form of varṇāśrama that is also practicable by the ignorant people of the world. This adjustment is explained thus:

*prayah sva-bhava-vihito*  
*nrnam dharmo yuge yuge*  
*veda-drgbhih smrto rajan*  
*pretya ceha ca sarma-krt*

**"My dear King, brahmanas well conversant in Vedic knowledge have given their verdict that in every age \[yuga\] the conduct of different sections of people according to their material modes of nature is auspicious both in this life and after death."**

So yes, varṇāśrama works in Kali-yuga! And Srila Prabhupada left it to the Krsna-conscious wisdom of his followers to establish the form of varṇāśrama suitable for both devotees and the common man. That is our mission.

**Be Yourself, Not Someone Else**

Although not the final argument that sceptics raise about varṇāśrama, the following hypothetical argument is the last one we shall respond to:

**"In today's world, like everyone else, we devotees are encouraged to dream the impossible, reach for the stars, and fulfil our full potential. But varṇāśrama-dharma says that we should be satisfied with what our own social identity offers. Or as Krsna says, 'It is far better to discharge one's prescribed duties, even though faultily, than another's duties perfectly.'In other words, every devotee should not get a higher university education and a well-paying job. Rather they should accept the role, work, or profession assigned to them by their nature, and the results that come from such efforts."**

The answer to this concern lies in the "full potential" that varṇāśrama encourages people to fulfil. That full potential is not directed to education, profession, or material achievement, but to spiritual attainment, to going back to Godhead, to attaining the real goal of human life. When devotees become too concerned with material goals, they are very likely to lose sight of spiritual goals. This will be the case when material goals are not suited to one's nature, but when goals are merely the stuff of desires or dreams.

Our natures, guna, facilitate a certain type of work. And since devotees are meant to avoid over-endeavour, we need to be satisfied with what comes easily without excessive effort. The second verse of Upadesamrta gives six causes for spiritual weakness, all of which relate directly or indirectly to this concept of everyone becoming an achiever and striving to reach their full potential:

*atyaharah prayasas ca*  
*prajalpo niyamagrahah*  
*jana-sangas ca laulyam ca*  
*sadbhir bhaktir vinasyati*

**"One's devotional service is spoiled when he becomes too entangled in the following six activities: (1) eating more than necessary or collecting more funds than required; (2) over-endeavouring for mundane things that are very difficult to obtain; (3) talking unnecessarily about mundane subject matters; (4) practising the scriptural rules and regulations only for the sake of following them and not for the sake of spiritual advancement, or rejecting the rules and regulations of the scriptures and working independently or whimsically; (5) associating with worldly-minded persons who are not interested in Krsna consciousness; and (6) being greedy for mundane achievements."**

From this verse we see that four defects in sadhana-bhakti are integral parts of today's consumer society and its propaganda. These faults are: *ati-ahara*—too much collecting; *prayasa*—over-endeavouring; *niyamagraha*—neglect of rules and regulations; and *laulya*—being greedy for mundane achievements. The result of succumbing to conforming to social expectations is often reflected in devotees' meagre spiritual lives and their excuse for such weakness, "I don't have time."

Moreover, society cannot function when everyone aspires to be a top earner and in a leading occupation—or even a leading service. If we liken society to a machine, say an automobile, and devotees to parts of that automobile, it becomes obvious that there is a need for different complementary roles in order for the machine to work. If every devotee wants to be a wheel, for example, the automobile will not be functional. Devotees have to accept their role, be it small or large, subservient or dominant, and when they do, the social mechanism takes shape and the automobile of society can take all its parts to their destination—Krsna.

So yes, devotees should accept work or service according to their ability, and be satisfied with the results that come from their efforts—even when those results are less than those attained by another devotee. We are not, as the saying goes, trying to "keep up with the Joneses," or in our case, the Dasas. But to achieve real satisfaction, one needs to reject the ethos of greed inculcated by today's materialistic, consumer-oriented society and live by what we need, by what our quota is, and not by our greed-fed wants. In the words of the Vedas:

**"Everything animate or inanimate that is within the universe is controlled and owned by the Lord. One should therefore accept only those things necessary for himself, which are set aside as his quota, and one should not accept other things, knowing well to whom they belong."**

Devotees should not shoot for the stars by doing anything and being anything. If one wants to shoot for the stars it should be in the firmament of the work that is borne of their nature. And for devotees to be content with their natural work and the results that come from it—with their quota—they must practise and perfect the quality of satisfaction, of which more is written in chapter 12 of this volume. In the meantime, however, readers may contemplate this wonderful instruction of Narada Muni describing the benefits of self-satisfaction—being satisfied with your nature and the work ascribed to it:

*sada santusta-manasah*  
*sarvah sivamaya disah*  
*sarkara-kantakadibhyo*  
*yathopanat-padah sivam*

**"For a person who has suitable shoes on his feet, there is no danger even when he walks on pebbles and thorns. For him, everything is auspicious. Similarly, for one who is always self-satisfied there is no distress; indeed, he feels happiness everywhere."**

In conclusion, much more could be said about these topics. We hope, however, that some objections have been suitably addressed and laid to rest. Following Srila Prabhupada's order, ISCKON and MKTHK must find the ways and means to make varṇāśrama the framework of its social structure. In that way we move from being a religion to a Society with established cultural norms.

## Part Two. The Twenty-one Qualities of Humans

## Chapter Four. General Conduct (Acara)

**The Qualities We Need to be Part of Varṇāśrama**

Components of social engineering include values, education, structure, politics, laws, enforcement, economy, and commerce. But more fundamental than these are people. After all, society is for the people and by the people.

But what kind of people?

What kind of people are required to make up a varṇāśrama society, and more importantly a Vaisnava varṇāśrama society?

If we liken a society to a building, then people are the bricks that make up the building. However well planned the building is, however nice it looks, however nice the doors and fixtures are, if the quality of the bricks is not to standard then the structure of the edifice is compromised, and in time it will collapse. This is seen today in all of the various political structures around the world: because people are degrading, their influence is dismantling morality, education, economy, and ultimately, the political structure itself. As an example of crumbling morality, we see that day-by-day same sex marriage is becoming the global norm.

Therefore it can be seen that varṇāśrama begins with delineating the ideal qualities and conduct of our Society's members. And establishing varṇāśrama can be realised by training devotees according to the character and work expected of them. This is the first phase of MKTHK's varṇāśrama vision and the content of this book.

So what are the basic criteria for all participants of a varṇāśrama society?

Narada Muni gives us the answer:

*satyam daya tapah saucam*  
*titikseksa samo damah*  
*ahimsa brahmacaryam ca*  
*tyagah svadhyaya arjavam*  
*santosah samadrk-seva*  
*gramyehoparamah sanaih*  
*nrnam viparyayeheksa*  
*maunam atma-vimarsanam*  
*annadyadeh samvibhago*  
*bhutebhyas ca yatharhatah*  
*tesv atma-devata-buddhih*  
*sutaram nrsu pandava*  
*sravanam kirtanam casya*  
*smaranam mahatam gateh*  
*sevejyavanatir dasyam*  
*sakhyam atma-samarpanam*  
*nrnam ayam paro dharmah*  
*sarvesam samudahrtah*  
*trimsal-laksanavan rajan*  
*sarvatma yena tusyati*

**"These are the general principles to be followed by all human beings: truthfulness, mercy, austerity (observing fasts on certain days of the month), bathing twice a day, tolerance, discrimination between right and wrong, control of the mind, control of the senses, non-violence, celibacy, charity, reading of scripture, simplicity, satisfaction, rendering service to saintly persons, gradually taking leave of unnecessary engagements, observing the futility of the unnecessary activities of human society, remaining silent and grave and avoiding unnecessary talk, considering whether one is the body or the soul, distributing food equally to all living entities (both men and animals), seeing every soul (especially in the human form) as a part of the Supreme Lord, hearing about the activities and instructions given by the Supreme Personality of Godhead (who is the shelter of the saintly persons), chanting about these activities and instructions, always remembering these activities and instructions, trying to render service, performing worship, offering obeisances, becoming a servant, becoming a friend, and surrendering one's whole self. O King Yudhisthira, these thirty qualifications must be acquired in the human form of life. Simply by acquiring these qualifications, one can satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead."**

In Part Two of this book, we will be studying these qualities one by one.

**Twenty-one Qualities**

The above directive has two main divisions. The first is the twenty-one qualities and duties that equate to religiosity, and the second, those that equate to the nine limbs of pure devotional service, the very means for liberation. Being faithful to the former facilitates perfecting the latter. In other words, there are twenty-one qualities that form a lifestyle that is suitable for liberation.

Although the purpose of religiosity is to make liberation easy, our focus in this book is on the twenty-one qualities. Devotees today have much more education in the practice of devotional service than they do on how to live in the mode of goodness, which is the lifestyle that facilitates their bhakti.

In his English rendering of the above verse, Srila Prabhupada says that these qualities "are the general principles to be followed by all human beings." So they are for everyone. They are the common denominator of conduct for all members of society.

What happens if some are absent in a person?

His Divine Grace adds that they "must be acquired in the human form of life."

Therefore, if a devotee does not live up to what is expected of a member of varṇāśrama, he or she should be taught how to cultivate those qualities either at home, at school, in the temple or a counsellor group—somewhere.

**What Is My Varna?**

These twenty-one qualities are not only the bedrock of varṇāśrama, but also the soil from which the qualities of the four varnas and asramas grow. With some additional distinctive qualities, each varna and asrama is characterised by the further refinement of some of these twenty-one qualities, resulting in eight unique divisions that distinguish one varna from another and one asrama from another.

Along with the distinguishing qualities of the varnas and asramas come their respective duties and corresponding rights. In this way varṇāśrama is both a democratic and undemocratic system. It is democratic in the sense that all its members have equal opportunity for liberation. It is undemocratic in the sense that its members' rights depend upon their social (varna) and spiritual (asrama) roles.For example, sudras cannot have independence, while the other social orders can; students must observe complete celibacy, while householders need not.

A feature of Kali-yuga is that all people are said to be even less than sudras. The Skanda Purana says, *kalau sudra-sambhavah*. This statement raises an obvious question: "If no one has the basic qualifications for varṇāśrama, then how can we form a varṇāśrama society? How can we say who is a brahmana and who is a vaisya?"

The answer is to be found in Srila Prabhupada's rendering of Narada's instruction:

**"... these thirty qualifications must be acquired in the human form of life. Simply by acquiring these qualifications, one can satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead."**

In other words, if we don't have the qualities, we should acquire them. Over and over, Srila Prabhupada emphasised training for the varnas and asramas: "So according to this varṇāśrama, there must be training."

Even if one is bom in a family of qualified brahmanas, still one is considered a sudra and must then cultivate brahminical qualities to qualify for that role in society.

**"The formula is that janmana jayate sudrah samskarad bhaved dvijah. Janmana jayate sudrah. Everyone who is born, first born by the father and mother... Then he is called a sudra. Even he is bom in a brahmana family, he is called a sudra. Then samskarad bhaved dvijah. Then, by culture... The cultural birth is called the second birth, dvija."**

How then do we determine who is of which varna? We should not look for premature answers to this question. Devotees must be trained in the twenty-one qualities in the same way that they are trained in the other nine devotional practices. When these qualities become properly manifest, then devotees' natures will be more readily visible and they can then determine their varna and asrama.

While much more can be written on this subject, we shall now review the twenty-one qualities one by one and briefly describe the appropriate activities that cultivate them. Readers will notice that in some of the following chapters more than one quality is described. The reasons for this are to keep this compendium as concise as possible, and because some qualities are closely related.

## Chapter Five. The Four Pillars of Religion: Truthfulness, Mercy, Austerity, Cleanliness

While touring the world to seek the origin of the inauspicious omens he had detected in Hastinapura, Pariksit Maharaja came across the personality of Kali abusing a cow and bull. Addressing the bull as the emblem of religion, the king said,

**"In the age of Satya your four legs were established by the four principles of austerity, cleanliness, mercy, and truthfulness. But it appears that three of your legs are broken due to rampant irreligion in the form of pride, lust for women, and intoxication. You are now standing on one leg only, which is your truthfulness, and you are somehow or other hobbling along. But quarrel personified \[Kali\], flourishing by deceit, is also trying to destroy that leg."**

Austerity, cleanliness, mercy, and truthfulness are considered the four principles of religion. In these verses of Srimad-Bhagavatam they are described as being systematically eradicated one by one with each passing age from Satya to Kali. Elsewhere in Srimad-Bhagavatam, it is stated that all four of the qualities decrease by one-fourth with each passing age.What both sources say is that with Kali-yuga the principles of religion will have all but vanished. This chapter describes those four invaluable attributes of human beings and especially of Vaisnavas.

**Truthfulness**

What is first and last in a list of items is generally of special importance. And so it is with truthfulness. Krsna lists it as a divine quality.And Srila Prabhupada says that truthfulness is the central point of all sacrifices meant to please Krsna, and the basic principle of every religion, including bhagavata-dharma.

Why?

One reason is that truthfulness is the last remaining of the four pillars of religion. The others—mercy, austerity, and cleanliness—have fallen to Kali. Therefore, cultivating truthfulness is the only hope to restore the fallen pillars and to prevent Kali from completely infiltrating human society.

What is truthfulness? Srila Prabhupada says, "A person whose word of honour is never broken is called truthful."

How firm should a devotee's word of honour be? A popular Hindi saying attributed to King Dasaratha sets the standard: *raghukula rita sada cali ai, prana jae para vacana na jai*, "Those in the line of Raghu are known for keeping their word. We may give up our lives, but we shall always keep our promise."

So how is truthfulness cultivated? It begins by recognising that Krsna is the supreme embodiment of truth, and that truthfulness means dedicating our lives to following His instructions. Truthfulness also includes being faithful to the lifestyle Krsna has set out for us, which means that as conditioned souls we are obliged to follow varṇāśrama-dharma.

The flip side of being truthful is to avoid falsity, which includes deceitfulness, hypocrisy, and insincerity. Narada also instructs that one should avoid the five branches of irreligion, namely: irreligion, religion for which one is unfit, pretentious religion, analogical religion, and cheating religion. There are many modern-day versions of these. In addition, dishonest social practices like corruption, bribery, and nepotism, as well as dishonest communication, must be avoided. Moreover, both gambling and speculative business enterprise destroy truthfulness, and so devotees must keep distant from them.

As with all qualities, practice makes perfect. Therefore practising truthfulness also includes avoiding telling half-truths, not being silent when an untruth is being spoken, and being respectfully straightforward in dealings. A person who is truthful is trustworthy, and a trustworthy person is accepted in society whatever their varna or asrama.

A simple example of practising truthfulness is the following: often when devotees want to talk to someone who may be too busy to listen, they will say, "May I speak with you for a moment?"

Most likely the subject at hand will take half an hour or more to discuss. Therefore this idiomatic approach is not a straightforward one: it is bending the truth for a purpose. A more honest approach would be to say, "I would like some of your time, probably a half hour or more. When can we meet?" That is being honest.

This example may appear trivial. But it is just this kind of trivial tampering with the truth that gradually deviates character.

In relation to varṇāśrama, truthfulness also means being honest about who we are and what our abilities are. This topic has been mentioned in chapter three under the heading of "Be Yourself, Not Someone Else."

Both our host societies and the nature of this age condition us to be achievers and to desire affluence. To those ends we struggle hard without having first carefully thought out who we are, what our nature is, and what our abilities are. When we are honest about these things, then we may see that our material achievements may surpass those of some, but will never equal those of others. That means that our standard of living may not equal that of another devotee.

And that is all right, because where all devotees achieve the same result is in the spiritual field. Therefore we should never compromise our chances for spiritual success by over-endeavouring for elusive material gain. The lesson to be learned here is that truthfulness means being true to our material nature and hence to our limitations. Moreover, truthfulness means being faithful to the real pursuit of going back to Godhead and not sacrificing that opportunity for any allurement, however enticing.

**Mercy**

Mercy means to bring someone relief from distress or to show compassion or forgiveness towards someone who could be treated harshly. It is a common word in devotees' vocabulary and very much an integral part of Lord Caitanya's mood of Krsna consciousness.

Mercy is the second of the four religious principles and a basic principle of brahminical culture. Sorrowfully it is lacking in this age, while whatever little mercy that exists is disappearing day by day. Srimad-Bhagavatam says that in Kali-yuga the decline of mercy is accompanied by a similar decline in the duration of life, the power of recollection, and moral or religious principles. And yet without being merciful one cannot surrender to the Supreme Lord. Clearly that is one reason why the wanton practice of meat-eating can only result in a semblance of religiosity, and not in pure devotion. As Srimad-Bhagavatam says, puman virajyeta vina pasughnat: meat-eaters cannot understand bhagavata-dharma.

From childhood, mercy should be taught and practised. Children should be taught to recognise those in need, learn to be kind to them—including animals—, to share what they have, and to feed the poor. The same practices should be continued throughout life, along with the greatest act of mercy, which is to bless others with Krsna consciousness. If devotees can sacrifice their lives to this end, then they are sure to be recognised by Krsna. But if they cannot, then they should actively support those who can.

Srila Prabhupada writes,

**"Spending fifty percent for the service of the Lord or for propagation of spiritual knowledge in society by way of the sankirtana-yajna is the maximum display of human mercy... greatest mercy... \[...\] When everyone is taught to sacrifice fifty percent of his accumulated gold for the Lord's service, certainly austerity, cleanliness and mercy automatically ensue, and thus the lost three legs of the personality of religion are automatically established."In pursuit of this ideal, the Hungarian yatra encourages devotees to give at least ten percent of their time or income.**

**The enemy of mercy is intoxication in both its gross and subtle forms. One who consumes liquor or other drugs—so readily available today—becomes indifferent to the suffering of others and even to one's own well-being. And being intoxicated with false ego, which is but a false conception of oneself, brings the same result. A very practical example of this kind of intoxication is when a person of a lower varna assumes the position of a higher one, thinking himself more qualified than he is.**

**By serving others—most notably Vaisnavas—, by cultivating the concept of being a servant, and by practising to see the oneness of all beings, one cultivates mercy and thus becomes divinely ornamented. It is said, "a person is great if he is decorated with the qualities of being very merciful toward the unfortunate, very powerful, superior, chivalrous, enthusiastic, expert, and truthful."**

**Austerity**

In translating the word tapah as "austerity," Srila Prabhupada further adds, "observing fasts on certain days of the month."No doubt there are other kinds of austerity which more generally refer to the voluntary acceptance of vows or activities which, while neither comfortable for the body nor soothing to the mind, are conducive to spiritual realisation. Like principles of religion such as charity, austerity is a corollary of devotional service and is not to be practised for any goal other than pleasing Krsna. In this regard Srimad-Bhagavatam says, vasudeva-param tapah, "the goal of austerity is to please Krsna."

As regards fasting, Srila Prabhupada says that it should be practised from childhood and suggests that it be done two or four times a month. His Divine Grace adds,

**"From the economic point of view, such two fasting days in a month \[ekadasi\] will save the state tons of food, and the system will also act very favourably on the general health of the citizens."**

The necessities of mankind are dependent on the pleasure of the demigods and the Lord, whose satisfaction is conditional on everyone practising austerity. When such austerity is lacking, the population of the world finds itself in all kinds of want.

By contrast, devotees know well that material and spiritual well-being are both dependent on following prescribed austerities. Fasting on Ekadasi and festivals like Janmastami, as well as adhering to vows of goodness, such as a householder's celibacy, are some examples of devotional austerity. Because devotees are faithful to these practices, they rarely find themselves in a condition of want.

In Satya-yuga, austerity, along with the other three principles of religion, was extraordinarily manifest. However, since that time austerity has been eroded due to the irreligious quality of pride.As people became more proud of their bodily identity, the brahminical quality of austerity decreased to non-existence. And because surrender to the Lord is not possible for persons devoid of austerity, the path of salvation has become increasingly evasive.

While brahmanas are naturally inclined to the practice of austerity, other orders need to embrace it out of duty, knowing that austerity will help them become sinless. Conversely, as they become purified, devotees in every order of life become increasingly drawn to the practice of austerity. Those who are sinless are naturally so inclined, as seen in the example of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, who was so absorbed in service that he had neither time to eat nor sleep.

The practice of austerity begins at childhood under the senior guidance of the guru. That is called the brahmacarya-asrama. With such training devotees are able to call upon resources of character, such as discipline and self-control, when they later enter the grhastha-asrama. That is the purpose of training in austerity: what is first done as an obligation later become a voluntarily practice.

Since pride is the enemy of austerity, one needs to address the roots of pride, which are unrestricted sense enjoyment and bodily identification. These two habits are subdued by serious scriptural study and regulated sadhana which in itself is a sort of austerity. The result of study and sadhana is increased mind and sense control, which then empowers one to embrace the austerities associated with one's varna and asrama. As already mentioned, as the heart becomes purified, austerity becomes easier and more desirable.

**Cleanliness**

In guiding Uddhava on the principles of varṇāśrama, Lord Krsna says,

**"My dear Uddhava, general cleanliness, washing the hands, bathing \[and\] avoiding that which is untouchable... should be followed by all members of society through regulation of the mind, words and body... One who has realised knowledge of Me should maintain general cleanliness, purify his hands with water, take bath, and execute other regulative duties not by force but by his own free will."**

Herein Krsna delineates some aspects of external cleanliness, which Srila Prabhupada has both summarised and supplemented in translating Narada's instructions with the words "bathing regularly at least twice a day, morning and evening, and remembering to chant the holy name of God."

In summary, both external and internal cleanliness should be a spontaneous practice for those who have surrendered to Krsna's will—for devotees.

Like the other three principles of religion, cleanliness is a prerequisite for surrendering to Krsna, and it also awards one protection from the influence of Kali as well as a life of prosperity. In addition, Krsna says that it is one of the nineteen requisite components of knowledge.

While external cleanliness is expected of all four varnas and asramas—especially of brahmanas—, in and of itself cleanliness does not do justice to the word sauca. The Vedic concept of sauca also calls for a corresponding internal cleanliness, which is characterised by spiritual practices that purify the material dust from the heart. Thus the word "cleanliness" implies a full range of internal purification, with special emphasis on cleansing excessive attachment to, and indulgence in, sex life. Cleanliness is curtailed in proportion to one's growing attachment for women or men, and for one addicted to sex it is completely spoiled.

Therefore, cleanliness of the mind can be cultivated by offenseless chanting and hearing of Krsna's names and qualities; cleanliness of words by avoiding abusive and unnecessary talk; and cleanliness of the body by adopting hygienic practices like bathing twice daily, keeping the hands clean, being neatly dressed, and maintaining orderly living and working conditions.

While one does not become Krsna conscious just by being clean, a Krsna conscious person is always clean. In that sense "cleanliness is next to godliness." Srila Prabhupada instructed and taught us in that way:

**"Another thing I request is that everything in the temple should be kept nice and clean. Everyone should wash hands before touching anything of Krsna's. We should always remember that Krsna is the purest and similarly only the pure can associate with Him. Cleanliness is next to Godliness."**

And cleanliness was on Srila Prabhupada's mind even in his last days with us. In his final description of varṇāśrama's standards, Srila Prabhupada emphasised, "Always neat and clean, rising early in the morning, see mangala-arati, in this way, stay in sattva-guna."

## Chapter Six. Tolerance and Discriminating Between Right and Wrong

In the Bhagavad-gita, Krsna first instructs us that we are not these bodies. The Lord's next teaching is on the need for tolerance, which He defines as remaining undisturbed during the appearance and subsequent disappearance of both happiness and distress.In his prayers to Krsna, Lord Brahma gives devotees the key to tolerance. That key is to recognise all perceptual feelings of happiness and distress as Krsna's mercy, krsna-karma—the reaction to past activities specially meted out for devotees by the Lord.
