Tolerance is one of the nineteen elements of knowledge as well as the natural quality of both brahmanas and ksatriyas. For brahmanas it may mean tolerating the risks that come with preaching, and for ksatriyas, the challenges of managing a preaching movement. But all devotees must possess tolerance as a prerequisite for the offenseless chanting of Krsna's names. The example Lord Caitanya gives is the nature of a tree: although it may be stripped of its fruit, leaves, branches, and bark, although it may even be cut down, a tree never protests but remains steadfast, just as it does through wind, rain, sun, and snow.

The inability of people in this age to control their mind and senses, to understand the workings of nature's laws, and to know the difference between body and soul means that they become intolerant at the slightest inconvenience. For this reason devotees need to regularly study these topics from scripture, then assimilate and finally realise them.

Meanwhile, both the execution of devotional service and residence in this material world offers plenty of opportunity to practise facing dualities of happiness and distress, cultivating freedom from anxiety of gain and loss, and bearing insult and dishonour from others. Such training becomes effective by devotees' complete and constant dependence on the good will of Krsna.

Prahlada Maharaja is the perfect example of tolerance—and he was just a child. Following his lead, if young children are taught tolerance by their parents and teachers, they will be well equipped to deal with the many impediments to making spiritual advancement, and will be able to progress to the goal of life with steadfast determination.

Devotees young and old should learn to follow Krsna's instruction:

**“If at times one does not obtain proper food one should not be depressed, and when one obtains sumptuous food one should not rejoice. Being fixed in determination, one should understand both situations to be under the control of God." (Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.18.33.)**

**Discrimination Between Right and Wrong**

The sixth quality mentioned by Śrī Narada is iksa, which literally means "to see." Srila Prabhupada translates it as "distinguishing between good and bad." The English equivalent of the word is "discrimination," which is defined as the ability to see the difference between one thing and another, between one person and another, and in relation to dharma, the ability to see the difference between right and wrong.

What constitutes right and wrong is called morality or ethics, a system of values that varies from society to society. In a society accustomed to meat eating, such dietary customs are good, but in a society of vegetarians, meat eating is bad. And so it goes on with countless relative questions of ethics, which from an absolute perspective are all wrong. As Kaviraja Gosvami says,

*'dvaite bhadrabhadra-jnana, saba—‘manodharma*  
*'ei bhala, ei manda',—ei saba ‘bhrama*

**"In the material world, conceptions of good and bad are all mental speculations. Therefore, saying 'This is good' and 'This is bad' is all a mistake." (Śrī Caitanya-caritamrta, Antya-lila 4.176.)**

Even in the Vedas there are different standards of right and wrong. Krsna says that those who follow mundane religious principles "must distinguish between good and bad, and to ensure one's physical survival one must recognise that which is auspicious and inauspicious." (Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.21.3.) In other words, for non-devotees good and bad are what is favourable or unfavourable for the common good—a selfish approach to life.

But varṇāśrama-dharma rests on the principle of hari-tosana, or determining good and bad based on whether it is pleasing or displeasing to Krsna.Therefore it is also called sanatana-dharma, because these principles are eternal and absolute, not relative. The difference between the two is that the morality prescribed by mundane religions does not satisfy the heart, whereas that prescribed by the varṇāśrama of Vaisnavas does.

Therefore, for a Vaisnava, discrimination—which is a function of intelligence—means to know what is pleasing and displeasing to Krsna, and to have both the wisdom and spiritual strength to do the former. This knowledge, which then strengthens intelligence, is acquired by a thorough study of Srila Prabhupada's books and by developing the self-discipline to live by them, a discipline that comes from strong sadhana.

Without cultivating and practising such discrimination, a devotee will not be able to remain insulated from the effects of a non-devotional lifestyle. As a result he or she may very well lose the advantageous position awarded by Krsna consciousness and again degrade into material illusion.

A transformation in lifestyle will not always be easy. Krsna s teachings are not for a localised aspect of life; rather, His instructions encompass every facet of life. As devotees, our lives evolve considerably from a blend of today s ultra-liberal norms and our own speculative designs to accepting Krsna's prescriptions on how to eat, work, dress, marry, and procreate—to name just a few. Such a transformation requires surrender to Krsna's will and recognition that what may now appear to be unpalatable over-regulation will in time transform into the sweetest nectar. Remember: conduct based on real discrimination becomes pleasing to the heart.

As Krsna says,

*yat tad agre visam iva*  
*pariname 'mrtopamam*  
*tat sukham sattvikam proktam*  
*atma-buddhi-prasada-jam*

**'That which in the beginning may be just like poison but at the end is just like nectar and which awakens one to self-realisation is said to be happiness in the mode of goodness." (Bhagavad-gita 18.37.)**

## Chapter Seven. Control of the Mind and Senses

Of the twenty-one religious qualities, some are more important than others. Control of the mind and senses is a practice that is indispensible in becoming a good person and a good devotee.

While Narada lists control of the mind and control of the senses as two separate items, because they are inseparable, and because the mind dominates over the senses, we have presented them together. Srila Prabhupada defines control of the mind, samah, as, "not allowing the mind to act whimsically," and control of the senses, damah, as, "not allowing the senses to act without control."

Many books have been written on these topics, but the most concise and authoritative is the Bhagavad-gita. We have therefore extracted the teachings of the sixth chapter, wherein Krsna instructs on sense control and even more so on mind control, which—not to belittle the former—is the most important of the two.

**Controlling the Mind**

Even if the gross senses are regulated by acts of devotion, the subtler mind may remain distracted and unengaged. This is called mechanical service. And if not upgraded, such devotion will lead to fall down and the abandonment of religious principles. On the other hand, if the mind is brought under control, the senses immediately follow suit. Therefore the Lord places the greater stress on control of the mind.

Both varṇāśrama and bhakti-yoga are based on dispensing with sense gratification, fruitive activities, and material desires. Candidates for both must have those three intentions. That is known as sincerity. And they must know that in taking on the mind they are facing a powerful enemy which, once vanquished, is a valuable spiritual ally.

Thus mind and sense control begin with sincerity, determination, and faith. Moreover, despite whatever challenges arise, one must not be deviated, but remain vigilant in keeping both the mind and senses engaged in practices authorised by Krsna.

The following are recommended practices for control of the mind:

Like a slippery fish, the mind must be carefully handled. The handler is the intelligence, fortified by deep and sustained scriptural study, and by the firm conviction that the mind can be tamed. Since the untamed mind is a foe, one can never let down one's guard. From the practical point of view, that means a devotee's life should be so carefully regulated that the mind cannot digress for a moment; instead it remains constantly under control of the intelligence. This, in short, is known as discipline.

Still, a slippery fish is hard to hold on to. And because the mind is restless, unsteady, obstinate, and very strong, we, like Arjuna, may be inclined to say, "It's impossible!''

But it is not! Krsna does admit that "it is undoubtedly very difficult to curb the restless mind." But He also says "it is possible by suitable practice and by detachment," abhyasena tu kaunteya vairagyena ca grhyate.

"Suitable practice" for Gaudiya Vaisnavas means an intense regimen of engagement within the framework of the nine limbs of devotion. Of these nine devotional practices, the yuga-dharma of chanting Krsna's holy names with humility is one that is to be given special emphasis. Srila Prabhupada draws our attention to another limb of bhakti, which is especially efficacious in controlling the mind: hearing about Krsna's pastimes.

His Divine Grace says, "Hearing about Krsna is a very powerful transcendental method for purging the mind of all misgivings."

The secret of this process lies in the extraordinarily attractive nature of Krsna and hence of His pastimes. Hearing or reading them draws the mind away from things that are unrelated to Him, and situates the mind within a state of transcendental satisfaction in which it becomes disinterested in material engagements. This state of spiritual satisfaction is called paresanubhava, a word that is derived from the following significant teaching of the sage Kavi Yogindra:

*bhaktih paresanubhavo viraktir*  
*anyatra caisa trika eka-kalah*  
*prapadyamanasya yathasnatah syus*  
*tustih pustih ksud-apayo 'nu-ghasam*

**"Devotion, direct experience of the Supreme Lord, and detachment from other things-these three occur simultaneously for one who has taken shelter of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, in the same way that pleasure, nourishment, and relief from hunger come simultaneously and increasingly, with each bite, for a person engaged in eating."**

The analogy above can be understood in this way: devotion may be compared to satisfaction, because they both take the form of pleasure; experience of Krsna and nourishment are comparable, because both sustain one’s life; and detachment and cessation of hunger are related, because both free one from further hankering so that one may experience peace.

There is an even further-illuminating difference between the example of practising bhakti and eating: while a person who eats becomes disinterested in food in proportion to his satisfaction, the devotee who experiences Krsna becomes increasingly attached to the Lord as he engages in service. This phenomenon can be understood if we acknowledge that Krsna's qualities are transcendental, as the atmarama verse confirms.

The word viraktih in the verse by Kavi Yogindra is the same detachment of which Krsna speaks, and it should be understood as something different than just renunciation. The latter indicates giving up something enjoyable while still maintaining a hankering for it. But detachment indicates that one gives up something materially enjoyable for something spiritually even more enjoyable—Krsna.

So the "constant detachment" that Krsna speaks of indicates that every time the mind of a practitioner has wandered away from Krsna, a devotee again drags it away from the mundane to focus it on the divine. In this way, because of Krsna's all-attractiveness, the tendency for the mind to wander decreases and decreases, to ultimately cease.

Srila Prabhupada gives a further example in which he compares the control of the mind to curing a disease by treatment and diet. Hearing of Krsna's pastimes is treatment for the "mad mind," and eating food offered to Krsna is the diet. "This treatment is the process of Krsna consciousness."

That concludes a summary study of the sadhana for controlling the mind, and making it a friend.

**Controlling the Senses**

With control of the mind, control of the senses becomes academic. The two no longer collude to distract a person, but rather the mind becomes the disciplinarian.

Having said that, even if the mind remains unbridled, senses under control of the intelligence can serve as an anchor for the mind. The gross nature of the senses makes them tangibly more manageable, and therefore the following are recommendations for their engagement:

As much as possible a devotee should avoid living, eating, working, and relaxing with non-devotees. Celibacy according to one's asrama, as well as moderate eating, sleeping, talking, and recreation—avoiding either excess or privation—are dominant practices. These are to be accompanied by constant engagement of the senses in devotional service and conversely by restraining them from mundane activities through appropriate vows.

That's it in a nutshell.

The immediate result of mind and sense control on the part of the practitioner is to manifest a sense of peace. The practitioner feels peace within himself, and others see his peaceful demeanour.

This peaceful mien is experienced and observed in a practitioner becoming indifferent to happiness and distress, heat and cold, honour and dishonour. It further manifests as apathy to the acquisition of wealth, equal treatment of all living entities, and the ability to relate to all conditions, things, and creatures as one would to the Lord. Such Krsna consciousness is made possible by the initially theoretical, and later realised, appreciation of Krsna's all-pervasiveness.

The spiritual benefits of being fully self-controlled are legion. Such accomplished devotees are freed from the reaction to past deeds, become very dear to Krsna, and are appreciated by devotees and non-devotees alike. Because the mind is like a lamp in a windless place, spiritual practices become easy and effective, enabling devotees to see not only their own spiritual nature, but to also rejoice in it.

Control of both the mind and senses eventually leads to their spiritualisation, which becomes not only a source of boundless bliss, but an unshakeable commitment to devotional service that can withstand the greatest difficulty and surmount the greatest obstacle. In such an elevated state, devotees are fully convinced that there is no greater gain to be had for the mind and senses than devotion to Krsna. Thus liberated, they are freed of all miseries.

To summarise in Krsna's words, "He whose mind is controlled and who strives by appropriate means is assured of success."

And if one doesn't control the mind?

Then one lives with the greatest enemy, an enemy that misleads the senses and inevitably degrades them into sinful conduct. Or in exceptional or marginal circumstances, "For one whose mind is unbridled, self-realisation is difficult work."In short, the best one can hope for from an uncontrolled mind is a long and rocky road to Krsna consciousness, in which one must confront the mind somewhere along the way and ultimately bring it under control.

## Chapter Eight. Non-violence

Non-violence has long been associated with—and identified as one of the qualities of—spirituality. Ghandhi's self-affiliation with non-violence led him to be considered a sadhu, a saint among politicians. And while non-violence is a duty of even the non-religious sections of society, the way that pacifists and non-devotees define non-violence is different than the Vaisnavas' definition.

Pacifists define non-violence as not harming another human's body, while Vaisnavas define non-violence as conduct which does not interfere with the spiritual development of another living entity. The Vaisnavas' definition indicates that the following people are violent: pacifists who do not use violence as the last alternative in preventing violence, people who obstruct the natural evolution of another for their benefit, spiritualists who mislead others with false religions, and Vaisnavas who do not take the trouble to preach.

In the world today we see extreme forms of violence everywhere. Violence is an unfortunate characteristic of Kali-yuga. Examples of this violence are the savage kinds of entertainment found in every kind of medium, the widespread killing of animals for food, and the many senseless wars that kill, maim, and dispossess millions of innocent bystanders. Overall, violence has become so rampant that the common man-young children included-is becoming immune to increasingly disturbing levels of violence. Gone are the days of innocence. Just as a society can be judged by its culinary habits, the same can be said of its level of violence. Thus the present high and pervasive levels of violence indicate a low-grade society. Kali-yuga is here.

Krsna says that non-violence is a perennial virtue of human society and one that must be observed by those in the varṇāśrama system:

*ahimsa satyam asteyam*  
*akama-krodha-lobhata*  
*bhuta-priya-hiteha ca*  
*dharmo 'yam sarva-varnikah*

**"Non-violence, truthfulness, honesty, desire for the happiness and welfare of all others, and freedom from lust, anger, and greed constitute duties for all members of society."**

Additionally, non-violence is a duty of a householder, and the very dharma of a sannyasi.

Householders engage in a variety of duties, and in so doing they may well inflict violence on other living entities if not careful. For example, a businessman should not bankrupt another, and a gardener should avoid harming innocent animals. Moreover, because greed for wealth—and wealth itself results in violence, unless a householder works as a servant of Krsna and engages his wealth in the Lord's service, that wealth will corrupt him, as a consequence of which others will suffer.

Unlike grhasthas, sannyasis have given up working to maintain a family, for they live for the greater family of humanity. However, if a sannyasi does not preach, or if he preaches solely to accumulate wealth and followers, he is as bad as—or even worse than—a materialistic householder. Krsna identifies equanimity and non-violence to be the main religious duties of sannyasis, bhiksor dharmah samo 'himsa.

Envy of other creatures is the root cause of violence. The Sanskrit word matsarya denotes envy or jealousy and has a broader connotation than its English equivalent. It refers not only to lusting after another's qualities or possessions, but also to disrespecting another's rights for the sake of one's own pleasure. Therefore, while people spend vast fortunes on pets, they thoughtlessly slaughter other animals to satisfy their perverted tastes. Similarly, while devotees preach about the need for cow-protection, they are unmindful of the violence against cows that is involved in their store-bought milk.

While pure devotion inspires non-violence, devotion in the mode of ignorance is itself contaminated by violence and envy.Unless such conduct is quickly purified, it will degrade into irreligious life, for a violent person, an animal killer, cannot understand God or pure devotion to Him. Even devotional service in passion makes one too self-centred to be mindful of the suffering of others. For example, although devotees sometimes talk about the dharma of grhasthas as one that includes food-distribution, they as a family may make no effort for it.

While devotees are generally assured of a human form of life, their negligence of others' needs will not go unpunished. And for non-devotees, their life of violence means that death becomes their gateway to the hell called Raurava. Sukadeva Gosvami says, "In this life, an envious person commits violent acts against many living entities. Therefore after his death, when he is taken to hell by Yamaraja, those living entities who were hurt by him appear as animals called rurus to inflict very severe pain upon him. Learned scholars call this hell Raurava. Not generally seen in this world, the rum is more envious than a snake."

How to be non-violent?

Since the cause of violence is envy, which when directed to Krsna is the cause of conditioned life, violence is not easy to uproot. Indeed we must begin by admitting that the first victim of our violence is Krsna. Conducting our lives in a way that recognises that He is the proprietor and that everything we have is for His service is a start. That kind of life means that one's assets—be they meagre or abundant—must actually be engaged in Krsna's service. Such charity cannot just be idle talk.

As far as other living entities are concerned, we must see them as equal to ourselves, treating them as we would expect to be treated, or as we would treat our favourite children.

This vision and its accompanying lifestyle manifest in one who meticulously practises devotional service. As Narada told Mrgari the hunter,

“Î hunter, good qualities like non-violence, which you have developed, are not very astonishing, for those who are engaged in the Lord's devotional service are never inclined to give pain to others because of envy."

## Chapter Nine. Celibacy

Of the twenty-one qualities, celibacy—restricting the association between men and women—is one that requires additional attention. It is most important. Varṇāśrama-dharma prescribes different kinds of celibacy according to different asramas. However, there is one underlying cause for which celibacy is prescribed for every walk of life, and that cause is given by Narada:

*kalpayitvatmana yavad*  
*abhasam idam isvarah*  
*dvaitam tavan na viramet*  
*tato hy asya viparyayah*

**"As long as a living entity is not completely self-realised—as long as he is not independent of the misconception of identifying with his body, which is nothing but a reflection of the original body and senses—he cannot be relieved of the conception of duality, which is epitomised by the duality between man and woman. Thus there is every chance that he will fall down because his intelligence is bewildered."**

This fascinating verse explains that the basic principle of bondage, which causes us to see things as being separate from Krsna and as objects for our enjoyment, is epitomised by the duality between man and woman. In our perverse age of homosexuality, transexuality, and over-sexuality, this epitome can be more contextually stated as the whimsically unregulated emotional and physical enjoyment between two conditioned souls.

The rules that regulate the general conduct between men and women are said to apply equally for grhasthas as they do for brahmacaris, vanaprasthas, and sannyasis. There are, of course, differences between them, as is implied by the duties inherent to the asramas themselves. We shall touch briefly on both the commonalities and the differences.

Common to all kinds of celibacy is what Srila Prabhupada calls the "basic principle of spiritual life." That principle is to restrict interaction between men and women in every spiritual order, and in ways that correspond to the purpose of that order of life. As Srila Prabhupada says, 'The injunction restricting association with women \[or women with men\] is the basic principle of spiritual life."

This common principle extends across a wide range of different practices. For example, brahmacaris and sannyasis are forbidden to even think about women, but grhasthas may indulge in sex during the period favourable for procreation, for the sake of progeny. Both fall in the category of celibacy.

In his word-for-word translation, Srila Prabhupada describes the spectrum of celibacy as

**"... continence or abstaining from misuse of one's semen (not indulging in sex with women other than one's own wife and not having sex with one’s own wife when sex is forbidden, like during the period of menstruation.)"**

Additionally Srila Prabhupada has given ample instructions on all aspects of celibacy. The challenge is whether devotees who are born to, raised in, and moulded by a sexually over-permissive society are willing to put those instructions to practice.

If we understand that the original cause of our bondage to this material world is material enjoyment, which finds its apex in sex-life, then we will embrace Srila Prabhupada's teachings not just in theory, but in practice. It is for this reason that the regular study of Vedic literature is so essential.

Although men may consider women to be the "weaker sex," the fact is that in the near proximity to women, they are like butter before fire. Narada says, "Woman is compared to fire, and man is compared to a butter pot. Therefore a man should avoid associating even with his own daughter in a secluded place. Similarly, he should also avoid association with other women. One should associate with women only for important business and not otherwise."

Narada's requirement for celibacy is strict. Therefore brahmacaris and sannyasis should avoid anything other than the most essential communication with women, while grhasthas should avoid being alone even with their own daughters, what then to speak of any woman other than their wives. Quite a cultural shift.

Other than remaining spiritually strong, celibacy is supported by austerity, cleanliness of the mind and body, satisfaction in one's varna and asrama, and friendship toward all living entities.

However, among these pillars of celibacy, cleanliness is pre-eminent, and it has already been defined and listed as one of the basic principles of religion. Cleanliness of the body, the mind, and the soul require the same acts of self-discipline, as does restraint from members of the opposite sex. Therefore, cleanliness is an essential aspect of celibacy. Where celibacy reigns, cleanliness also prevails. Conversely, where cleanliness is absent, there illicit association and prostitution will abound, and where uncontrolled association between the sexes is rampant, cleanliness will be spoiled.

Thus, without adhering to the principles of celibacy, one becomes a puppet in the plan of Kali, and one's prosperity, as that of one's dependents, gradually wanes. Srila Prabhupada offers a very contemporary version of celibacy for youth, which he proposes as official government policy:

"There must be compulsory marriage of young boys and girls attaining twenty-four years of age and sixteen years of age respectively. There is no harm in co-education in the schools and colleges, provided the boys and girls are duly married, and in case there is any intimate connection between a male and female student, they should be married properly without illicit relation. The divorce act is encouraging prostitution, and this should be abolished."

To conclude: the vow of celibacy taken by devotees at initiation is equivalent to the traditionally recognised "great vow, brhad-vrata. By this vow one becomes brilliant like fire and bums to ashes the propensity to perform material activities. Thus celibacy slackens the shackles of material life, and naturally draws one closer to Krsna's devotional service.

## Chapter Ten. Charity

Charity is one characteristic of varṇāśrama-dharma that has world-wide recognition and praise. Throughout India one will find wells, roads, temples, gardens, dharmasalas, and orchards that were gifts of charity from kings, merchants, and other pious souls. The long-standing tradition of householders in the upper three orders of society has been to voluntarily help those in need, and even more so to serve charitable, religious, or transcendental causes.

While such giving was not always free of motive, it was generally purifying for the donor. Based on the conviction that dharma would return to a donor an amount more than initially given, charity was a tradition that reinforced itself, and its vestiges remain visible today, albeit not with the same clarity of purpose as in days gone by.

Charity is compulsory for the three higher orders of society and for grhasthas. Krsna says,

*ijyadhyayana-danani*  
*sarvesam ca dvi-janmanam*  
*pratigraho 'dhyapanam ca*  
*brahmanasyaiva yajanam*

**“All twice̮born men—brahmanas, ksatriyas and vaisyas—must perform sacrifice, study the Vedic literature and give charity..."**

Considering that all Gaudiya Vaisnavas initiated by the pancaratrika system are twice born, that principle can be extended to mean all the varnas, and as we will see—if they have the resources—all the asramas. In other words, charity is a universal duty.

The Bhagavad-gita says that some sages will argue that vanaprasthas, sannyasis, and liberated souls need not give charity. However, Krsna's opinion is that even they are purified by acts of charity. Therefore since they are all recipients of gifts, these great souls should also give away something, if not everything, of what they have received. And since brahmacaris live solely for the benefit of their guru, we see from Srila Prabhupada's example how the spiritual master engages his disciples in charity. In ISKCON, brahmacaris and brahmacarinis learn to give the gift of transcendental knowledge in exchange for a charitable donation, and also to distribute prasada. Thus giving charity is for everyone.

But how much should one give?

While that controversial question is short, the answer could form a book. To summarise, Srila Prabhupada gives a variety of answers. The most common is fifty percent of income. And then there are variations on either side of that median. On one side is "at least fifty percent" and on the other up to fifty percent."The amount and how it is calculated may be up for argument, but Srila Prabhupada's expectation that one give some percentage of income is not. And an objective Vaisnava will see that while Srila Prabhupada was accommodating, he reiterated the fifty percent formula.

Practical application of that formula may be difficult for average wage earners whose income is already committed in many ways. However, those who are affluent—and especially those young devotees who are looking for a career outside ISKCON and have not yet made financial commitments to mortgages and so on—would do well to plan how they can meet that formula.

The plan has to be based on the implicit faith that doing what is right will give a right result, dharmo raksati raksitah. It means that, from day one of their employment, a young devotee sets aside half their income for Krsna's service and only lives on the other half. Those faithful souls who do so can be assured of spiritual and material prosperity.

To whom should one give, when, and how?

The best person to give to is Krsna. Srila Prabhupada has established ISKCON as Krsna's representative, because ISKCON's mission is to further Krsna's service. Devotees who are full-time representatives of the society—sannyasis, brahmanas, or other authorised members—should be given charity with the understanding that one hundred percent of what is given to them belongs to and goes to ISKCON's service. Scriptures therefore recommend that charity be given to first-class transcendentalists because they will fully utilise every penny they receive in Krsna s service.

Nowadays it has become fashionable to give donations to senior devotees with the proviso that they not use the funds for ISKCON, but for themselves or their projects. That was certainly not Srila Prabhupada's vision of charity. He warns that only very pure brahmanas can accept charity.Krsna also warns that if charity compromises their austerity, spirituality, and fame, brahmanas should accept alternate means of livelihood.The conclusion is that chanty should be given to a person who is engaged in serving the mission of Krsna consciousness. To recap: that means to a person serving in the mission of ISKCON; and in Hungary, in the service of MKTHK.

When charity is not of such a transcendental nature but is subject to the modes of nature, then time, place, and person become governing factors. And while charity in goodness is authorised, that in passion and ignorance is a waste of money, time, and energy. The aggregate of such inferior charity undermines its very purpose, which is to free one from material entanglement and to please the Supreme Lord.

Like devotional service, the result of charity depends on the faith of the donor. Pure faith yields a permanent result, other kinds of faith an impermanent one. Householders who are duty bound to give charity are protected from family trappings by their donations, elevated to Vaikuntha by building a temple and maintaining the deity of the Lord, and awarded with love of God by furthering Lord Caitanya's mission.

As there are guidelines for giving charity, there are guidelines for accepting it. Among the four orders, only pure brahmanas may accept charity. On the other hand, for vanaprasthas, living by charity—which quickly frees one from illusion is compulsory. Charity may be taken from înló seven, three, or one donors—as determined by one's vow—and from the four occupational orders beginning with brahmanas, as dictated by necessity.

Although ksatriyas are almost as qualified as brahmanas, even in an emergency they are forbidden to accept charity, what then to speak of the lower orders.

Since charity, like fame, is created by and ordained by the Lord, it is a quality and practice that is characteristic of godly men endowed with a divine nature. The story of Bali Maharaja is narrated in Srimad-Bhagavatam as the ultimate standard for giving charity, and is the inspiration for us to follow. Indeed, the following words of Bali Maharaja are fit to be remembered by all charitable souls:

*sulabha yudhi viprarse*  
*hy anivrttas tanu-tyajah*  
*na tatha tirtha ayate*  
*sraddhaya ye dhana-tyajah*

**"O best of the brahmanas! Many men have laid down their lives on the battlefield, being unafraid of fighting, but rarely has one gotten the chance to give his accumulated wealth faithfully to a saintly person who creates holy places."**

## Chapter Eleven. Reading Scripture

Reading scripture is mentioned twice in the list of thirty qualities to be cultivated and acquired by all human beings. It is both a religious and devotional practice. Without reading scripture, no one can be religious or attain pure devotion. It is therefore incumbent on all occupational and spiritual orders of life. No one is exempt from reading scripture. By reading scripture one will know what is right, what is wrong, and what one's occupational and spiritual duties are, and can thus integrate those values into one's life. Without reading scripture, one's life is based on speculation and whimsy, and Krsna says that such a person will be a failure in anything he attempts:

*yah sastra-vidhim utsrjya*  
*vartate kama-karatah*  
*na sa siddhim avapnoti*  
*na sukham na param gatim*

**"He who discards scriptural injunctions and acts according to his own whims attains neither perfection, nor happiness, nor the supreme destination."**

And yet it is often found that devotees do not read scriptures regularly, a phenomenon that Srila Prabhupada himself observed and lamented.

Srila Prabhupada says,

**"...to read Vedic literature, to understand Vedic literature, to act according to the direction of the Vedic literature. This is called svadhyaya-yajna."And in elaborating on svadhyaya as one of the twenty-one qualities, Srila Prabhupada describes the scriptures to be read by Vedic and non-Vedic cultures: "... reading of transcendental literatures like Bhagavad-gita, Srimad-Bhagavatam, Rāmāyaṇa and Mahabharata (or, for those not in Vedic culture, reading of the Bible or Koran)."**

While brahmanas and brahmacaris should devotee a good portion of their time to reading, everyone is expected to make some time, daily, for scriptural study or scriptural discussion. This is confirmed by the Bhagavatam with the words, nityam bhagavata-sevaya, "regularly serve Srimad-Bhagavatam."However, Vaisnava tradition offers the study of these aforementioned transcendental literatures not just in book form, but in dramatic performance, poetry recitation, dance, and song. The idea is that our entire cultural life should embody, and not diverge from, the histories and teachings of scriptures.

Varṇāśrama is about vaidhi-bhakti, regulated devotional service. One of the implications of regulation is that devotees prioritise their duties and schedule their time to facilitate those priorities. Since reading literature is a high priority, it always finds its way into the competent devotee's timetable.

The result of such reading, hearing, and seeing of scripture is to awaken devotion to Krsna, to always think of Him, and to learn to see everything in relation to Him. Krsna advises Arjuna that while performing one's prescribed duty one should think of Him, and such remembrance is possible by reading or hearing.

Conditioned souls will be tempted to think that reading the same book, like Srimad-Bhagavatam, or even one genre of literature, the Vedas, will be boring. However, such concern is unfounded. The nature of scriptural topics, especially about Krsna, is such that it becomes fresher and more relishable with every successive reading.

Even if in the beginning one finds reading scripture difficult, it is part and parcel of austerity for the mind and speech. As one becomes elevated from ignorance and passion to goodness, one's appreciation for reading scripture increases. Having come to goodness, study and practice of scripture invokes and sustains the Vedas within the practitioner, and thus one becomes learned and wise.

Thus by reading scripture under the guidance of brahmanas, Vaisnavas, or the spiritual master, one can very easily chart a true course in both the social and spiritual orders, and avoid the pitfalls of conditional life. Thus one is elevated to pure devotion and by further study of scripture attains liberation from material existence and the perfection of life. Vyasadeva says,

*sadyo hrdy avarudhyate 'tra krtibhih susrusubhis tat-ksanat*

**"As soon as one attentively and submissively hears the message of Bhagavatam, by this culture of knowledge the Supreme Lord is established within his heart."**

It should be noted that reading scripture is not just about becoming learned. Being steeped in scripture may not be for everyone. But everyone should be familiar with scriptures, even busy householders. After all, if Krsna is meant to be the centre of family life, hearing about him collectively, as a family function, is essential. It is for this reason that the Puranas have been compiled. Ideally, families should sit together morning and evening to read stories from Srimad-Bhagavatam, Krsna Book, Śrī Caitanya-caritamrta, and from the increasingly available devotee publications, especially Prabhupada-lilamrta.

Children have an insatiable urge to hear, read, and see, and an equally insatiable ability to absorb what they hear and see. If they are not given transcendental subject matter, they will turn to frivolous topics and thereby fill their hearts with material desires, rather than attachment to Krsna. And worse than that, they will watch television, videos, and movies. When that happens the purpose for conceiving them is more or less nullified, for their chances at pure devotion has become greatly compromised.

Therefore, as much as Krsna consciousness is about distributing books, it is equally about reading books.

## Chapter Twelve. Simplicity and Satisfaction

**Simplicity**

Simplicity should not be confused with simple-mindedness, which is an indication of little intelligence or judgement. Since simplicity is a prerequisite of the most learned brahmana scholar, it should be understood in proper context as an absence of shrewdness, pretentiousness, and deceit. From another perspective, simplicity may be appreciated as a natural demeanour that makes a person very easily understood in speech, action, and thought, and which, if summarised in one word, would be "sincerity".

Srila Prabhupada defines the Sanskrit words for simplicity, arjava and amaya, as "freedom from mental duplicity" and "without duplicity." His Divine Grace gives an example:

**"Simplicity means that without diplomacy one should be so straightforward that he can disclose the real truth even to an enemy."**

Krsna names simplicity as one of the nine natural qualities of a brahmana,while Narada lists it amongst the twenty-one qualities previously mentioned in chapter four. And so it should be, as simplicity is a godly quality and one of the constituents of knowledge.

Like all qualities, simplicity can be cultivated, and so Krsna says that doing so is both an austerity of the mind and the body. Checking duplicitous thoughts and expressing oneself openly in word and deed are practices that are incumbent on brahmanas and those cultivating a brahminical nature—which means all devotees.

Interestingly, simplicity is also a quality of sudras. Narada says, "Offering obeisances to the higher sections of society \[the brahmanas, ksatriyas, and vaisyas\], being always very clean, being free from duplicity \[simplicity\], serving one's master, performing sacrifices without uttering mantras, not stealing, always speaking the truth, and giving all protection to the cows and brahmanas—these are the symptoms of the sudra."

What about vaisyas and ksatriyas?

While it is understandable that in matters of diplomacy and trade one cannot always be as straightforward as one might like—politics sometimes means telling lies and business means bending the truth—still by nature, not by their conduct, both leaders and merchants should be simple-hearted. Indeed Srila Prabhupada insists that all members of society must have and cultivate this quality—no one is exempt for any reason:

**“As far as the brahminical quality of simplicity is concerned, not only should a particular order of life follow this principle, but every member, be he in the brahmacarya-asrama, grhastha-asrama, vanaprastha-asrama or sannyasa-asrama. One should be very simple and straightforward.”**

And of course that is why Narada says that every human being must possess the quality of simplicity.

Aside from making a conscientious effort to be guileless, freedom from the same can be attained by thinking and acting for the welfare of others—for the whole world. Deceit arises when one only thinks of or prioritises one's self-interest. But when one places the welfare—most notably the spiritual welfare—of others in the forefront, then one becomes free of the tendency to think in terms of "I" and "mine." Distributing books, prasada, and the holy name, speaking on Krsna's behalf, or supporting those activities helps loosen the knot in the heart so that one becomes a simple servant of Krsna and a well-wisher to all.

In conclusion, simplicity should not be misunderstood as the expressions of ungentlemanly thoughts, as is expressed by the urban idiom "tell it like it is." Rather, devotees must keep in mind Krsna's instruction on the austerity of speech, that devotees should speak words that while truthful are also "pleasing, beneficial, and not agitating to others."

**Satisfaction**

Devotees may reflect on the importance of this quality by comparison to the other twenty. By some standards it may be considered the most important of all. In Srila Prabhupada's words, santosa, satisfaction, means “being satisfied with that which is available without severe endeavour,” and as such it sets the benchmark by which other qualities like mercy, austerity, and cleanliness are practised.

While satisfaction can be taken to be synonymous with gratification, meaning the fulfilment of desires or needs, Srila Prabhupada's definition is set in the context of being content with the material conditions that come naturally as a consequence of one's karmic quota. The first verse of Śrī Isopanisad echoes this contentment with the words tena tyaktena bhunjitha ma grdhah kasya svid dhanam.

Commenting on satisfaction as a quality created by Krsna, Srila Prabhupada says, "Tusti, satisfaction, means that one should not be eager to gather more and more material goods by unnecessary activity. One should be satisfied with whatever is obtained by the grace of the Supreme Lord; that is called satisfaction.”

The following elaboration follows Srila Prabhupada's definitive insight into the meaning of satisfaction:

**As already mentioned, satisfaction—along with qualities like intelligence, knowledge, and truthfulness—is created by Krsna, and like simplicity and gravity it is one of the austerities of the mind. As with many qualities, satisfaction is a state of mind, which when absent there will certainly be absent in the senses. In other words, if the mind is not satisfied, the senses will not be. And in the complete absence of satisfaction one will be easily driven to sacrifice other qualities in order to satisfy insatiable material desires.**

Thus we have given special emphasis to its cultivation and refinement.

And while Krsna describes satisfaction as an outstanding and natural quality of both brahmanas and sudras, Srila Prabhupada and Narada explain that it must be also practised by, and present in, everyone.

Before examining how satisfaction is cultivated, let us see what Krsna means when He says that He has created satisfaction in human beings, and what the dynamics of satisfaction—real and imagined—are.

Narada tells the Pracetas,

*yatha taror mula-nisecanena*  
*trpyanti tat-skandha-bhujopasakhah*  
*pranopaharac ca yathendriyanam*  
*tathaiva sarvarhanam acyutejya*

**"By giving water to the root of a tree one satisfies its branches, twigs, and leaves, and by supplying food to the stomach one satisfies all the senses of the body. Similarly, by engaging in the transcendental service of the Supreme Lord one automatically satisfies all the demigods and all other living entities."**

The first part of the verse gives two examples of what is described in the second part, which essentially says that by pleasing Krsna with service, one becomes satisfied.

While Krsna is not in need of anything, He is nevertheless pleased when His devotees serve Him, just as a father is pleased by an insignificant gift from his son. Thus when Krsna says that He creates satisfaction, it means that Krsna creates a connection between Himself and all living entities, a connection by which they can sense a fraction of the pleasure and satisfaction He feels from their offerings of service.

The dynamics of that satisfaction are described in the two examples given in the verse. Krsna is the root of all existence and His satisfaction guarantees satisfaction in His servant. The unstated converse is that living beings who strive to gratify their senses by the whimsical dictates of their minds may experience temporary enjoyment, but they are never satisfied.

Thus the key to satisfaction is connection to Krsna through service, in the absence of which neither sense gratification nor renunciation can satisfy anyone.

The well-known verse beginning with bhaktih paresanu-bhavo viraktir equates bhakti with tusti, satisfaction.Reading that, and the earlier verse, one may wonder how one becomes satisfied with service to Krsna, which is an ever-expanding spiritual exchange. In his commentary to this verse Jiva Gosvami explains that while a devotee becomes disinterested in material acquisitions, His thirst for service and Krsna remain insatiable. That is the true nature of satisfaction: materially satisfied, spiritually thirsty.

In this regard Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura warns that while the abovementioned satisfaction manifests in proportion to the practitioner's devotion, there is a lag-time between the offering of service and appearance of the result of service—satisfaction.In the intervening period, devotees are recommended to exercise patience. Needless to say, the quality of devotion also determines the speed at which it bears the fruit of satisfaction.

It should be clarified that service to Krsna means service to Him and to His devotees, especially to spiritual masters, who are like His second bodies.

Based on the above principles, the practice and attainment of satisfaction is clearly based on engaging in service to Krsna and His devotees. Such service not only ensures satisfaction but also eradicates the taste for sense gratification.

The first step, therefore, is to be convinced that we are all eternal servants of Krsna, krsnera nitya-dasa.That conviction comes by hearing revealed scriptures and by accepting that our senses were created to be used in Krsna's service.

Thereafter a devotee undertakes activities for Krsna's satisfaction. Srila Prabhupada says,

**"If Krsna wants, the devotee can do anything which is ordinarily undesirable; and if Krsna does not want, he shall not do that which he would have ordinarily done for his own satisfaction."**

For example, devotees distribute books in conditions of excessive heat and cold, and they also refrain from habits to which they were long accustomed.

There are many other examples of service to Krsna that satiate a devotee, beginning with thinking and conversing about Him. Since the conditioned mind is habituated to thoughts of sense enjoyment, which only fuels dissatisfaction, it is best diverted by reading the Puranas and Mahabharata.

Eating foods in the mode of goodness is a service dear to all devotees and is fully effective, provided that it is cooked for and offered to Krsna before being respected.

More demanding a practice than honouring prasada is to constantly restrain oneself from over-endeavouring for that which is difficult to attain, and to be content with what comes naturally in the balanced execution of one's duties. This kind of training is very much integral to the grhastha-asrama, where parents always feel a compulsion to provide more and better facilities for the family. It is not necessary. As Krsna says, "One who faithfully worships God as the supreme controller experiences santosa, full satisfaction in whatever situation the Lord arranges.”

Devotees must keep in mind Krsna's warning that living for the satisfaction of the senses is a life passed in vain, for it is a faulty endeavour. Although it may appear to be like poison at first, living for the satisfaction of Krsna's senses will result in transcendental bliss beyond our imagination. It just requires knowledge of, conviction of, and dedicated practise of that truth.

A most helpful analogy in keeping oneself satisfied is given in the following verse by Narada Muni. It is such valuable advice that readers may want to memorise it. Following that is Narada's instructions on satisfaction, of which this wonderful sloka is a part:

*sada santusta-manasah*  
*sarvah sivamaya disah*  
*sarkara-kantakadibhyo*  
*yathopanat-padah sivam*

**"For a person who has suitable shoes on his feet, there is no danger even when he walks on pebbles and thorns. For him, everything is auspicious. Similarly, for one who is always self-satisfied there is no distress; indeed, he feels happiness everywhere."**

And here is Narada Muni's instruction on being satisfied:

**“Even if a man is poor, he should not endeavour to improve his economic condition just to maintain his body and soul together or to become a famous religionist. Just as a great python, although lying in one place, not endeavouring for its livelihood, gets the food it needs to maintain body and soul, one who is desireless also obtains his livelihood without endeavour.**

**"One who is content and satisfied and who links his activities with the Supreme Personality of Godhead residing in everyone's heart enjoys transcendental happiness without endeavouring for his livelihood. Where is such happiness for a materialistic man who is impelled by lust and greed and who therefore wanders in all directions with a desire to accumulate wealth?**

**"For a person who has suitable shoes on his feet, there is no danger even when he walks on pebbles and thorns. For him, everything is auspicious. Similarly, for one who is always self-satisfied there is no distress; indeed, he feels happiness everywhere.**

**"My dear King, a self-satisfied person can be happy even with only drinking water. However, one who is driven by the senses, especially by the tongue and genitals, must accept the position of a household dog to satisfy his senses.**

**"Because of greed for the sake of the senses, the spiritual strength, education, austerity, and reputation of a devotee or brahmana who is not self-satisfied dwindle, and his knowledge gradually vanishes.**

**"The strong bodily desires and needs of a person disturbed by hunger and thirst are certainly satisfied when he eats. Similarly, if one becomes very angry, that anger is satisfied by chastisement and its reaction. But as for greed, even if a greedy person has conquered all the directions of the world or has enjoyed everything in the world, still he will not be satisfied.**

**"O King Yudhisthira, many persons with varied experience, many legal advisers, many learned scholars, and many persons eligible to become presidents of learned assemblies fall down into hellish life because of not being satisfied with their positions."**

## Chapter Thirteen. Rendering Service to Saintly Persons

In a society wherein spiritual values are the norm and spiritual advancement the goal, the saints who represent these principles are highly respected and the object of service for everyone. For this reason varṇāśrama holds service to saintly persons as a core value.

While such a practice is applicable to all varnas and asramas, it is said that grhasthas have a special responsibility in this regard. Amongst the many kinds of saintly persons, Narada emphasises those who are samadrk, which Srila Prabhupada translates as "saintly persons who make no distinctions between one living being and another and who see every living being as a spirit soul."And among such sadhus, Vaisnavas are more special still, for they recognise that all souls are servants of Krsna. This chapter will centre around service to Vaisnavas, although the general principle applies to other holy men and women.

Scriptures emphasise that service to and association with Vaisnavas is not just a religious duty but a choice spiritual practice that Caitanya Mahaprabhu places on par with chanting Hare Krsna and hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam.Thus devotees of faith naturally seek out service to Vaisnavas, and in that way their material and spiritual advancement is assured.

Service to Vaisnavas is of such eminence that the Lord considers it to precede and excel even service to Him. Srila Prabhupada would often cite the following verse to make this crucial point:

*aradhananam sarvesam*  
*visnor aradhanam param*  
*tasmat parataram devi*  
*tadiyanam samarcanam*

**"My dear Devi! Although the Vedas recommend worship of demigods, the worship of Lord Visnu is topmost. However, above the worship of Visnu is the rendering of service to Vaisnavas, who are related to Lord Visnu."**

There are four principles of service to Vaisnavas: reception, respect, association, and service.And the discriminating householders should render these kinds of service according to Vaisnavas' level of advancement, which means increasingly elaborate service to the third, second, and first class devotees. If one lacks the discrimination to distinguish the relative spiritual strength of Vaisnavas, then one should worship all devotees equally and according to one's full ability.

In this regard some words of caution are in order: since a neophyte grhastha does not know how to properly relate to Vaisnavas, he will show prejudice to them based on their varna, asrama, initiated status, and bodily beauty, thus risking Vaisnava aparadha. Therefore such a grhastha should take guidance from an intermediate devotee on how to worship Vaisnavas and then make every effort to abandon his miserly mentality by rising to a higher status of devotion.

The general etiquette in receiving a Vaisnava is that a host offers obeisances immediately upon seeing his guest, greets them with words of welcome, and offers them a sitting place. After enquiring about the Vaisnava's welfare, one should offer them prasada and other suitable services according to the host's means and the guest's status. When a Vaisnava departs, they should be accompanied some distance and bade a sweet farewell. It is said that for a period of twelve years the family deity will refuse the worship of the grhastha who fails to properly receive a Vaisnava guest.

Grhastha-dharma means being educated in the details and intricacies of receiving and hosting Vaisnavas. That includes making distinction between non-devotee guests, what then to speak of Vaisnavas. For example, Srila Prabhupada wrote,

**"If a karmi is a friend, you just greet him with 'Hare Krsna' and with folded hands touch your forehead. If the karmi is a superior relative, then chant Hare Krsna and bow down to him on the ground. That should be the etiquette in our society transactions."**

Just as the reception for non-Vaisnavas varies with their status, the same holds true for Vaisnavas, among whom the welcome for one's gurus is more special still. For example, if two Vaisnavas—one more advanced than the other come at the same time, the senior should be greeted first with greater respect. And if these Vaisnavas come at different times, they should still be received with a welcome proportionate to their advancement.

Showing respect is the second aspect of serving Vaisnavas, and it is based upon similar principles as receiving them. In addition to seeing the soul, both worshipper and worshipped recognise the presence of the Supreme Lord in the other, and so offer respect accordingly.

And yet different Vaisnavas are offered different levels of worship.

Scriptures say that one must immediately offer obeisances upon seeing a sannyasi or if one fails to do so one must fast for the day.Additionally, Rupa Gosvami gives three levels of respect corresponding to the three levels of Vaisnavas—beginner, intermediate, and advanced. The acarya says that they should be honoured within the mind, with humble obeisances, and by menial service, respectively.Once again, if a grhastha or devotee is unable to determine the advancement of a Vaisnava, then he may offer him respect suitable to an advanced devotee—washing his feet for example. No offense is incurred by offering too much respect.

Association with Vaisnavas is the most general form of service and it includes physical interaction, developing a personal relationship, and receiving and giving spiritual guidance. And while even immature Vaisnavas should be offered kind words and respect, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura says that one should neither befriend nor hear from such devotees:

**"Even if one is a Vaisnava, if he is not of good character his company should be avoided although he may be offered the respect of a Vaisnava."**

On the other hand, the association of intermediate and advanced devotees should be sought out as objects of friendship and veneration. Such devotees may be served with gifts and prasada, and advanced devotees should especially be taken shelter of by revealing one's mind and enquiring confidentially. The invaluable guidance of such saints is the true reward for serving them.

Devotees may note that while we are obliged to treat all Vaisnavas in a friendly way, we are not meant to befriend them all. Devotees who are critical, addicted to sinful acts, and not of good character should be politely avoided. Service to Vaisnavas should not become a spiritual disservice to oneself.

Assisting Vaisnavas is the fourth and final aspect of service to them, and it is expressed in two main ways: serving their bodily needs (vapu) and serving their instructions (vani). Both are important. Giving gifts and offering prasada are two ways of serving Vaisnavas' vapu, and serving some aspect of Srila Prabhupada's mission is a way of serving any ISKCON Vaisnava's vani.

In both kinds of service, consideration should be given to devotees' spiritual advancement, age, seniority, institutional authority, and also to their needs. In consideration of the latter, Sanatana Gosvami warns,

**"One who sees a devotee of the Lord in great trouble and anxiety but fails to help him incurs the displeasure of the Lord."**

Additionally, devotees should be careful not to withhold assistance to Vaisnavas because of their varna, asrama, or gender. Scriptures warn that such mundane mentality will spoil one s devotion and, when institutionalised, contaminate ISKCON.

As always, Krsna is the perfect example of everything, including rendering service to Vaisnavas. Although Sudama Vipra was poverty stricken, Krsna welcomed him into His own bedroom, sat the brahmana on His bed, personally washed his feet and fed him while engaging Rukmini in service to him. Finally, although unfit for Him, Krsna accepted Sudama's very meagre gift of chipped rice, and in turn the Lord gifted His friend with immeasurable wealth.

Krsna's behaviour was not a drama to show us the importance of service to Vaisnavas. This pastime reveals Krsna's deep conviction that His own divine powers are attributed to His serving devotees. The Lord beautifully expresses that conviction in this way:

*yat-sevaya carana-padma-pavitra-renum*  
*sadyah ksatakhila-malam pratilabdha-silam*  
*na srir viraktam api mam vijahati yasyah*  
*preksa-lavartha itare niyaman vahanti*

**"Because I am the servitor of My devotees, My lotus feet have become so sacred that they immediately wipe out all sin, and I have acquired such a disposition that the goddess of fortune does not leave Me, even though I have no attachment for her and others praise her beauty and observe sacred vows to secure from her even a slight favour."**

## Chapter Fourteen. Gradually Taking Leave of Unnecessary Engagements

This chapter studies taking leaving of and observing the futility of unnecessary engagements, but in the reverse order to which Narada mentions them. The reason is that the reverse—observing and gradually taking leave of unnecessary activities—is the natural order in which the two occur.

**Observing the Futility of the Unnecessary Activities of Human Society**

What are those unnecessary engagements? They include family obligation and attachment, making a living, social and political obligation, and sports and entertainment, to name just a few.

The word iksa is pivotal to understanding these two qualities and has already been explained earlier as the ability to distinguish between good and bad. The word also means "discrimination," and in this verse Srila Prabhupada chooses to translate it as "discussing"—a very interesting and instructive rendition.

The purpose of Krsna consciousness is to free us from the material world, which Krsna labels as being both miserable and temporary, duhkhalayam asasvatam.But this process of liberation usually takes a lifetime, during which practitioners transition through at least three asramas—brahmacarya, grhastha, and vanaprastha. To succeed in this lengthy effort, devotees must be careful not to fall prey to the fault of niyamagraha—losing sight of the goal of varṇāśrama—and of neglecting the regulations that ensure their forward progress.

How to ensure such success?

Two things are recommended: always be conscious that everything in this world is an illusion, and ensure that one is becoming disentangled from that illusion.

To be aware of the ultimate futility of material activities requires a mature level of Krsna consciousness in which one sees things in their proper perspective. This is especially so in the grhastha-asrama, where family, home, and work oblige one to be more absorbed in material activities.

By regulated spiritual practise, studying scriptures, and above all by associating with saintly souls, devotees acquire and retain the vision to distinguish between reality and illusion. The nature of household life is to bind conditioned souls to its trappings; therefore, instead of trying to enjoy the results of the work, householders should offer these results to Krsna. How to do so can be understood through devotee association, and is beautifully expressed in this song by Thakura Bhaktivinoda:

*amar sarvasva-deho, geho anucar*  
*bhai, bandhu, dara, suta, dravya, dwara, ghar*

**"All my possessions: body, home, servants, brothers, friends, wife, sons, personal belongings, fencing and gateways...**

*se saba hoilo tava, ami hoinu das*  
*tomara grhete ebe ami kori bas*

**"...all of these things are now Yours, for I have become Your servant. I am but a mere occupant in Your house.**

*tumi grha-svami, ami sevaka tomar*  
*tomara sukhete cesta ekhona amar*

**"You are the owner of the house, and I am Your most obedient servant. My only activity now is endeavouring for Your happiness."**

Moreover, householders must be vigilant not to fall into the trap of endeavouring too hard for economic development or satisfaction of the senses. To guard against such a danger they must practise being satisfied only with what they need for maintenance according to time and place, and not engage in any forbidden work, ugra-karma. This sattvika lifestyle keeps devotees aloof from unnecessary mundane activities and its deleterious effects.

Part of the grha-dharma of a family head is to engage and edify his wife, children, other family members, and friends in the same lifestyle, so as to enable them to also progress to life's goal. If, however, one's family members and acquaintances insist on pursuing a life of passion and ignorance, then those who are wise may remember the teachings of Srimad-Bhagavatam:

*jnatayah pitarau putra*  
*bhratarah suhrdo 'pare*  
*yad vadanti yad icchanti*  
*canumodeta nirmamah*

**"An intelligent man in human society should make his own program of activities very simple. If there are suggestions from his friends, children, parents, brothers or anyone else, he should externally agree, saying, 'Yes, that is all right,' but internally he should be determined not to create a cumbersome life in which the purpose of life will not be fulfilled."**

Thus the introspective devotees, through proper deliberation, gradually come to the conclusion that they must give up attachment to wife, family, and the rest, and transfer their attachments fully to Krsna.

**Gradually Taking Leave of Unnecessary Engagements**

Having concluded the need for detachment from their families, devotees then begin the conscious effort to take leave of material attachments and the activities that cause them. This detachment is like a counter current in a river: on the surface one appears to be materially attached, but beneath the surface material attachment wanes day by day.

Continued association with saintly persons fuels this process of detachment, as does enthusiastic attention to the pastimes of the Lord as narrated in scriptures like Srimad-Bhagavatam. Gradually devotees become fully purified at heart and in that purified state they see all living entities, including family members, as spirit souls. Moreover, devotees become convinced that all work to maintain the body is a burden that has value only to the extent that it facilitates service to Krsna.

Working to keep body and soul together, taking only as much as required, and abandoning all sense of proprietorship, a devotee is gradually freed from affection for wife, husband, and children, exactly like a person awakening from a dream. In this wakened state a grhastha may live in human society unattached to family affairs, although to ordinary people they externally appear very much attached. And while some devotees—even though neophyte—may appear to live like paupers, in truth they are the most opulent of all, for they have conquered the Supreme Lord, who is never conquered by anyone.

## Chapter Fifteen. Remaining Silent and Grave

Rupa Gosvami begins his summary guide to devotional service with an instruction to control the urges of the tongue:

*vaco vegam manasah krodha-vegam*  
*jihva-vegam udaropastha-vegam*  
*etan vegan yo visaheta dhirah*  
*sarvam apimam prthivim sa sisyat*

**"A sober person who can tolerate the urge to speak, the mind's demands, the actions of anger, and the urges of the tongue, belly and genitals is qualified to make disciples all over the world."**

This verse shows the connection between the three urges of speech, thought, and body. Speech, being first in the sequence, means that controlling the urge to speak is essential to complete self-control. Since varṇāśrama is for the self-controlled, it can be said that a symptom of varṇāśrama is the subject matter that its members do or do not speak about. Such controlled speech is called mauna.

There are different meanings and applications to being silent, which, as we shall see, is also a feature and indication of gravity. Generally people understand the vow of mauna to be avoiding all talk. And that is suitable for those who have nothing sensible to speak about. However, the essence of mauna is control of speech, and for devotees that means avoiding frivolous talk or gossip, and speaking only about Krsna. That is the perfection of remaining silent.

The effect of such silence is that others cannot readily know what is on one's mind, and that is the definition of gravity. Thus a silent person is also grave, and the two qualities are consequently inseparable.

Silence is also a potency of Krsna, and in addition to it contributing to the austerity of speech, it is the key to secrecy. The Lord says, maunam caivasmi guhyanam, "of secret things I am silence."

While controlling the urge to speak is a duty of all humans, along with studying the Vedas it is an activity that must be further cultivated by brahmacaris. The sannyasis' staff of renunciation also represent mauna, and renunciates are considered fallen if they cannot command such control.

Additionally, because both speech and taste are dependent on the tongue, they are interrelated. Devotees must therefore control their speaking habits in order to control their eating habits and subsequently the other connected senses.

As with celibacy, silence is often referred to as a vow, and devotees feel further empowered in controlling the urge to speak by accepting a self-composed vow. There is no secret what such a vow would entail: speaking only of things related to Krsna—if not always directly then at least indirectly. This is the practice of mystic yogis who know that without adhering to such a vow they will never control their minds or senses. Even ordinary people who have only a shadow of sobriety, and are unwilling to advertise their minds and lives to the world, know that silence is the only way.

Such conduct gains respect in society, what to speak of among devotees. For while a talker may be temporarily entertaining, he or she is never taken seriously by either the cultured or the mature. Silence thus garners respect, bestows desired benedictions, and makes one steadfast in the battle of conquering the sex urge through restricted eating habits.

To resist the demands of speech is certainly an austerity, but devotees take solace that by such austerity they are protected from the material energy. Indeed, the best form of speech is sankirtana—talking about Krsna or chanting His names. That form of speech not only liberates one but purifies the heart and makes one addicted to further sankirtana.

When Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami asked Lord Caitanya for instructions on sannyasa, the Lord's answer was brief:

*gramya-katha na sunibe, gramya-varta na kahibe*  
*bhala na khaibe ara bhala na paribe*  
*amani manada hana krsna-nama sada la'be*  
*vraje radha-krsna-seva manase karibe*

**"Do not talk like people in general or hear what they say. You should not eat very palatable food, nor should you dress very nicely. Do not expect honour, but offer all respect to others. Always chant the holy name of Lord Krsna, and within your mind render service to Radha and Krsna in Vrndavana."**

The bulk of these instructions relate to the control of speech—what to say and what not to say. It begins by exercising caution in hearing "village talk." To be concise, that means gossip. In today's world it translates to debating sports, politics, and the weather, and hearing and watching radio, internet, television, and movies. Sharing the content of these aforementioned items is the electronic form of gossip.

But, if one's heart is free of such mundane subjects, it will be very easy to control one's speaking habits. And to keep the tongue from gaining a vantage point, devotees should remain moderate in their eating habits. But most importantly, their minds should be absorbed in thoughts of Krsna, in preaching, and in service. Additionally, their tongues should be constantly vibrating the Lord's holy names. These are the essential practices by which devotees gain mastery over their tongues.

Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati also adds reading mundane literature and philosophy to the list of things from which devotees should keep distant. Excessive education also falls in the same category, for it robs one of a short life in which krsna-katha is the only path to sanity. Bhaktivinoda Thakura says,

*jada-vidya jata, mayara vaibhava,*  
*tomara bhajane badha*  
*moha janamiya, anitya samsare,*  
*jivake koraye gadha*

**“\[O Krsna!\] All the so-called knowledge of this world is born of the flickering potency of Your illusory energy (maya). It is an impediment to the execution of devotional service to You. Indulgence in mundane knowledge verily makes an ass of the eternal soul by encouraging his infatuation with this temporary world.**

When cultivated, remaining silent and grave helps control sex desire. By contrast, excessive irreligious sexual activity devastates devotees' ability to remain silent and grave, as it does to be clean and merciful.

In conclusion, the health of a varṇāśrama society will be seen by the content of its members' communication. Speaking krsna-katha will be an indication of a thriving swan-like society, and uncontrolled speech of a crows' culture. That is the conclusion of Srimad-Bhagavatam:

**"Those words which do not describe the glories of the Lord, who alone can sanctify the atmosphere of the whole universe, are considered by saintly persons to be like unto a place of pilgrimage for crows. Since the all-perfect persons are inhabitants of the transcendental abode, they do not derive any pleasure there."**

## Chapter Sixteen. Spiritual Knowledge

Once again Narada Muni mentions an activity of human beings which may be the most important of all. The reason for its eminence is that self-identification, atma-vimarsana, is the basis of a society's values and goals.

All societies in this world are based on bodily self-identification, resulting in the present proliferation of social structures that reflect the many races, colours, and nationalities around the world. But varṇāśrama-dharma is based on spiritual self-identification, which means that it is a universal, hence a uniquely singular, social structure. Because spiritually everyone is the same, equal.

Even a social system that uses varṇāśrama's divisions and structure but is based on bodily identification is called asura-varṇāśrama, the varṇāśrama of demons.And the Varṇāśrama that is based on spiritual self-identification is called daiva-varṇāśrama, spiritual Varṇāśrama. That is what we want to establish in Hungary and in ISKCON.

The phrase atma-vimarsana translates as "self-knowledge" or "research into one's identity." We see that self-knowledge is undeniably Krsna's first priority in the Bhagavad-gita, and it was by far the philosophical topic on which Srila Prabhupada spoke more than any other.

Bodily self-identification means that sense gratification within the term of people's stay in this body becomes the priority. And history has shown that hedonistic life quickly eclipses the spiritual values that religions try to protect and promote. This is the rise and current reign of what is called the secular society.

The lifestyle of secular societies is clearly visible around the world today in materialism, consumerism, and sense gratification, the values and activities of which are transmitted to people through education, entertainment, culture, economy, politics—indeed through every sphere of life. The result is that after a long spiritual evolution to reach the human form of life, people of the world will fall back into animal species or lower.

The basic flaw of today's societies—which results in such a catastrophic failing to the world's people—is a lack of atma-vimarsana, or contemplation on whether we are this temporary body or something more substantial—an eternal conscious being. In short, they do not know what life is or what it is for.

Srila Prabhupada so often lamented the lost condition of the world:

**"Where is such thing as transmigration of the soul being taught in classroom?"**

Srila Prabhupada's answer to his own question was ISKCON. His books were the texts elucidating the culture that flourishes when everyone identifies as being spirit soul, and Varṇāśrama is the social structure that houses such a spiritual culture.

And we see the difference that knowing our true identity makes. Devotees have a different approach to life: they see the world as a training ground for another destination, and this life their golden opportunity to reach that destination.

And yet we also see that some devotees still become victim to the allurements of this world, that others are complacent about their good fortune, and still others dabble with the temptations of the senses. This may be attributed in part to a lack of proper atma-vimarsana, which no doubt is connected to insufficient study and discussion of scripture.

We can see the power of having even a little training in atma-vimarsana by the empowerment and wisdom it gives devotee children. If those children are then systematically educated in the understanding of what it means to be a spiritual part of Krsna and the importance of embracing a corresponding lifestyle, they will then grow to become the kind of Vaisnavas whom Srila Prabhupada envisaged could light fires by mantra.

And when the same study of the science of the soul is undertaken by adults, they will not only be ideal examples for successive generations, but they will be able to fulfil their goal of becoming lovers of Krsna. In that way a Varṇāśrama society can be gradually built up on a firm foundation: knowledge of who we are.

## Chapter Seventeen. Food Distribution and Divine Vision

On countless occasions Srila Prabhupada mentions the distribution of prasada along with book distribution and chanting Hare Krsna. As can be seen from Narada Muni's instruction, anna-adya-adeh samvibhago bhutebhyas ca yatharhata, the distribution of food—which when offered to Krsna becomes prasada—is a timeless Vedic tradition, not one recently introduced by Srila Prabhupada.It is an integral part of the eternal sankirtana movement of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, and thus an act of worship. Narada Muni explains how prasada distribution is worship:

**"Therefore, my dear King, first offer prasada unto the brahmanas and the demigods, and after sumptuously feeding them you may distribute prasada to other living entities according to your ability. In this way you will be able to worship all living entities—or, in other words, the supreme living entity within every living entity."**

A Vaisnava knows that Krsna is in every living entity, human and otherwise, and feeding them is akin to feeding the Lord—not because everyone is Krsna, but because they are His parts and parcels. And offering food to others is a form of worship in which there is no qualification required of the worshipper. It is accessible to all.
