Aside from being an act of worship, prasada distribution is also a form of preaching, an act of mercy, and a traditional expression of love.

Because food offered to Krsna is spiritual, when consumed it purifies the heart and makes the recipient qualified to take further interest in Krsna consciousness. There are endless stories of how devotees become practitioners simply by first eating prasada. Therefore prasada distribution is preaching.

In addition to preaching, distributing food is an act of kindness which is universally appreciated and cuts across all religious, cultural, and political boundaries. Everyone must eat to survive. And while Vaisnavas see all conditioned souls—be they materially impoverished or wealthy—as being in need of Krsna's grace, devotees are not indifferent to the hungry and the poor.

Srimad-Bhagavatam defines an intermediate devotee as someone who shows mercy to those who are in need.In Srila Prabhupada's words, "So for the balisa, innocent, it is our duty to show them mercy, give them food, give them shelter... "

Most devotees live a very comfortable life and do not know what hunger is. But one who has known hunger knows the importance of prasada distribution and how it is appreciated by the recipients. In a lecture in 1973, Srila Prabhupada alluded to a "long history" in which he lived in poverty and his meals were taken from the many temples he would visit. Srila Prabhupada knew what it meant to be in need.

Finally, for Vaisnavas prasada distribution is an expression of love that is common to all cultures and religions. Rupa Gosvami says offering and accepting prasada are two of the six exchanges of love between Vaisnavas.And needless to say, Vaisnavas love not just each other, but all living entities.

Thus prasada distribution is everyone's duty, and more so of those who can afford it. With the imbalance in global wealth today there is a constant scarcity of food. Prosperous men cannot live comfortably while twenty thousand people daily die of hunger in the world. The duty of those who have is to share with those who have not, including with animals. That formula contributes to global prosperity.

In ISKCON, prasada distribution takes places in temples, restaurants, at festivals, and in Food for Life distribution points. Lord Caitanya would follow kirtana performances with prasada distribution, and Srila Prabhupada says that such activity is an integral aspect of temple worship. Prabhupada even encouraged devotees who do prison preaching to distribute prasada to inmates.

But while institutionally prasada distribution is widespread, it remains sorely absent in devotees' households. Srila Prabhupada sets the following standard for grhasthas:

**"In the Bhagavatam it is stated that a householder, before taking his lunch, should call out on the street, 'If anyone is hungry, please come to my place and eat! And he should see that in his household even the lizard does not go hungry. Even a snake should not hungry."**

Srila Prabhupada said that his father had at least three to five guests for lunch daily. If householders cannot do that, they can always have some packaged prasada with them to offer to whomever they meet, especially the poor. That is the minimum. Certainly they can also support ISKCON's Food for Life activities, but direct service to others is also essential for householders in order to help loosen the knots of possessiveness that come with family life.

His Divine Grace also recommends that devotees running businesses distribute prasada to employees. Among other benefits, such activity will pre-empt conflict between labour and management.

One may wonder to whom and in what order prasada should be distributed.

When a variety of souls are present Srila Prabhupada gives the following guideline:

"Whenever there is a ceremony for distribution of prasada, the prasada is offered first to the brahmanas, then to the children and old men, then to the women, and then to animals like dogs and other domestic animals."

The final topic to consider is "what should we distribute by way of prasada?"

While the prasada devotees hand out to beggars on the street or at traffic lights may be a small package of nuts or cookies, such gifts are more for purification than nutrition. Srila Prabhupada's minimum for a meal was khichri, but he preferred "sumptuous prasada." The letter below outlines just exactly what sumptuous means. Srila Prabhupada assured devotees that since everything belongs to Krsna, if we are liberal in this act of charity then the Lord will supply us with what we need for ourselves and with what we need to give to others.

**Letter to: All ISKCON Temple Presidents**

**Calcutta**

**January 18, 1977**

**To All ISKCON Temple Presidents,**

**Please accept my blessings. Now you must arrange in each temple there must be sufficient stock of prasada for distribution. You can keep first class cooks, two or three, and they should be always engaged. Whenever any guest comes, he must get prasada. This arrangement must be made, that the cooks prepare ten-twenty servings at a time, of puris and subji, and you can add halava and pakoras, and the visitors must be supplied immediately. Whenever a gentleman comes, he must be served. As the twenty servings are being distributed, immediately the cooks prepare another twenty servings and store it. At the end of the day, if no one comes, our own men will take, so there is no loss. You cannot say, 'it is finished,' 'it is not cooked yet,' 'there is no supply for cooking" etc. This must be enforced rigidly. The temple is managed by Srimati Radharani, Laksmiji; so why should there be want? Our philosophy is, if anyone comes, let him take prasada, chant Hare Krsna and be happy. Everything is being supplied by Krsna, Krsna is not poor, so why we should deny them? This should be done at any cost. There is no difficulty, it simply requires nice management. At the end of the day you may sell or give away. If we believe that Krsna is providing and maintaining everyone, then why should we be misers? This means losing faith in Krsna, and thinking that we are the doers and suppliers. We are confident Krsna will supply! Let the whole world come, we can feed them. So please do this nicely, begin at once.**

**Hoping this meets you all in good health.**

**Your ever well-wisher,**  
**C. Bhaktivedanta Swami**

**Seeing Every Soul as a Part of the Supreme Lord**

The successful implementation of varṇāśrama-dharma depends upon a certain mentality, or better yet, a certain realisation by its members. The Lord describes this consciousness in the Bhagavad-gita:

*vidya-vinaya-sampanne*  
*brahmane gavi hastini*  
*suni caiva sva-pake ca*  
*panditah sama-darsinah*

**"The humble sages, by virtue of true knowledge, see with equal vision a learned and gentle brahmana, a cow, an elephant, a dog and a dog-eater \[outcaste\]."**

In this verse Lord Krsna uses the phrase sama-darsinah, which translates as "equal vision."

Narada's words to Yudhisthira Maharaja convey the same message in a specific context. The sage says, tesv atma-devata-buddhih, which means, tesu, in regard to all the living entities who should be fed prasada—especially those in the human form—one should have the understanding, buddhi, that their self, atma, is a part of the Supreme Lord devata.

The context of Narada's definition of this twenty-first quality implies that without such vision, one will not be inclined to feed other living entities. Moreover, as we shall see, many of the other qualities of human beings remain stunted if devotees lack the intelligence to understand the equality of souls behind the many different bodies they inhabit. In composition, what is mentioned last is generally the most important. Let's see why this is so with this quality:

While scripture uses many terms to describe equal vision, the quotes above describe two kinds: the lesser vision is the developed intelligence that comes from having acquired proper knowledge through education; the superior vision is the actual realisation that comes from spiritual advancement through purification of the heart—Krsna describes the former, Narada the latter.

All living entities are expected to cultivate the liberal intelligence that does not discriminate between living entities based on their different bodies, or different bodily designations. The culture that has this religious principle at its core is known as the Aryan culture.

When Prahlada Maharaja witnessed first-hand his father's enmity towards Lord Visnu, he described the dualist distinction of friend and enemy as one induced by the influence of the material energy. Identification with matter, especially with the material body, makes one identify with the actions and reactions of matter. The interactions of matter then cause many varieties of bodies, which subsequently act in many ways under the sway of the modes of nature. This false identification is called mano-dharma, or mental speculation, and it influences a conditioned soul to react differently to different things, conditions, and living entities, thinking "this is good" or "this is bad."Allowed to go unchecked, such a mentality degrades until people become like Hiranyakasipu, demoniac.

The understanding or realisation that underlies equal vision is twofold. It is to see the soul in every living entity as part of Lord Krsna, as well as to see Lord Krsna Himself present within every living entity.

Education of children and adults must inculcate the equal vision understanding, and this cultivation cannot be emphasised enough. When by purification one comes to the stage of spiritual realisation called brahma-bhuta, such a vision and its ensuing conduct is spontaneous.

*brahma-bhutah prasannatma*  
*na socati na kanksati*  
*samah sarvesu bhutesu*  
*mad-bhaktim labhate param*

**"One who is thus transcendentally situated at once realises the Supreme Brahman and becomes fully joyful. He never laments or desires to have anything. He is equally disposed toward every living entity. In that state he attains pure devotional service unto Me."**

Preceding such advancement, however, devotees must learn to interact and relate to all living beings as befitting one who knows "we are not this body."

We have earlier mentioned that equal vision is a core principle of varṇāśrama. When it is absent, then the material distinction between varnas and asramas immediately sets in, and we have daiva-varṇāśrama no more. When devotees identify exclusively with others' bodies, neglecting the soul and Krsna therein, the balance required to counterweigh seeing others' conditioned nature is lost. The result is that a plethora of biases like racism, sexism, and casteism rear their ugly heads.

In such an environment people become miserly, secular, sectarian, and political. As a result they fail in the duties incumbent upon civilised human beings, including distributing prasada, hosting guests, and dispensing charity.

However, where equal vision is spoken of in schools, homes, and temples, there devotees are equipoised. Such cultured Vaisnavas do not value others based on duties or appearance, nor do they think of others in terms of lesser or greater, lower class or higher class.

Devotees often ask how they can be freed of envy. Cultivating equal vision is the way.

Aside from educating devotees, devotees must be taught to put equal vision to practice by showing compassion to all through preaching, by learning how to be respectful to everyone, and by feeling the pain and happiness of others. By such conduct devotees will not only worship Krsna, gradually bringing Him under their control, but they will also have the co-operation of the demigods in their passage through life. Indeed, Lord Nrsimha says that by their presence such devotees will purify even the most contaminated land and the most unfortunate people.

Maharaja Rantideva is an example of a great soul who saw every living entity in relationship to the Lord, and as a result he became very famous for his acts of charity.

Sukadeva Gosvami describes how, after fasting for forty-eight days without even water, Rantideva was about to break his fast. Just at that time a brahmana appeared, and so the king properly fed his guest, seeing him as the Lord's representative. When the king was again about to eat, then a sudra appeared, and Rantideva treated him just as he had the brahmana. When a third guest appeared, Rantideva fed him, and since there was nothing left he settled on drinking, but when a thirsty guest appeared, the king offered that person his water. Of course this was all Krsna's arrangement to demonstrate to us how a devotee should see everyone as being no different than the Lord. Being pleased with Rantideva's equal vision, Krsna blessed the king with His confidential service. If we follow Rantideva's example, Krsna will bless us in the same way.

After concluding the twenty-one qualities, Narada happily exclaims, rajan, "O Maharaja Yudhisthira!" and then begins to describe the nine limbs of sadhana-bhakti, which we shall touch on in the following chapter.

## Chapter Eighteen. The Nine Limbs of Pure Devotion

After the twenty-one religious qualities, Narada Muni mentions a further nine devotional qualities, bringing the total to thirty, trimsal-laksana. The comparative merits of the two groups in their varṇāśrama role should be clearly understood: the devotional acts are the major ingredients and the religious qualities the minor; the devotional acts are the basis of daiva-varṇāśrama and the religious qualities their limbs; the devotional acts are the foundation and the religious qualities the ornaments.

The current portion of this volume elaborates on the twenty-one qualities in depth and encourages their cultivation. The purpose of such elaboration is to assist devotees in acquiring the divine qualities that manifest from the offenseless devotional practice expressed in the well-known words of Prahlada Maharaja:

*yasyasti bhaktir bhagavaty akincana*  
*sarvair gunais tatra samasate surah*  
*harav abhaktasya kuto mahad-guna*  
*mano-rathenasati dhavato bahih*

**"One who has unflinching devotion for the Personality of Godhead has all the good qualities of the demigods. But one who is not a devotee of the Lord has only material qualifications that are of little value. This is because he is hovering on the mental plane and is certain to be attracted by the glaring material energy."**

Devotees sometimes question why they should cultivate the good qualities of the demigods, when those traits automatically manifest as a consequence of practising bhakti. Isn't that the message of this verse?

The answer is found in the proper understanding of the kind of devotion being mentioned. The phrase bhaktir bhagavaty akincana indicates devotion "without any motive" or "without material desire."That is the characteristic of a high-grade intermediate devotee, madhyama-adhikari. Bhaktivinoda Thakura names it as a characteristic of devotees at the stage of taste, ruci, which Jiva Gosvami confirms, adding that it is symptomatic of devotees who are desireless.

One implication of this verse is that devotees situated at a less mature devotion, such as anartha-nivrtti, may cultivate, but will not fully realise, these qualities. Therefore—and this is the crucial point—to help them come to higher stages of devotion, devotees need the assistance of the mode of goodness. Cultivating the twenty-one qualities does that, and in time devotees' bhakti truly becomes akincana.

In His own dissertation on varṇāśrama, Lord Krsna affirms His desire for devotees to practise the twenty-one qualities and summarises them in His own words:

**"My dear Uddhava, general cleanliness, washing the hands, bathing, performing religious services at sunrise, noon, and sunset, worshipping Me, visiting holy places, chanting japa, avoiding that which is untouchable, uneatable, or not to be discussed, and remembering My existence within all living entities as the Supersoul—these principles should be followed by all members of society through regulation of the mind, words, and body."**

In the same way that one primes a pump by pouring a little water into it so that large quantities of water can be siphoned from the ground, practising a good quality like cleanliness will help the natural quality of cleanliness manifest from the purified heart—and so it is with the other twenty qualities.

Those qualities of a pure devotee are the expressions of the soul's nature, which are often described as twenty-six in number.Discerning readers will easily see that the twenty-one qualities we have discussed are contained within the twenty-six. However, there is a difference between the two. The cultivated qualities are the material reflection, abhasa, of the twenty-six spiritual qualities.

A second implication of the yasyasti bhaktir verse—one that this chapter takes pains to stress—is that the good qualities cannot be properly cultivated and will not become lasting ornaments of a devotee without practising the nine limbs of bhakti.

This is also explained in the following words of Narada:

*naiskarmyam apy acyuta-bhava-varjitam*  
*na sobhate jnanam alam niranjanam*  
*kutah punah sasvad abhadram isvare*  
*na carpitam karma yad apy akaranam*

**"Knowledge of self-realisation, even though free from all material affinity, does not look well if devoid of a conception of the Infallible \[God\]. What, then, is the use of fruitive activities, which are naturally painful from the very beginning and transient by nature, if they are not utilised for the devotional service of the Lord?"**

In other words, even if one has the twenty-one qualities in full but is not a practising devotee, then those qualities will wane and disappear. That is because those qualities are material, therefore temporary, and neither spiritual nor permanent. So they become temporary assets at best.

An example is Narakasura, who was the son of the Lord. By the bad association of Banasura, he became a demon. In other words, by being separated from devotional service Naraka lost his good qualities.

Such fall-down is forecast in the yasyasti bhaktir verse. For this reason, the company of devotees in which one is inspired to rise to higher standards of devotion is the basis of becoming a good person. Good association means devotees who are adept at practising the nine limbs of pure devotional service. Better yet are those devotees who have perfected them.

In conclusion, to become a good devotee one must become a good person. But even if one is a good person, if one is not a devotee, what to speak of a good devotee, such goodness is useless.

## Part Three. Duties and Characteristics of the Varnas

## Chapter Nineteen. The Acaryas' Desire for Daiva-Varṇāśrama

**Establishing Varṇāśrama**

Earlier sections of this book elaborated on Srila Prabhupada's request that his followers fulfil his mission to establish varṇāśrama-dharma in ISKCON and in the world.

Srila Prabhupada said,

**"In order to serve the mass of people, to bring them to the ideal position, we should try to introduce this varṇāśrama, not that we are going to be candidates of varṇāśrama. It is not our business. But to teach them how the world will be in peaceful position we have to introduce."**

Re-establishing varṇāśrama was not Srila Prabhupada's innovation, but rather the vision of earlier acaryas. Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura opined that introducing varṇāśrama amongst Vaisnavas, and from them to society at large, would counteract sahajiyaism and casteism and resolve the social ills plaguing the world.

The Thakura wrote to his transcendental son Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura, "People of this world who are proud of their own aristocratic birth attack the pure Vaisnavas. Thus they commit offences. The solution to the problem is to establish the order of daiva-varṇāśrama-dharma—something you started doing. You should know that to be the real service to the Vaisnava. Because pure devotional conclusions are not being preached, all kinds of superstition and bad concepts are being called devotion by such pseudo sampradayas as sahajiya and ativadi.”

Bhaktivinoda Thakura appealed to his countrymen to do away with casteism, reject the imperfect social systems of the west, and return to varṇāśrama. In his Krsna-samhita he writes, "O great souls! You who wish to engage in the welfare of your country, please re-establish this faultless system given by your forefathers after collectively refining it!... If the Varṇāśrama system is faultlessly re-established, then India will advance in all respects."Bhaktivinoda Thakura concludes his appeal by explaining that the purpose of Varṇāśrama is to spiritually elevate practitioners by engaging them in duties in which Krsna is the centre.

**Daiva-varṇāśrama**

In the earlier quote the word daiva, meaning divine, qualified the kind of Varṇāśrama followed by Vaisnavas. Daiva-varṇāśrama is the social structure for those fortunate souls who have embraced devotion to Krsna as their life. This social structure for devotees is distinct from the regular Varṇāśrama of non-devotees, and completely distinct from asura-varṇāśrama, which is based on hereditary considerations. In this book, unless otherwise specified, the word "varṇāśrama" means "daiva-varṇāśrama."

Varṇāśrama is meant to regulate sense gratification which is also the purpose of vaidhi-bhakti. Varṇāśrama being favourable for and connected to devotional service, is counted as one of its secondary limbs.Vedanta-sutra also encourages devotees, especially neophytes, to follow varṇāśrama as a means of cultivating transcendental knowledge.

The texts say, *vihitatvad asrama-karmapi*, ''Scriptures order that devotees should follow the duties of the asramas," and sahakaritvena ca, "Because by doing so devotees will become steeped in knowledge."In this way, the conduct and duties enjoined by varṇāśrama become assistants of both transcendental knowledge and of bhakti. In Bhaktivinoda Thakura's words, "Varṇāśrama-dharma is like the foundation of one's supreme occupational duty."

And while liberated devotees may no longer be internally dependent on varṇāśrama, it generally remains their visible conduct in order to set a suitable example for others less advanced. Commenting on sruti, Baladeva Vidya- bhusana says that even devotees who meditate on and serve the Lord with love follow the codes of varṇāśrama for the sake of people in general, even as they engage in devotional activities like lovingly hearing of Krsna's pastimes.

The references cited above are a few of the instructions given by previous acaryas on the relevance of varṇāśrama to devotional service. From them we can see that the second half of Srila Prabhupada's mission was the mission of his predecessors. As Srila Prabhupada's dutiful followers, we too must adopt that mission by beginning to apply it in our own lives.

## Chapter Twenty. Principles Underlying Devotees' Nature and Work

**Conditioning Shapes Nature (bhava) and Work (karma)**

Varṇāśrama is not just a good idea had by ancient sages. It is the social system consistent with the way the material world was created, and how the human beings within it are conditioned. It takes into account the all-pervasive characteristics of the modes of nature, and recommends a lifestyle favouring the mode of goodness that in turn spontaneously gives rise to interest in religious principles, the practise of which awakens transcendental knowledge. And as the Hamsa-avatara says, "If one uses his mind and body to cultivate knowledge, then such enlightenment destroys the influence of the modes of nature that generated one's body."In short: one becomes fully Krsna conscious and returns to Godhead.

And Krsna says, *tasya kartaram api mam*, "I am the creator of this \[varṇāśrama\] system." Therefore we can rest assured that varṇāśrama is perfect, and like gravity, applicable to all peoples, at all times, and in all places. However, according to the global variation in how the modes of nature manifest at different places, some details of the application of varṇāśrama may vary. Narada Muni explains this as follows:

**"My dear King, brahmanas well conversant in Vedic knowledge have given their verdict that in every age \[yuga\] the conduct of different sections of people according to their material modes of nature is auspicious both in this life and after death."**

To better appreciate the excellence of varṇāśrama and its role in raising devotees of different natures to perfection, it is helpful to understand how the modes of nature shape conditioned souls, resulting in their different qualities (guna) and activities (karma).

The original, pure nature of a living entity is called his svabhava. When a living entity turns away from Krsna with a desire for independent enjoyment, and comes under the sway of the material energy, he becomes covered and his svabhava becomes conditioned by the modes of nature. Baladeva Vidyabhusana says,

**"The primordial material desire that is active since time immemorial is called one's nature, svabhava."**

To facilitate his desires, the living entity is awarded a material body. Identifying with it he then acts in ways that are contrary to his constitutional nature. In other words he serves himself, instead of Krsna. Krsna confirms that activity, karma, is born of svabhava:

*svabhava-jena kaunteya*  
*nibaddhah svena karmana*  
*kartum necchasi yan mohat*  
*karisyasy avaso 'pi tat*

**"Under illusion you are now declining to act according to My direction. But, compelled by the work born of your own nature, you will act all the same, O son of Kunti."**

And although initially all conditioned souls share one desire, which is to enjoy independent of Krsna, because their unique spiritual nature is reflected in their material nature how living entities want to enjoy Krsna's creation becomes manifold. Thus, although material desire is one, it manifests in unlimited ways, resulting in unlimited kinds of action. This is expressed by the words, *prayah sva-bhava-vihito nrnam dharmo*, "The conduct of different sections of people varies according to their material modes of nature."

Srila Prabhupada's translation above of svabhava further implies that while people's nature and work are varied due to the countless permutations of the modes, in terms of social structure they are categorised according to goodness, passion, mixed passion, and ignorance.

It should be noted that the unique variety in living entities' svabhava results in a unique variety of bodies, each with its unique bodily features. Krsna explains,

*srotram caksuh sparsanam ca*  
*rasanam ghranam eva ca*  
*adhisthaya manas cayam*  
*visayan upasevate*

**"The living entity, thus taking another gross body, obtains a certain type of ear, eye, tongue, nose and sense of touch, which are grouped about the mind. He thus enjoys a particular set of sense objects."**

Because of the enjoyment afforded by a set of sense objects the living entity becomes "captivated" in his material body.And although he is transcendental and spiritual, in his bewildered condition the living entity thinks that he is a temporary lump of flesh that is sometimes an animal, sometimes a man, and sometimes a goddess. As a result the living entity acts in different ways according to the bodily instrument in which he resides, and with which he strives to satisfy his endless desires. These activities are of two kinds, attachment and repulsion, which are both driven by the two misconceptions of "I" and "mine." "I" relates to the body that the living entity inhabits, and "mine" to the field of influence he has accumulated due to his activities, karma.

And every activity further entangles him in the modes of nature and conditions him to a materialistic outlook. How this conditioning takes place is described by the Lord:

**"Material nature consists of three modes—goodness, passion and ignorance. When the eternal living entity comes in contact with nature, O mighty-armed Arjuna, he becomes conditioned by these modes."**

One may wonder how the living entity is connected to the modes of nature. The answer lies in the living entity's identification with the material body, which in turn is attached through a subtle control mechanism to the modes of material nature. This control mechanism is similar to how a puppeteer moves a puppet with invisible wires, the difference being that the wires of the modes are the attachment of the living entity. The modes of nature act according to the living entity's karma and svabhava, and when they do, they influence his sense organs—like his eyes and ears—subjecting his mind to experiencing all kinds of happiness or distress. Krsna succinctly explains this connection in the Gita in this way:

**"Nature is said to be the cause of all material causes and effects, whereas the living entity is the cause of the various sufferings and enjoyments in this world. The living entity in material nature thus follows the ways of life, enjoying the three modes of nature. This is due to his association with that material nature. Thus he meets with good and evil among various species."**

Thus the living entity is trapped in material existence, and from just one primordial desire he becomes entangled in a hopeless, labyrinthine network of action and reaction, and increasing and diverse desires.

When a living entity recognises his state of suffering, he tries to free himself, but the fetters of bondage and the attachments in the heart cannot be destroyed by any form of pious activity, mystic yoga, or study of scripture. Only devotional service beginning with the association with pure devotees can liberate one. Sanat-kumara says,

*yat-pada-pankaja-palasa-vilasa-bhaktya*  
*karmasayam grathitam udgrathayanti santah*  
*tadvan na rikta-matayo yatayo 'pi ruddha-*  
*sroto-ganas tam aranam bhaja vasudevam*

**"The devotees, who are always engaged in the service of the toes of the lotus feet of the Lord, can very easily over- come hard-knotted desires for fruitive activities. Because this is very difficult, the nondevotees—the jnanis and yogis—although trying to stop the waves of sense gratification, cannot do so. Therefore you are advised to engage in the devotional service of Krsna, the son of Vasudeva."**

And so the purpose of varṇāśrama is to somehow give the opportunity for all living entities to associate with devotees and to then engage in devotional service—even if unknowingly at first.

As already mentioned, living entities are distinguished by the qualities, guna, manifest of their material natures, svabhava, in accordance with the material modes in four main categories. These categories represent four social divisions: brahmanas, ksatriyas, vaisyas, and sudras. The daiva-varṇāśrama system strives to raise people in the last three divisions to the mode of goodness by regulated conduct and all four divisions to pure devotional service by association with Vaisnavas. By the power of pure devotion these souls are released from the reaction to acts performed in previous lives and in their current life. In this way living entities become again qualified for the eternal life, which is their birthright.

Having come to Krsna consciousness, devotees have attained, in Yudhisthira Maharaja's words, "the ultimate goal of life—devotional service."And yet the perfection of devotional service, loving devotion, is still to be reached by strong sadhana and by utilising the modes of nature to their advantage. Krsna described to Uddhava how this latter process takes place:

**"The three modes of material nature, namely goodness, passion and ignorance, pertain to material intelligence and not to the spirit soul. By development of material goodness one can conquer the modes of passion and ignorance, and by cultivation of transcendental goodness one may free oneself even from material goodness."**

To facilitate their practice of devotional service, devotees who are still conditioned by the modes of nature must also follow Krsna's advice and embrace the regulations of Varṇāśrama. This combination, yuta, of bhakti and varṇāśrama, quickly awards devotees the perfection of life.

For how long should one continue following varṇāśrama?

Krsna says,

*tavat karmani kurvita*  
*na nirvidyeta yavata*  
*mat-katha-sravanadau va*  
*sraddha yavan na jayate*

**"One should continue to perform the Vedic ritualistic activities until one actually becomes detached from material sense gratification and develops faith for hearing and chanting about Me."**

The answer to the question is that devotees who experience a preponderance of material desires and lack of steadiness in devotional activities should strictly follow the Varṇāśrama system. The above verse denotes the devotional stage of steadiness, nistha. Devotees who have surpassed that stage will also find the practice of Varṇāśrama advantageous, and of course, their adherence to social norms is important for both a cohesive society and to inspire those less advanced.

Therefore devotees should act according to their svabhava, but in ways that will elevate, not degrade them.

**Elevation by Cultivating Goodness**

According to their conditioning by the modes of nature, devotees have different religious duties, karma. The personal quality, guna, that indicates a devotee's svabhava, identifies a corresponding duty. Although in Kali-yuga the overpowering influence of ignorance complicates devotees' identifying their actual qualities, and hence their varnas, the propensities for a certain lifestyle and profession are an indication of it. However, living under the influence of today's egalitarian environment and classless society, in which everyone thinks, "I can be what I want to be," necessitates that devotees identify their varnas with the help of objective companions. More will be said about this in later chapters.

The process of cultivating the mode of goodness in tandem with devotional practices is based on devotees' associating with things that are already in goodness. The more strongly devotees are situated in goodness, the more spontaneous becomes the following of religious principles and hence devotional service. Thus the influence of passion and ignorance that have long encumbered devotees' natures are destroyed, as is the inclination for sense gratification and irreligious conduct.

Krsna mentions ten items as being primarily influential in invoking the presence of a mode of nature. When these ten are of the nature of goodness, then devotees can be assured of residence in that mode. The ten items are- scriptures, water, association with one's children or with people in general, place, time, activities, birth, meditation, mantras, and purificatory rituals.

Of these ten, scriptures, meditation and mantras are spiritual activities, association with people, activities birth and purificatory rituals are religious acts, and water, place, and time, are relevant material considerations.We shall elaborate on these briefly, keeping in mind that when these ten items in the mode of goodness are prescribed, the implication is that the same items in lower modes should be shunned.

**Scriptures**

Some scriptures petition people in different modes of nature and present that part of the Absolute Truth acceptable to those people. Thus while such texts may, for example, regulate people in the mode of ignorance, they may not elevate them to goodness, what to speak of transcendence. Scriptures like Srimad-Bhagavatam, Bhagavad-gita and the books Srila Prabhupada has written, reveal the Absolute Truth in its entirety, and, being transcendental, their study and application brings one to goodness and from there to transcendence.

**Meditation**

Like scriptures, there are different deities visnu-tattvas, demigods or demons—that people meditate on and pray to as their worshipable Lords for different purposes and in different ways. Following the prescription of the Bhagavatam, devotees meditate on Krsna to attain His loving service through pure, unmotivated devotion. Such meditation favours the mode of goodness.

**Mantras**

Of all mantras the Hare Krsna maha-mantra, the Brahma-gayatri, and our pancaratrika-mantras are the best. Chanting them with pure intent gives devotees access to the highest perfection.

**Association with family and others**

While one cannot be selective about association with family members, one can be selective how one associates with them. The quality of association that invokes goodness is described by Narada: "Thus one should gradually become detached from affection for his wife and children, exactly like a man awakening from a dream."Association to fulfil business and social responsibilities should also be done in the mood of detached duty. These kinds of association are made possible by regular association with advanced Vaisnavas.

**Activities**

Activities refer to one's duties in one's varna and asrama, which are to be performed for spiritual perfection and not for sense gratification. The Lord adds that duties should be done "... without false ego, with great determination and enthusiasm, and without wavering in success or failure."Activities thus undertaken are in the mode of goodness.

**Birth**

Srila Prabhupada often comments on the importance of the purificatory rite of garbhadhana-samskara in determining the nature of offspring. Obviously one cannot determine one's own birth, but the approach to the birth of one's offspring certainly determines the consciousness of the conceiver and the consciousness of the children. The combination of these two very much determines the atmosphere in which a family will live. For this reason birth is emphasised amongst other rites as being especially important.

**Purificatory Rituals**

The Vedas are replete with many material and spiritual rites meant for regulating and purifying conditioned souls. Of these Sanatana Gosvami has selected those suitable for pure devotees and presented them in his Śrī Hari-bhakti-vilasa. Following those rituals elevates and keeps one situated in goodness. The clothing one wears is an example of a material activity that is conducive to Krsna consciousness. Western clothes are designed for sense gratification, notably to invoke passion. Wearing them does not have the same favourable psychological effect as wearing loose fitting dhotis and saris.

**Water**

This item, along with place and time, like all other things, is situated in different modes of nature. Vaisnavas should select that water, place, and time that are under the domain of goodness.

Pure water refers to flowing, uncontaminated water, like a river, and preferably a sacred river. This was one reason why civilisations congregated around pure bodies of water. Bathing with cold water and bathing thrice daily are also activities that invoke goodness, indeed all activities in Vedic culture are inaugurated by touching water and perhaps by even bathing. Thus the quality of water is essential for physical, mental, and spiritual well-being.

**Place**

The best place to reside is Vrndavana or Mayapur. However, devotees who cannot do so may transform their homes into a dhama by installing Deities and living there as befitting a holy place. One reason that Srila Prabhupada established rural communities was that country life is in the mode of goodness, while city life is passion. Therefore devotees who live and work in cities must compensate for their passionate environment by spiritualising their environment. That will be conducive to Krsna consciousness.

**Time**

Time is also a vehicle of the modes of nature, and doing the appropriate thing at the recommended time yields a favourable result. Diti and Kasyapa conceived at an inauspicious time and the result were twin demons. Rising early, before the auspicious brahma-muhurta, and performing sadhana is easier and more productive than at other times of the day. Similarly, devotees would do well to research when the mode of goodness favours other activities like eating, performing ceremonies, undertaking some work, and so on.

In summary, devotees should be careful to live under circumstances protected by the mode of goodness and cultivate those things in that mode. In this way their knowledge and sense of renunciation will increase, and their life will be in harmony with devotional principles.

It is the Lord's instruction that devotees who still identify with the body and the mind must embrace a life favouring goodness.By so doing devotees become desireless and attain unflinching devotional service to Krsna.

**The Cessation of Karma**

Although this book cannot address every question that its readers may raise, there is one important question that we shall now answer. We believe this answer will shed further light on the need for devotees to follow a life guided by the principles of varṇāśrama.

Our Krsna conscious upbringing has led us to understand that by surrendering to Krsna, we become free of karma. And this is Krsna's final declaration on the subject:

*sarva-dharman parityajya*  
*mam ekam saranam vraja*  
*aham tvam sarva-papebhyo*  
*moksayisyami ma sucah*

**"Abandon all varieties of religion and just surrender unto Me. I shall deliver you from all sinful reactions. Do not fear."**

That being the case, devotees will ask, "If Krsna has freed us of our karma, why do we have to act according to karmic principles dictated by svabhava, guna, and karma as prescribed by varṇāśrama?"

One answer to this question has already been given, that complete freedom from sinful reaction comes when one is chanting Krsna's names without offense. This stage of surrender takes place at a developed stage of Krsna consciousness—like nistha or beyond. Devotees who have not yet attained that stage of advancement must still manage the conditioning of their past deeds in a way that assists their devotional life.

The second answer is the one on which we shall now focus. The consequence of this answer reveals that devotees even beyond the stage of nistha need to take their past karma into consideration. Indeed, even perfected souls in this world do the same. Let us see how and why.

Scriptures are replete with statements confirming the power of transcendental knowledge, devotional service, and the holy name in destroying the effect of pious or sinful acts.

That eradication includes even karma that is fructifying at present, prarabdha-karma.Sukadeva Gosvami says, natah param karma-nibandha-krntanam..., "One who desires freedom from material bondage should chant the names of Lord Krsna."And Srila Prabhupada writes, "If one understands the purpose of Krsna's coming to this earth and performing His activities, one is immediately liberated."

So yes, devotees are liberated from the results of past acts. And yet they appear not to be.

Why do we say that? Because even after chanting hare krsna offenselessly they still have their body, and are still alive.

The body devotees currently inhabit is their prarabdha-karma. It is the presently unfolding result of past acts. It's continuing existence is a testament to the continuing existence of the effect of past karma. And in addition to the gross body, the subtle body, propensities, and abilities also remain apparently unchanged.

Now, according to the statements of scripture cited earlier, if one chanted Krsna's name a dramatic result would be for the chanter to immediately die. He would give up his body, the visible testament to his past karma. But we don't see that. Therefore, there must be some other principle at work that continues to sustain some aspect, a visible aspect of devotees karma That is why a chanter continues to live This principle is Krsna's personal intervention He sustains devotees' material bodies so that thay can continue to advance and ultimately attain love for Him.Krsna attests to His intervention in His devotees' lives in this famous verse:

*sri-bhagavan uvaca*  
*yasyaham anugrhnami*  
*harisye tad-dhanam sanaih*  
*tato 'dhanam tyajanty asya*  
*svajana duhkha-duhkhitam*

**"The Personality of Godhead said: If I especially favour someone, I gradually deprive him of his wealth. Then the relatives and friends of such a poverty-stricken man abandon him. In this way he suffers one distress after another."**

In commenting on Vedanta verses that convey the same message, Baladeva Vidyabhusana confirms that the karma of devotees—attested to by the presence of their material bodies—remains by Krsna's divine will. The acarya says that the Lord does this so that devotees can preach. And he calls the karma Krsna sustains "a shadow" of real karma, something like the charred remains of a burnt piece of cloth. He concludes by saying,

**"In this way, the statement that prarabdha-karmas must be experienced by liberated souls is fulfilled."**

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura also likens devotees' karma to a burnt cloth. He says that events related to the body—sickness, sufferings, and even the tendency to sin—are Krsna's mercy, not the reactions to a devotee's past. But they continue to remain as long as the Lord desires. In this way devotees have the opportunity to perfect their Krsna consciousness and to preach.

Cakravarti Thakura also says that even for perfected devotees, for devotees at the platform of prema, the "apparent" or the "impressions of" past karma still remain by Krsna's will. This is seen in the sufferings of Yudhisthira Maharaja and his sinless brothers.

In summary, while Krsna frees devotees of their karma, He also keeps a token of that karma, managing it for the upliftment of His devotees who appear to ordinary people to be living under universal laws of karma.

Thus in answer to the question "Why devotees freed of karma should follow Varṇāśrama, an institution to regulate karma?", we reply in short, "Because karma remains."

Because Krsna maintains devotees' karma, we can understand that devotees' svabhava and guna, and its corresponding propensities for work, also remain. Therefore, devotees must organise their lives according to the Varṇāśrama social system that Krsna set up. That will help those devotees who are still conditioned to advance and it will set a proper example for others still distant from devotional service.

To conclude on this topic, devotees may consider that there are two kinds of activities in this world—material and spiritual. Varṇāśrama is material and bhakti is spiritual.

While devotional service spiritually qualifies us for so many kinds of work we do not become automatically materially capable to do that work.

For example, a Vaisnava is said to be qualified as a brahmana. That is certainly the case. However, that qualification does not exempt him from the requisite brahminical training all brahmanas must undertake in order to be eligible for deity worship, such as cooking. And even after training it may be seen that a devotee is not a good cook because it is not his propensity. So eligibility does not equate with capability.

Similarly, although devotees have become eligible for liberation, because the conditioning and limitations of the body and mind remain, devotees must act according to them so that they actually become capable of being liberated. In other words, the qualities symptomatic of devotees' svabhava, which is formed by their apparent prarabdha-karma, dictates the varna and asrama for which they are suited for. By practising Krsna consciousness while executing their prescribed duties, devotees will attain that for which they are qualified—transcendence in Krsna's service.

## Chapter Twenty-One. Work as the Basis of Varna

By nature the soul is active, and so every living entity is inclined to some kind of work. As conditioned souls we act according to our svabhava, constrained as it is by the modes of nature. Krsna implies that the interaction of the living entities and the modes of nature is akin to that of puppets being manipulated by a puppet master:

*na hi kascit ksanam api*  
*jatu tisthaty akarma-krt*  
*karyate hy avasah karma*  
*sarvah prakrti-jair gunaih*

**"Everyone is forced to act helplessly according to the qualities he has acquired from the modes of material nature; therefore no one can refrain from doing something, not even for a moment."**

Vaisnava philosophy clarifies that being forced to act helplessly does not mean that human beings have no free will. While certain aspects of life, like the physical body and one's nature, are unchangeable, how one engages his body and his nature is a matter of choice. It is for that reason that Krsna says, "Nature is said to be the cause of all material causes and effects, whereas the living entity is the cause of the various sufferings and enjoyments in this world."Scripture also confirms that the responsibility for pious and sinful acts weighs upon the performer—the living entity in a human body.

In summary, the picture of the living entity as a puppet is not completely accurate, for this puppet has some degree of freedom to act. Therefore we shall now review principles of karma, or independent action, and in that way better understand the duties relating to different orders of life.

**The Soul's Inclination to Work**

While the word karma has many meanings, we shall now use it in the context of those prescribed duties by which living entities maintain their life for the purpose of self-realisation.

There are two aspects to karma—duties and prohibited acts. Conduct that is not conducive to spiritual elevation is a prohibited act and is of two types—sinful activity, vikarma, and inactivity, akarma.

Sinful activity is any conduct that is outside one's prescribed duty. That implies a wide spectrum of vikarma with an equally broad scope of reaction, from minor to severe. However minor a reaction may be, it still burdens one's efforts for self-realisation, and therefore wise devotees make every effort not to be entangled in vikarma.

In addition to forbidden activities, there is inactivity, akarma, which is to neglect one's prescribed duty. While akarma may sometimes appear to be renunciation or even spiritual advancement—as in the case of devotees who seclude themselves—it is also sinful because it is neglect of what one is meant to do.

Clearly the only obstacle-free path in life, as in spiritual advancement, is to do one's duty. And ignorance of what constitutes karma, akarma, and vikarma is no excuse. To emphasise this Krsna repeats Himself thrice, indicating that, "... \[they—karma, akarma, and vikarma\] should be understood, should be understood, should be understood."

As mentioned in chapter six, it is the duty of every human being to know right from wrong. Ignorance of these aspects of karma is no excuse. The modes of nature award reactions for all forms of karma whether one is in knowledge of their governing principles or not. Devotees must, therefore, educate themselves about the karmic laws that govern their activities in the same way they learn the laws of the road when they purchase an automobile.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda describes three kinds of duties—constitutional, conditional, and desired.

Duties that are innate to one's physical, social, and spiritual condition and subsequently are always necessary, are constitutional. Examples of these are the maintenance of one's body and family, giving in charity, and worshipping Lord Krsna according to one's means.

Conditional conduct is dictated by circumstances. For example, one may be obliged to give up one kind of work to maintain one's family for another kind of work. Or in order to avoid some unfavourable situation, one may be obliged to relocate his family to another land.

Those activities in which personal desire, rather than one's nature or circumstances, are the impetus are called desired karma. Wanting to have a child, to purchase a larger house, or to improve one's living conditions are a few examples.

There are many considerations—such as avoiding excessive endeavour—that devotees must consider in all three kind of duties. And guidance as to what constitutes the scope of proper conduct comes from one's own wisdom and from impartial advisors. Both are required.

To recognise the duty appropriate to them, devotees should factor the following three parameters into their decision-making—their nature, dharma, and devotional service.

The governing principle of Varṇāśrama is that devotees' eligibility for duties is determined by the qualities characteristic of their nature. In absence of this consideration devotees will not be able to properly execute an activity and hence society cannot function smoothly.

While one's nature is the underlying consideration to determine devotees' prescribed work, the principles of dharma, religious codes, determine how that prescribed work should be accomplished. One cannot go about life in any old way. Work must be done according to scriptural guidelines, otherwise, as Krsna says, one will be neither successful nor happy, and above all one will not please Him.

But neither working according to one's nature, nor following the laws of dharma, can award the goal for which Varṇāśrama strives—spiritual perfection. For that, devotees' work must be inextricably connected to devotional service, which they can accomplish by the guidance of their spiritual masters. Thus, moral codes, acts of chastisement, governance, divisions of work, marriage, and atonement to name some must be seen in the light of devotional values and should be connected with those acts of devotion that further Srila Prabhupada's mission.

**The Result of Being Devotionally Dutiful**

We have repeatedly asserted that following varṇāśrama-dharma benefits practising devotees' acts of devotion. Let us now see how being devotionally dutiful is conducive.

The mechanism that varṇāśrama offers its followers is the power of the mode of goodness, which when harnessed by devotees elevates them from the lower modes of nature. The duties prescribed for Vaisnavas are those characteristic of goodness that when followed "conquers the modes of passion and ignorance."Srila Prabhupada used the term "dovetailing" all things in the mode of goodness with service to Krsna.

Following the duties Srila Prabhupada prescribed for us, beginning with rising early, is to be in the mode of goodness. Acting according to our whims for the purpose of material gain is to be in the mode of passion. And when devotees have no idea why they do something—"I don't know"—is to be in the mode of ignorance.

How do devotees handicapped by the influence of lower modes get the strength to follow the duties in the modes of goodness?

By strict spiritual practice! By following the regulative principles, chanting sixteen quality rounds a day and attending a morning program, devotees will not only be able, but will be inclined, to execute duties in the mode of goodness. If they don't follow the minimum standard Srila Prabhupada requested, then they will struggle to do the right thing.

In this way, when devotees dovetail everything—sattvika objects, activities, time, and place—with the devotional service of the Lord, they become fixed in goodness and lead a happy life. As their spiritual practices become more potent they gradually become free of the modes of goodness and rise to the transcendental platform.

In summary, the successive benefits for practising devotees following varṇāśrama are: they lose their taste for fruitive activities, they become free from the influence of ignorance and passion, they then become desireless, niskama, and ultimately they transcend the mode of goodness and come to the perfectional stage of pure devotion.

## Chapter Twenty-Two. Eligibility for the Varnas

The Vedic plan for regulating human life prescribes two kinds of religious performances: one is the path of attachment, pravrtti-marga, and the other the path of detachment, nivrtti-marga. Because daiva-varṇāśrama is aimed at encouraging devotees—whatever their degree of detachment and whatever their varna and asrama—to make service to Krsna their priority, it is the path of nivrtti-marga. Lord Kapila summarises the process in this way:

*nivrtti-dharma-nirata*  
*nirmama nirahankrtah*  
*sva-dharmaptena sattvena*  
*parisuddhena cetasa*

**"By executing one's occupational duties, acting with detachment and without a sense of proprietorship or false egoism, one is posted in one's constitutional position by dint of complete purification of consciousness, and by thus executing so-called material duties he can easily enter into the kingdom of God."**

As mentioned already, with every religious performance or every human act, there must be a corresponding qualification. And so it is with varṇāśrama. Every social and spiritual division has a corresponding qualification and one's propensity to act in those respective divisions is one's qualification. Propensity is determined by nature and nature is recognised by one's qualities. In this way our qualities, gunas, are the qualification, adhikara, for our varna.

This Part Three of the book is meant to help devotees identify their personal qualities and consequently the varna for which their nature qualifies them. Of course the power of pure devotion is such that devotees can do the work of any varna for Krsna's service. However, in the scheme of Srila Prabhupada's desire to establish Varṇāśrama, devotees will generally serve and work in a way that corresponds to their propensities.

**The Infectious Effect of the Modes of Nature**

The connection of living entities with the material world dates back to a time immemorial. The creation is supervised and pervaded by the three modes of nature, goodness, passion, and ignorance, and by the inevitable contact with the modes, living entities adopt the qualities of those modes.

The connection with the modes of nature has an infectious effect. One takes on the mode of nature to which one is inclined, and reflects the qualities of that mode. Krsna says,

**"When the eternal living entity comes in contact with nature, O mighty-armed Arjuna, he becomes conditioned by these modes."**

Because human beings have a certain amount of free will, their contact with the modes of nature is related to their activities karma. Thus their activities determine the qualities that characterise living entities. The interconnection between acts and qualities means that actions both determine and can change the mode of nature that govern us. The elevation of humans by those regulated activities that systematically raises them to higher modes of nature is the active principle of varṇāśrama.

Eventually varṇāśrama raises living entities to the mode of goodness where they are highly motivated to practise pure devotion, which then raises them to the stage of transcendence. Because different kinds of work correspond to different modes of nature, by accepting the work for which one is qualified, followers are systematically elevated through different social categories by the varṇāśrama system. In Vedic times this process of, for example, raising a sudra to a brahmana, would take multiple lives. However, by the combined grace of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, Krsna's holy names, and devotional service, elevation to perfection can take place in one life. Thus all devotees can rise to a brahminical standard of devotion.

But we cannot establish varṇāśrama if we are all working as brahmanas. To establish a social structure that follows the four-fold divisions of work, devotees will generally act in the varna for which they have corresponding qualities.

This chapter is meant to help devotees see how their propensities relate to the modes of nature, what the characteristic qualifications for the varnas are, and how by cultivating the twenty-one qualities they can more clearly understand what varna they are naturally suited for. Only when we are clear about our present conditioned nature can we, with clarity say, "this is my varna."

**Qualities of the Modes and Their Influence**

The qualities of the modes of nature are inherent in their very name and are self explanatory—goodness, passion, and ignorance. But the influence that each mode exerts on the living entity manifests multifarious attributes. Therefore scriptural descriptions of the modes are about the effect that they have on conditioned souls and about the subsequent conduct that souls exhibit.

Analysing the qualities of the wind, for example, is helpful in understanding why the qualities of the modes are generally described by their effects:

**The wind is defined as the movement of air. Not much more can be said about it. But when we examine the effects of the wind, which includes rustling of leaves, caressing the body, carrying the aroma of flowers, and toppling trees, we get a much better picture of the wind. So it is with the modes of nature. They are better understood when their effects are studied.**

Krsna comprehensively summarises the effects of the modes in the following way:

Goodness is characterised by mind and sense control, tolerance, discrimination, being dutiful, truthfulness, mercy, considering past and future events, satisfaction, generosity, renunciation, faith, being embarrassed at improper action, charity, simplicity, humility, and self-satisfaction.

The mode of passion is characterised by relentless desire, great endeavour, audacity, dissatisfaction, pride, praying for material advancement, a sense of superiority, sense gratification, quarrelsomeness, desire for praise, ridicule of others, boastfulness, and justifying one's actions by one's strength.

Finally the major effects of ignorance are intolerant anger, stinginess, speaking without scriptural authority violent hatred, living as a parasite, hypocrisy, fatigue, quarrel, lamentation, delusion, unhappiness, depression, sleeping too much, false expectations, fear, and laziness.

The Lord also summarises the effect of the modes in the Gita:

**"O son of Bharata, the mode of goodness conditions one to happiness; passion conditions one to fruitive action; and ignorance, covering one's knowledge, binds one to madness."**

While the modes of nature inspire unique characteristics, there are two ways in which the modes interact that results in the living entities' possessing qualities that are characteristic of more than one mode. These two interactions are: competition among the modes for dominance, and the many and varied ways in which they combine. The effect on human beings is to be seen in their varied natures, appearance, and behaviour.

Although unique in many ways among other species, human beings tend to be characterised by their common quest for religiosity, economic development, sense gratification, and liberation, which result in corresponding faith, wealth, sensual enjoyment, and liberation from material suffering.

As already explained, human beings are formed not only by the actions of the modes of nature, but by their own chosen conduct, karma. For devotees, the quality of their spiritual practices and occupational duties invokes a certain mode or combination of modes.

Those who are self-controlled clear thinkers will invoke the mode of goodness, those who give in to lust and daydreaming invoke passion, and those who are frustrated and angry invoke ignorance.

Let us further study the different attributes that the modes invoke in devotees who choose to conduct their lives in different ways. Such a study will reveal to devotees the results that correspond to how they choose to serve, work, and live.

**Goodness**

Devotees are said to be in goodness when they worship Krsna and offer the results of their work to Him in order to become free of past and present karma. One should not mistakenly think that only men—and not women—can be situated in goodness. Interestingly, Krsna emphasises the equality of both genders saying *purusam striyam eva va*, "Any person, whether man or woman..." The unmotivated and detached performance of duties that is both steady and free of violence invokes the mode of goodness, whomever the performer.

A devotee situated in goodness, which is luminous, pure, and auspicious, can subdue the influences of passion and ignorance as a consequence of which he or she becomes happy, virtuous, and knowledgeable. These qualities free the devotee from the fear of bodily suffering and mental agitation, and make his consciousness clear and his senses controlled. Thus endowed spiritually, devotees effectively purify their hearts and quickly realise their relationship with Krsna.

**Passion**

The motive underlying passion is to desire personal benefit. When devotees thus choose to worship Krsna by their prescribed duties, they are situated in the mode of passion Such Vaisnavas worship the deity forms of the Lord to enhance their material enjoyment, fame, and wealth. The hard work required to achieve these ends is accompanied by anxiety and struggle, and results in an increase in material attachment and in a separatist mentality.

The word "separatist" means that a devotee's interest is different than Krsna's. The devotee wants to enjoy materially, Krsna wants the devotee to cease material enjoyment. The further result of being a separatist is to distance oneself from devotees practising detachment, which then gives rise to a partisan mentality amongst devotees in different modes. To rationalise a separatist conduct requires devotees to distort their intelligence which then results in irreligious activity. Thus devotees become further entangled in material objects and activities, their bodily health wanes, and they become mentally perplexed.

**Ignorance**

It is hard to conceive of devotees wanting to worship Krsna in order to commit violence to others—what to speak of to devotees. Yet this is the intention of one in the mode of ignorance. Unfortunately ISKCON's history does continue to witness such brutality by devotees. Aggression is not only physical, but also emotional, psychological, and sexual. It manifests when ignorance conquers the higher modes, clouds devotees' consciousness, and causes them to become foolish and dull. These devotees are short tempered and constantly under the dominion of lamentation, illusion, and daydreaming. In today's world, their mental instability is known as depression.

In addition to violence, Lord Kapila also mentions envy, pride, and anger as the quality of devotees in the mode of ignorance.Like those in the mode of passion, devotees in ignorance are also separatists, but to the extreme. Needless to say, worship in ignorance fails to capture Krsna's heart and is simply a formula for rebirth in the material world. It is a quality sincere devotees want to avoid or be quickly elevated from.

**The Four Natures Caused by the Modes**

Although the modes are three in number, because of the unique qualities that manifest when passion and ignorance combine, the three modes form four different natures.

When a devotee's mind, intelligence, and senses are situated in goodness, their lives are akin to a life of truth, and their nature is to seek out and take pleasure in knowledge and austerity.

When devotees are steadfast in their duties but are driven by ulterior motives and the desire for recognition, their natures are characteristic of the passionate quest for sense gratification.

The moral downward spiral caused by sense gratification invokes qualities of ignorance like greed, dissatisfaction, false pride, hypocrisy, and envy. The result is the mixed mode of passion and ignorance, which is characterised by narcissistic selfishness.

Finally, when human degradation reaches its nadir, cheating, lying, sloth, sleepiness, violence, depression, lamentation, bewilderment, fear, and poverty reign. Thus devoid of good qualities, human beings exhibit the quality of madness. The state of the world's population in the age of Kali is characterised by ignorance. Generally, it is this inherited conditioning and environment from which devotees strive to raise themselves.

**The Four Occupational Duties**

In Satya-yuga, human beings were all situated in the mode of goodness and did not require a strict social structure to maintain harmony among each other for the purpose of attaining life's goal. With the mode of passion assuming prominence in Treta-yuga, and self-interest a characteristic of most humans, the Lord saw a need for providing humans with a social structure. Thus from the universal form were manifested the four social orders: the brahmanas from the Lord's face, the ksatriyas from His arms, the vaisyas from the thighs, and the sudras from His legs. And each social division was distinguished from the other by its unique duties and behaviour.

In order to inspire them with their respective codes of conduct, the four varnas were placed under the jurisdiction of different modes of nature.

The natural teachers of society, the brahmanas, are in the mode of goodness and thus inclined to Vedic knowledge and averse to sense gratification. The protectors of society are the ksatriyas, their heroism and chivalry being characteristic of the mode of passion. Inspired by passion to provide grains and commodities for society, and by ignorance to perform manual labour, the vaisyas have the distinction of being under the combination of these two modes. To complete and perfect the social order by which man can please the Lord, the mode of ignorance inspired sudras to serve the higher three orders.

Thus the four social divisions, their occupational duties, living conditions, and distinctive qualities came into being from the Supreme Lord and His agents, the modes of material nature.

From this we can see that the eligibility for a specific role in society is the qualities of the corresponding mode of nature. If people are placed in a social role without considering their qualification, then they will not be able to properly fulfil their duties; moreover, the framework of society will be corrupted. Therefore a person's occupational duties are determined after considering their nature. This is also the Lord's conclusion:

*brahmana-ksatriya-visam*  
*sudranam ca parantapa*  
*karmani pravibhaktani*  
*svabhava-prabhavair gunaih*

**"Brahmanas, ksatriyas, vaisyas and sudras are distinguished by the qualities born of their own natures in accordance with the material modes, O chastiser of the enemy."**

After introducing the four varnas, Krsna goes on to describe the qualities of each. The brahmanas are peaceful, self-controlled, austere, pure, tolerant, honest, knowledgeable, wise, and religious. The ksatriyas are heroic, powerful, determined, resourceful, courageous, generous, and natural leaders. Vaisyas seek prosperity while sudras are inclined to serve.

And while social considerations may set these roles as higher and lower, they are equal in their qualification for devotional service, and its perfection.

Therefore devotees who adopt a varna according to their nature and continue to worship Krsna according to the process given to us by Srila Prabhupada, quickly attain the perfection of going back to Godhead. Krsna concludes His instructions on varṇāśrama to Uddhava with that same assurance:

**"Thus, one who has purified his existence by execution of his prescribed duties, who fully understands My supreme position and who is endowed with scriptural and realised knowledge, very soon achieves Me. Those who are followers of this varṇāśrama system accept religious principles according to authorised traditions of proper conduct. When such varṇāśrama duties are dedicated to Me in loving service, they award the supreme perfection of life."**

## Chapter Twenty-Three. The Qualities of Sudras and Vaisyas

The history of the world bears witness to the decline and demise of varṇāśrama-dharma. With the onset of Kali-yuga and the appearance of non-Vedic religions and philosophies, the values underpinning varṇāśrama-dharma quickly eroded and the population of the world, as a class, were reduced to sudras or lower. The personified earth says,

*tasmin lubdha duracara*  
*nirdayah suska-vairinah*  
*durbhaga bhuri-tarsas ca*  
*sudra-dasottarah prajah*

**"In the Kali age people tend to be greedy, ill-behaved and merciless, and they fight one another without good reason. Unfortunate and obsessed with material desires, the people of Kali-yuga are almost all sudras and barbarians."**

Describing Kali-yuga, the Bhagavatam says elsewhere, "...all the social classes will be reduced to the lowest level of sudras."

Over the course of the last five thousand years the lower classes have systematically assumed the duties of the higher, purificatory rites have been neglected, brahminical culture and cow protection forgotten, and worst of all—devotional service to Krsna as the means of perfecting human life rejected for sub-religious and irreligious beliefs.

This unfortunate condition of society in which people are lower than sudras and completely ignorant of the goal of life is a state of emergency. And the challenge facing the Krsna consciousness movement is how to remedy this emergency situation.

In Srimad-Bhagavatam, both Narada Muni and Lord Krsna speak of times of emergency as occasions when lower classes can adopt the duties of the higher. Srila Prabhupada refers to such scriptural support for the mission of the Krsna consciousness movement. His Divine Grace writes "... there is now an emergency, and therefore it is now time to train those who are considered lower and condemned, so that they may become brahmanas and take up the work of spiritual progress."

In other words, we were all from sudra backgrounds, but because the situation of the world was so dire, Srila Prabhupada had to train us as brahmanas in order to engage us for the purpose of re-establishing a sane social order and for serving Lord Caitanya's mission.

And train us he did. Srila Prabhupada trained his followers as brahmanas who understood the purpose of scriptures, could worship deities, and who taught the world about the goal of life. And as his followers became learned and experienced, Srila Prabhupada asked them to introduce the other social orders, "Now you can do something." Srila Prabhupada continued, "In order to serve the mass of people, to bring them to the ideal position, we should try to introduce this varṇāśrama."

Additional aspects of varṇāśrama were already in place in ISKCON. The asramas and their basic duties had been introduced from the beginning. Then, to facilitate devotees' practice of Krsna consciousness and to showcase the ideal society to the world, Srila Prabhupada requested his followers to adopt the duties of the four social orders, and to do so in consideration of their own natures.

We have earlier explained how a person's conditioned nature is expressed by characteristic traits or qualities, and when these qualities are unclouded, they disclose a person's inclination for a certain kind of work. According to the workings of the modes of nature, work is classified into four divisions called varnas, which are characterised by corresponding responsibilities. Thus a person's manifest qualities makes him eligible for a certain varna.

This chapter and the one following are devoted to the qualities that characterise the four social orders.

Although scriptures extol the brahmana as the head of society and more is written about them in scriptures than about the other orders, we describe the sudras first, because they are the bedrock of the varṇāśrama society ISKCON is constructing.

**Sudras**

We call sudras the bedrock for two reasons: the first is that the qualities of sudras are what most of us are endowed with. The second is because sudras are the workforce, without which the other three orders can hardly function. Additionally, by the testament of Srimad-Bhagavatam cited earlier, sudras are the majority of the population.

Readers will note that the qualities that Srimad-Bhagavatam attributes to Vaisnavas in a sudra occupation are exalted by anyone's measure today. Seen in that perspective the kalau sudra-sambhavah declaration sounds more like praise than condemnation. While it is sometimes mentioned that the labourer class have no "specific qualifications," this obviously does not refer to the daiva-varṇāśrama system of Vaisnavas and not to the sudras who cultivate the thirty human qualities."No specific qualification" refers to a low grade of sudra.

Since sudras are meant to serve the other three orders of society, their primary quality is an attitude of service. Service, like sudras themselves, appears from the universal form of the Lord who is pleased when sudras embrace service as their very life and religious duty.

Astute readers will no doubt note the advantage that working as a sudra offers: the cultivation of pure devotional service. Krsna consciousness is about becoming a humble, menial servant of the Lord. When one's social duties are completely based on service they are in harmony with cultivating a devotional service attitude. By contrast, other varnas have a degree of lordship that may handicap the development of the service mood required for bhakti. Srila Prabhupada confirms, "It is actually better to be a sudra than to be a brahmana and not develop the service attitude, because that attitude alone satisfies the Lord."

The second important qualification of a sudra is the very foundation of the service attitude—satisfaction. A servant must be satisfied with his position and not covet being a master. We are in this world because we were not satisfied with being Krsna's servants. Similarly a sudra should not be envious of his or her master, for that not only causes social instability, but is unfavourable for their cultivation of pure devotion. We have already spoken in detail about satisfaction in chapter twelve, but we cannot fail to re-emphasise the importance of this outstanding quality for all varnas.

Srila Prabhupada explains that a reason for the problems in human society is that the vaisyas and sudras have excessive influence over leaders.Exerting undue influence is a sign of dissatisfaction. However, if qualified brahmanas and ksatriyas work in harmony with each other, then the subordinate classes will flourish and remain satisfied.

Śrī Krsna gives the primary qualities of servitude and satisfaction as the attribute of a pure worker:

*susrusanam dvija-gavam*  
*devanam capy amayaya*  
*tatra labdhena santosah*  
*sudra-prakrtayas tv imah*

**"Service without duplicity to the brahmanas, cows, demigods and other worshipable personalities, and complete satisfaction with whatever is obtained in such service, are the natural qualities of sudras."**

In assessing their nature, devotees may honestly consider, "Can I be successful and secure in maintaining my family without being guided by another. If the answer—unprompted by envy of others way of life — is "no" then devotees may immediately recognise that they should be serving or working for someone who is more self-sufficient. This is why in today's world most people seek employment. They are not self-sufficient.

The next quality that Srimad-Bhagavatam gives repeated emphasis to is honesty. In an age where truthfulness is fast disappearing, being honest when working for someone who enjoys greater affluence will be a challenge. But always speaking the truth, not stealing, and freedom from duplicity are qualities that constitute honesty and are also mentioned individually throughout scriptures as the qualification of a worker.

Even in today's world, if a job applicant has an earlier record of dishonesty and theft, he will fail to find employment. Even a dishonest employer will not want to hire a dishonest worker.
