Therefore honesty is a universally recognised quality for all sections of society, and it must also be abundantly present in the sudra.

To accept a social role that is often referred to as being "lower" than all others requires humility. And no one will employ a worker who is arrogant and disrespectful. So a sudra is humble, and scriptures call him sannati, one who is obedient to and offers obeisances to the higher sections of society.

A natural condition for service is to be clean. Whether a worker is cleaning or doing other kinds of work, he is working for someone for whom cleanliness is a way of life, and so he can be no less. This is especially so when a sudra serves brahmanas. Thus another quality of a worker is cleanliness.

There are two other spiritual qualities in addition to the character traits already mentioned. These are to engage in sankirtana and to feel responsible for upholding brahminical culture—meaning protecting cows and brahmanas

As regards nama-sankirtana, Narada says amantra-yajna "Participating in sacrifices without chanting mantras."The sage emphasises that sudras must be inclined to spiritual life, they must engage in sacrifices. Because only the three higher classes have eligibility—which means the background gurukula education—for chanting Vedic mantras, sudras may participate in Vedic sacrifices and hear the mantras, but not chant them.

However, the restriction of not chanting mantras does not apply to Krsna's names. Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the origin of all mantras, has said, tatrarpita niyamitah smarane na kalah, "There are no hard and fast rules for remembering and chanting Krsna's names."The conclusion is that sudras must have a desire to chant Hare Krsna congregationally, and also by japa. It is these activities in which they become spiritually equal to brahmanas and have eligibility for the perfection that brahmanas may also attain by the same means. Included in chanting Hare Krsna is the basic sadhana that all regulated devotees must sustain in order to retain and cultivate their taste for sankirtana. And that sadhana also includes being initiated with Brahma-gayatri and Gaudiya pancaratrika- mantras and worshipping the deity.

Finally, sudras must understand and be favourably disposed to brahminical culture, and the daiva-varṇāśrama society of which they are an integral part. Their knowledge of brahminical culture—the culture which raises members of society to the mode of goodness and beyond may not be detailed, but it should be substantive enough to know the significance of protecting cows, brahmanas, women, children, and elders.

Reading the eligibility for being a sudra, devotees will see that it is no small thing: service attitude, satisfaction, humility, honesty, cleanliness, commitment to sadhana and to varṇāśrama, are qualities which even readers may find lacking. However, for society to be on a sound footing these qualities must be present in workers. If they are not, then they should be acquired. And even if they are all present, they should continually be refined just as a diamond must be polished to free it of unavoidable dust.

One may ask, "Can't I be part of varṇāśrama just as I am, without cultivating the qualities that I lack?"

The answer to this question will be "no." It is like asking whether one can expect to be a citizen of Hungary without wanting to follow its laws or paying taxes. There is eligibility for everything, and requisite qualities and duties are the eligibility for being part of the Varṇāśrama system.

Krsna describes those who are unwilling or unable to acquire the qualities expected of members of society:

*asaucam anrtam steyam*  
*nastikyam suska-vigrahah*  
*kamah krodhas ca tarsas ca*  
*sa bhavo 'ntyavasayinam*

**"Dirtiness, dishonesty, thievery, faithlessness, useless quarrel, lust, anger and hankering constitute the nature of those in the lowest position outside the Varṇāśrama system."**

**Vaisyas**

Just as sudras are unique as the workforce of society vaisyas are unique as its merchants. Being born from the thighs of the Supreme Lord, vaisyas are meant to responsibly nourish society and as such conscientiously execute their duties in a way that the needs of other orders are provided for. To achieve that end, the mercantile class must be attentive to the direction of brahmanas and ksatriyas and not use their economic influence to manipulate the higher orders.

Unfortunately today's state of affairs sees the powerful mercantile class dominate the course of society by controlling politicians with powerful lobby groups and the rest of society by advertising the useless consumer items they manufacture. In this way merchants enslave society by economic sorcery, only to enrich themselves and plunge the world into a darkness wherein no one has a clue about the goal of life.

True vaisyas will not destroy society solely for personal gain. They constantly endeavour to earn money and advance the cause of economic development, but they do so while also advancing in Krsna consciousness under the guidance of a spiritual master.

Thus the primary quality of a vaisya is a responsible eagerness for making money.

Narada calls this quality *udyamo nityam naipunyam*, "always endeavouring with expertise in earning money. Here the word "expertise" refers not only to good business sense, but to a sound sustainable profession that is both materially and spiritually in line with the universal principles enunciated by Śrī Isopanisad:

*isavasyam idam sarvam*  
*yat kinca jagatyam jagat*  
*tena tyaktena bhunjitha*  
*ma grdhah kasya svid dhanam*

**"Everything animate or inanimate that is within the universe is controlled and owned by the Lord. One should therefore accept only those things necessary for himself, which are set aside as his quota, and one should not accept other things, knowing well to whom they belong."**

This approach to responsible economic development is possible by the training vaisyas receive in gurukula. Like brahmanas and ksatriyas, gurukula education is compulsory for vaisyas. As a result they are trained in—among other things—self-control which protects them from being blinded by material opulence and sense gratification.

In addition to self-control, vaisyas' eagerness for economic development is balanced by dedication to religious principles and religious sense gratification, by devotion to Krsna and guru, by striving for spiritual advancement, and by faith in Vedic civilisation.

Whatever their occupation, vaisyas follow the religious principles incumbent upon pure Vaisnavas, such as performing purificatory rites, samskaras, as well as the duties that result from such samskaras. Marriage is one such samskara and not divorcing a subsequent duty.

Similarly, because the human form of life, especially in the grhastha-asrama, entails a certain level of sense gratification, Vaisnava vaisyas are always careful not to stray into the realm of unrestricted sense enjoyment. Therefore they follow the four regulative principles and adhere to the twenty-one qualities of human beings.

Devotee vaisyas are twice-born initiates, as such they are as keen to advance spiritually as they are to make money Thus they are expert in sadhana and their deep faith in pleasing Krsna is reflected in their strict adherence to the instructions of spiritual masters, both in regards to their spiritual and their business life.

In other words, like the other orders of society, vaisuas are dedicated to Vedic civilisation, which means the spiritual principles of pure devotion and the regulations of daiva-varṇāśrama. They have full faith that living and work- ing under the guidelines of scriptures will award them the most desirable material and spiritual result.

There are two other qualities of vaisyas that deserve mention: integrity in their work and attachment to their varna.

Although a vaisya may bend the truth to sell a commodity, he has the integrity not to cheat his customer. The integrity of vaisyas is pride in the quality of the services and commodities that he provides to customers, a quality that he does not compromise. This is his honesty and truthfulness.

This integrity extends into attachment to varna in the sense that, unless there is an earth-shaking emergency, a vaisya will not sacrifice even his own given profession, what to speak of his varna.

The occupational duties of vaisyas are agriculture, trade, banking, and protection of cows. Even if a vaisya is less well-off protecting cows than he would be by doing trade, if cow-protection is his assigned occupation he will not sacrifice it. In the same way a vaisya will not accept the work of a sudra, employment, simply for material gain. He recognises that the laws of karma are infallible, and sacrificing one's prescribed duties is an illusion that brings misery in the long run.

Even more inviolable is the principle that, as with a sudra, a vaisya never accepts the duty of the higher orders. To do so would result in upheaval for his family and for society. In this regard Narada Muni says,

*jaghanyo nottamam vrttim*  
*anapadi bhajen narah*  
*rte rajanyam apatsu*  
*sarvesam api sarvasah*

**"Except in a time of emergency, lower persons should not accept the occupational duties of those who are higher. When there is such an emergency, of course, everyone but the ksatriya may accept the means of livelihood of others."**

However, once the emergency has passed, all members of society must give up their inferior occupation and resume their own duties.

In the case of a destitute vaisya, Krsna says that he may adopt the occupation of a sudra on condition that he gives up that lower engagement once his economic situation has improved.Such is the integrity required of vaisyas, and the final quality of the mercantile order described in this chapter.

A dominating characteristic of vaisyas will be obvious to the reader. They are self-sufficient. Whereas sudras find neither happiness nor stable sustenance being on their own, vaisyas would not think of being in the employ of another. Indeed vaisyas can make their fortune out of, literally, nothing. The mode of passion gives them the creativity and ability to be the independent earners that sudras are not.

Having described the qualities of Vaisnavas that makes them eligible to assume the role of either a sudra or a vaisya, this chapter concludes. The following chapter presents the natural qualities required to be either a ksatriya or brahmana.

## Chapter Twenty-Four. The Qualities of Ksatriyas and Brahmanas

The leadership of society has two aspects: the visionary and the executive. In daiva-varṇāśrama the brahmanas are the visionaries and the ksatriyas the executors. When these two work in harmony, following the eternal principles of leadership given by the Lord, then society is prosperous, but above all, it directs human beings to the goal of going back to Godhead.

This chapter describes the qualities that define ksatriyas and brahmanas, and what makes them eligible for their respective duties.

**Ksatriya**

Srila Prabhupada would often judge leaders by the standard of Yudhisthira Maharaja, whose perfect governance was reflected in the quality of life of his subjects both human and non-human. The result of dharmic leadership is narrated in this well-known verse:

*kamam vavarsa parjanyah*  
*sarva-kama-dugha mahi*  
*sisicuh sma vrajan gavah*  
*payasodhasvatir muda*

**"During the reign of Maharaja Yudhisthira, the clouds showered all the water that people needed, and the earth produced all the necessities of man in profusion. Due to its fatty milk bag and cheerful attitude, the cow used to moisten the grazing ground with milk."**

Good leadership is in harmony with Krsna's will and with the intricate laws of karma. Therefore it assures all living entities the most bountiful benefit of the earth's resources. Under the rule of a religious leader, people are free of mental and physical disturbances, and under such rule even the modes of nature manifest their best feature of goodness.

However, when Krsna departed from this world, Kali-yuga infiltrated the ruling class and over time leadership became corrupt and hence the condition of people miserable. The basic criteria of varṇāśrama is that social status is determined by corresponding personal qualities. That criteria was supplanted by the rule of "might makes right." As a result, no matter how unqualified a person is, if he can capture the throne by force or deceit, he can be king.

And while today's democratic system of voting for leaders has supplanted the process of conquering thrones, the flaw of leadership by the unqualified remains the norm. And so people continue to suffer.

What are the qualities of a leader?

A ksatriya has many unique qualities, however Krsna gives the single defining quality that summarises them all "... a fearless king, just like a father, must save all of the citizens from difficulty and also protect himself."

In other words, the prime duty of a leader is to give protection to his dependents. It should be clear that protection means not only from physical danger, but also from sinful acts and inauspiciousness. A leader, therefore, protects his dependents from their own and others' irreligious conduct, while he himself remains a model of good behaviour and pure devotion.

The many qualities of a ksatriya can be divided into four categories: spiritual, religious, leadership, and enforcement. Ksatriyas are often pictured on a horse in the thick of battle. But leading a charge is a ksatriyas last resort and just a part of his leadership duties. To be an all-round ruler a person must have qualities other than that of the enforcer of dharma.

Because ksatriyas attend gurukula and are trained along with brahmanas, just as Krsna and Balarama studied alongside Sudama Vipra, ksatriyas acquire many brahminical qualities. Dominant among spiritual qualities are exemplary devotional practices, control of the mind and senses, and dedication to brahminical culture and Vaisnavas.

Among the twenty-one religious qualities expected of humans, four are especially pronounced in leaders. They are truthfulness, tolerance, forgiveness, and generosity. A GBC member or temple president must have the faith of his followers, and that faith can only be won if a leader is seen to be religious, and being religious means being trustworthy. Leaders who are true to their word, who show tolerance in all circumstances, who know how to forgive as they do to chastise, and who are charitable with their resources, time, and selves, spontaneously garner the trust of others. These qualities command respect.

Implicit in these four attributes is another of the twenty-one, and that is knowing the difference between right and wrong. A leader is well versed in the principles of justice, dharma, and pure devotion, and knows their application according to time, place, and circumstance.

Although being spiritually and religiously inclined are essential for a leader, they do not suffice as the complete eligibility for the ksatriya-varna. A leader must know how to lead. On the other hand there are countless leadership courses on offer today that teach many of the leadership qualities, while neglecting the religious and spiritual qualities mentioned earlier. Such courses, therefore, fall short of what it takes to make a real leader, which only Krsna and His pure devotees reveal.

The leadership qualities are determination, steadiness, resourcefulness, patience, dynamism or charisma, and always being jolly.

The first four qualities describe a leader's approach to any project or activity. A ksatriya is determined in performing his duty, and he sustains his firmness of purpose until he reaches his goal. He is always alert to efficient ways of overcoming obstacles and remains patient in the conviction that by doing the right thing he will eventually get the right result. Once again, wisdom in understanding the principles of dharma is also implicit in qualities of leadership.

The next two qualities are quite unique. They can be called the magnetic qualities that attract followers: charisma and cheerfulness. Just as brahmanas exude a sense of peace, ksatriyas inspire a following. In the way that magnets naturally draw iron, the dynamic shelter that leaders exude attracts followers. And because leaders are confident that they are under the protection of dharma, they remain jolly even in the direst circumstances, inspiring confidence in their dependents. These are truly the qualities of leaders.

And when other leadership qualities fail to inspire people to follow dharma, or when rogue elements disturb the peace of religious minded citizens, the ksatriya can and will resort to the use of proportionate force.

To successfully resolve disagreement with a show of force means that ksatriyas were well trained in the use of arms and were also physically fit. These traits were well put to use by the leader's heroic spirit and fearlessness in the face of opposition. It is well known that one of the ksatriyas' codes of honour was never to turn down a challenge, even in the likely outcome of defeat. That is a leader's nature, cakirtir maranad atiricyate, "dishonour is worse than death."

Readers may wonder, "Should the Vaisnava leaders in daiva-varṇāśrama also exercise such force?"

The answer is that in a host society, aside from self-defense, devotees are not legally empowered to use force. They can, however, aggressively pursue justice through mediation or through MKTHK's own court system. Failing all in-house resolution of conflict, leaders can appeal to the court system of Hungary. A leader must see that his dependents are safeguarded and when they are not, that offenders are brought to justice in a way that justice is both done and is seen to be done.

**Brahmanas**

In the Brhad-aranyaka Upanisad it is said *tam etam vedanu- vacanena brahmana vividisanti brahmacaryena tapasa sraddhaya yajnenanasakena ca*, "By Vedic study, celibacy, austerity, faith, sacrifice and fasting, the brahmanas strive to understand the Supreme Personality of Godhead."

The quest for understanding the Absolute Truth is the overriding feature of a true brahmana. In the way that the sudra maintains himself by service to others, the vaisya dedicates his life to economic development, the ksatriya to protecting dependents, so the brahmana gives his life for searching after the Absolute Truth. The intellectual's absolute commitment to knowing the truth is embedded in the very word brahmana, which is defined as, brahma janatiti brahmanah, "He who knows the Absolute Truth is a brahmana."

The Upanisad verse mentions secondary qualities that facilitate, or are a consequence of, the pursuit for truth, qualities of which some brahmanas may have more, others less. But because Krsna is especially inclined to anyone who is trying to know Him to any degree, He is known as Brahmanya-deva.And because the goal of Vedic culture is to know Krsna, it offers the highest respect to all kinds of brahmanas by being known as brahminical culture.

Scriptures sometimes describe brahmanas who possess secondary qualities but have apparently lost interest in knowing or serving the Absolute Truth. Prajapati Daksa and Durvasa Muni are two such examples.

On the other hand, we also see brahmanas who are sold life and soul to Krsna's service and yet lack the secondary qualities. Srila Prabhupada's disciples, especially in ISKCON s formative years, are examples. Although they were not trained in basic brahminical conduct, because of their devotion to Krsna, Srila Prabhupada considered his disciples to be superior to those non-devotee brahmanas in India whose goal in life was making a living from patrimony.

But Srila Prabhupada also wanted his disciples to represent brahminical culture in detail, not just in essence. His Divine Grace repeatedly said things like, "... if one wants to become a brahmana, then he must be properly trained up," and "... if you actually have become transcendental servant of Krsna, if the qualities of brahmana is lacking, then you are not perfect. The brahminical qualification must be there."One purpose of this compendium is to provide the inspiration and the material for training those who are brahmanas in essence, but not otherwise so.

What are the attributes that a devotee should acquire to fully meet Srila Prabhupada's requirements to be a recognised brahmana?

Let us begin at the beginning by recognising what attributes human beings start with and how humans acquire the brahminical attributes. The full verse from Atri-smrti cited earlier in part says,

*janmana jayate sudrah*  
*samskarad dhi bhaved dvijah*  
*veda-pathad bhaved viprah*  
*brahma janatiti brahmanah*

**"All living beings are born equal, endowed solely with the qualities of sudras. Being subject to reformatory rites including initiation, human beings subsequently become twice-born, dvija. By thorough study and application of the Vedas, especially the Bhagavatam and the Gita, they are recognised as learned scholars, vipra. And when the learned realise that the Absolute Truth and the goal of the Vedas is Lord Krsna and serve Him, they are then celebrated as true brahmanas.”**

This verse clearly shows the stages of training that precede attaining the qualities that make a person eligible as a brahmana. The two main aspects of training are purificatory rites and education. And of course these have their subdivisions.

Before we speak of the qualities that such training requires let us consider the two kinds of candidates for brahminical training: those with previous samskaras and those without them.

In speaking to Uddhava, Lord Krsna mentions those who are already inclined to a certain varna and asrama due to having acquired the eligibility in their previous lives. He says, "The various occupational and social divisions of human society appeared according to inferior and superior natures manifest in the situation of the individual's birth."For these devotees—like the children of our devotees, or their children—, if they have cultivated brahminical qualities in their past lives, those will be manifest in them to greater or lesser degrees.

Then there are those devotees who do not have attraction to brahminical life from a previous birth and these can be divided into two: those who have no inclination to be a brahmana and those who do. While both should be trained in the thirty qualities of humans, those devotees whom senior Vaisnavas find unfit for the qualities and work of brahmanas should be trained in other, more suitable varnas. That is the principle of yasya yal laksanam proktam pumso varnabhivyanjakam, meaning that devotees should be recognised for a varna by their qualities.

According to the *janmana jayate sudrah* verse, training is the same for both classes of devotees who show an inclination towards brahmana varna.That training begins with the assumption that their qualities before training are those of sudras. In the rest of this chapter we shall therefore treat both candidates for the brahmana-varna as one category.

The following are the qualities that make one eligible to be a brahmana: self-control, austerity, cleanliness and purity, satisfaction, being forgiving, simple straightforwardness, learning, wisdom, mercy, truthfulness, peacefulness, tolerance, honesty, and religiousness.

While all these qualities are to be cultivated by every human being, and most certainly by Vaisnavas, their presence in brahmanas is manifest to the extreme.

For example ksatriyas are also forgiving, and yet brahmanas are more so, so much so that their degree of forgiveness qualifies them to become a Brahma. Jamadagni told his son Parasurama

**"My dear son, we are all brahmanas and have become worshipable for the people in general because of our quality of forgiveness. It is because of this quality that Lord Brahma, the supreme spiritual master of this universe, has achieved his post."**

From this verse it may appear that forgiveness is a brahmanaẏs most important quality. However, Srila Prabhupada gives emphasis to wisdom:

**"The main qualification of a brahmana is to be inclined to the Vedic wisdom.”**

And Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura cites the Mahabharata to substantiate that simplicity is the identifying feature of a brahmana,

**"The Mahabharata, Vana-parva, has established that simplicity is the only identification of a brahmana. \[...\] A brahmana proves his simplicity by opening his heart and speaking the truth, even if it hampers his own self-interest."**

Srila Prabhupada also tells the story of Satyakama Jabala from the Jabalopanisad (also found in Mahabharata) to emphasise truthfulness as the outstanding brahminical quality. The boy Satyakama wanted to join the asrama of Gautama Muni and when asked what his lineage was, the boy openly declared that he was the illegitimate son of his maidservant mother. Gautama replied,

**"My dear son! No one other than a brahmana can speak such truth that you have spoken. Therefore you are a brahmana, and I accept you."**

From these examples it is clear that while devotion to Krsna is the defining feature of a brahmana, because of their unparalleled eminence, any of the secondary qualities can identify a brahmana. The clarity and unmistaken distinctiveness of these attributes is expressed in these words, *tam brahmanam aham manye vrttena hi bhaved dvijah* "Indeed, the only criteria for being a brahmana is to possess pure characteristics."

Where the aforementioned qualities like self-control are present to the extreme and without adulteration, there is a brahmana. So when a devotee says, "It seems like I have the qualities of many varnas," in all likelihood he is not a brahmana.

Of the fifteen characteristics of a brahmana, five—austerity, purity, straightforwardness, peacefulness and tolerance—are unique to the varna while the other ten are present much more than in any of the other varnas both qualitatively and quantitatively.

As already mentioned, due to conditioning, the above- mentioned qualities are present to a greater or lesser degree, as a result of which there are different grades of brahmanas. For example, some are grhasthas while others are brahmacaris, vanaprasthas, or sannyasis. Then some brahmanas are attached to fruitive activities, others are attracted to austerities, others to the study of the Vedas, and still others to the practice of yoga. By Krsna's estimation, Vaisnava brahmanas are above all the others, as expressed by the words, sa me yuktatamo matah, "In my opinion devotees are the best."

Vedic literature prohibits social prejudices obstructing someone who manifests brahminical qualities, be he a sudra, from acting as a brahmana, as it does accepting someone who manifests no suitable qualities to act in the role of a brahmana.

In evidence of this Yudhisthira Maharaja was once questioned by a snake, "O Maharaja Yudhisthira! Please tell me, who is a brahmana?"

The king replied, "A person whose character displays truthfulness, charity, forgiveness, sobriety, gentleness, austerity, and lack of hatred to all living entities is called a brahmana."

The snake continued, "But sudras may also display the qualities you mentioned."

Maharaja Yudhisthira again replied, "If that is so, if qualities like austerity are seen in a sudra, he should immediately be accepted as a brahmana and if they are absent in a so-called brahmana he should be known as a sudra.

Scriptures give examples of brahmanas who were not bom in brahmana families, but their self-effulgent qualities revealed them to be great sages: Rsyasrnga was born from a deer, Kausika from kusa grass, Jambuka Rsi from a jackal, Vyasadeva was born from a fisherman's daughter, Gautama from the back of a rabbit, Vasistha from the celestial courtesan Urvasi, and Agastya was born from a pitcher.

While many examples can be given of the outstanding and unique qualities of brahmanas, to keep this book from becoming too voluminous we shall not do so. We shall, however, remind the reader that whether one is or is not born with some or all of the brahminical qualities, devotees inclined to and approved for the services of a brahmana must acquire and cultivate the corresponding qualities.

We conclude this chapter with Narada's ultimate declaration on how varna, including that of a brahmana, is determined. It is this book's constitutional statement:

*yasya yal laksanam proktam*  
*pumso varnabhivyanjakam*  
*yad anyatrapi drsyeta*  
*tat tenaiva vinirdiset*

**"If one shows the symptoms of being a brahmana, ksatriya, vaisya or sudra, as described above, even if he has appeared in a different class, he should be accepted according to those symptoms of classification."**

## Chapter Twenty-Five. Fidelity to Duty is the Basis of Social Order

To live in this world requires some form of subsistence, and subsistence is dependent on some form of work. Krsna thus declares that all living entities—bar none—must work, and their work will be determined by their natures, *na hi kascit ksanam api.* For this reason Krsna has created the varṇāśrama system so that human beings may engage in work that corresponds to their natures. That is the natural way of things and to do otherwise is unnatural. The eligibility for any kind of work is the innate ability of the worker to perform it, and that ability is a product of the worker's nature. Adhering to the kind of work for which we have an aptitude and refraining from work for which we are not qualified, is a religious principle. Krsna tells Uddhava,

*sve sve 'dhikare ya nistha*  
*sa gunah parikirtitah*  
*viparyayas tu dosah syad*  
*ubhayor esa niscayah*

**"Steadiness in one's own position is declared to be actual piety, whereas deviation from one's position is considered impiety. In this way the two are definitely ascertained."**

Earlier chapters have detailed human beings' different natures, the character traits that correspond to those natures, and the four kinds of work that suits the categories of human nature in its four divisions. The four kinds of work are the four varnas.

Just as character traits, guna, are the unique factors defining which varna one is eligible for, similarly each varna is defined by very specific, hence very unique activities, karma. These are called prescribed duties. Along with prescribed duties are prohibitions—acts that are forbidden for a specific varna. In this way both eligibility and prescribed duties define social order. This kind of natural order is sorely lacking in today's world, as a result of which we are beset with economic, political, and communal upheaval.

Our libertarian upbringing has accustomed as to do as we wish, which is contrary to the principles of varṇāśrama outlined above. These principles may at first appear restrictive. However, rather than focusing on the restrictions, readers are encouraged to focus on the end result, which is the social order and harmony that eventually elevates human beings to perfection.

A parallel may be seen, for example, in the many restrictions to driving an automobile. A driver cannot drive on any side of the road, at any speed, without stopping at red lights, and so on. There are many rules of the road. While these rules are restrictive, they also assure the driver's own safety, protect pedestrians and—most important of all—enable the diver to quickly reach his destination. Drivers will agree that once they have mastered the rules of the road, those rules become second nature and facilitate reaching the destination. It is the same way with the followers of varṇāśrama. The principles and restrictions may be daunting to the newcomer, but they become second nature to those followers who have mastered varṇāśrama life and understand well the tangible benefits thus accrued.

Because Krsna has created the varṇāśrama system and because it is meant for the elevation of conditioned souls, the duties it prescribes are accepted as religious principles. And when those prescribed duties are accomplished for Krsna's pleasure, they become devotional service and the means to attain unflinching devotion to the Lord. In this way, the execution of prescribed duties awards human beings the supreme perfection of life.

We have explained earlier how association with the three modes determines a person's nature, and how the qualities born of that nature determines his eligibility for work. The modes of nature similarly inspire people to work in different ways, meaning that even in the same varna, the quality of work is dependent on motivation: different motivations result in works and workers of different quality.

Work impelled by envy and violence by a worker who cannot distinguish between right and wrong is in the mode of ignorance. Work performed with a desire to enjoy its results by a worker blinded by selfish desire is in the mode of passion. Work performed as an offering to Krsna by a dutiful worker free of attachment is in the mode of goodness. But work done for the pleasure of Krsna by a worker fully surrendered to Him is transcendental to the modes of nature.

Daiva-varṇāśrama is for devotee-workers, whose motives are to either worship Krsna or to please Him. It is a social system meant for the sadhaka and the transcendentalist. By dutifully executing their prescribed duties these devotees purify their existence, become free of all distress and attain unflinching faith in Krsna. Living and working in that way, the pure-hearted practitioner becomes endowed with all transcendental perfection and quickly attains Krsna's full favour.

However, if devotees become slaves to their senses, especially the tongue and genitals, and wilfully neglect the prescribed duties of their varna, then their spiritual strength, learning, austerity, and reputation dwindles and ultimately vanishes. Although these aberrant devotees sometimes concoct ideologies to support their pretentious conduct, Srimad-Bhagavatam rejects such pretentious religion, called abhasa-dharma, as being but a dim reflection of real varṇāśrama.

Narada Muni advises us not to change our varna or asrama just because we are in a situation of difficulty or because another situation promises greater happiness. He emphasises the importance of being satisfied with whatever material circumstances our fate unfolds and of improving our spiritual standing by mature Krsna consciousness practice. He then quotes the famous verse that emphasises the power of material and spiritual satisfaction:

*sada santusta-manasah*  
*sarvah sivamaya disah*  
*sarkara-kantakadibhyo*  
*yathopanat-padah sivam*

**"For a person who has suitable shoes on his feet, there is no danger even when he walks on pebbles and thorns. For him, everything is auspicious. Similarly, for one who is always self-satisfied there is no distress; indeed, he feels happiness everywhere."**

Narada warns devotees who follow the dictates of their senses, that even if they enjoy every attainment in the world, they will never be satisfied. The only path that leads to happiness is the one paved by the cobblestones of fidelity to Krsna's instructions. Not only did Krsna devise the varṇāśrama system, but He manifested the varnas and asramas from His own universal form:

*vipra-ksatriya-vit-sudra*  
*mukha-bahuru-pada-jah*  
*vairajat purusaj jata*  
*ya atmacara-laksanah*

**"In Treta-yuga the four social orders were manifested from the universal form of the Personality of Godhead. The brahmanas appeared from the Lord's face, the ksatriyas from the Lord's arms, the vaisyas from the Lord's thighs and the sudras from the legs of that mighty form. Each social division was recognised by its particular duties and behaviour."**

Since the varnas are so intimately connected with the Lord, it behooves Krsna's devotees to serve Him according to their activities and qualities. That is ya atmacara-laksanah.

## Chapter Twenty-Six. Duties of Sudras and Vaisyas

In a society of devotees, the Vedic tradition that sudras should not reside and accept gurukula training does not apply, the reason being addressed by the principle of yasya yal laksanam proktam—where the qualification for education and initiation is found, it should not be denied. In the greater varṇāśrama society that Srila Prabhupada envisaged for the world, where people of a worker's nature are not Vaisnavas, then the Vedic system holds, once again for the same reason—there is no qualification for spiritual training.

Gurukula training will be described in the chapter elaborating on brahmacarya-asrama. One point to be noted here is that such training for devotees is not solely to study scriptures, but to imbibe the missionary mood and purpose of the Krsna consciousness movement. Devotees must be trained in the mission that Srila Prabhupada has bequeathed us, the mission which is the underlying cause for establishing varṇāśrama, and the foundation for all of our society's activities. Maintaining our spiritual tradition and disciplic lineage depends upon devotees understanding and taking ownership of the mission throughout the generations.

The tendency in Kali-yuga is for people to shirk their duties and responsibilities. For example, servants abandon a master who has fallen on hard times, masters abandon incapacitated servants even if the two have been related for generations, and cows are slaughtered when they can no longer give milk.

To offset such irreligious conduct, people must be educated in the twenty-one qualities as well as in the duties of nonviolence, truthfulness, honesty, care for others, and freedom from lust, anger, and greed. Krsna wonderfully discloses how He reciprocates with those who care for others, especially for saints:

*samuddharanti ye vipram*  
*sidantam mat-parayanam*  
*tan uddharisye na cirad*  
*apadbhyo naur ivarnavat*

**"Just as a ship rescues those who have fallen into the ocean, similarly, I very quickly rescue from all calamities those persons who uplift brahmanas and devotees suffering in a poverty-stricken condition."**

It is certainly everyone's duty to secure their own welfare, for without it there is no question of executing life's goal. The senses, mind, and life-air should be in a fit condition in order to pursue spiritual truths. Nonetheless such maintenances should be accomplished within the scope of prescribed activities that correspond to one's varna and with the things, endeavours, process, and living place appropriate to that status of life. Varṇāśrama forbids one to maintain oneself, or others, by means that are not prescribed for one's nature. This is an inviolable principle.

However, that principle is inviolable as long as there is no emergency. The material world always hosts exceptional circumstances therefore varṇāśrama also recognises that sometimes it is not possible to act according to one's varna. But even then, even in emergencies, one cannot do just anything.

A brahmana can adopt the duties of a merchant or of a leader, but he cannot accept the role of worker in someone else's employ. Krsna calls such degradation sva-vrtti, the occupation of a dog. In an emergency a king can act either as a vaisya or brahmana, with the provision that he never beg for a living. The vaisya is not barred from accepting the occupations of a worker, and if a sudra has no master, he can adopt some simple means of sustenance like making straw baskets and mats.

But crisis situations are temporary, and so when circumstances normalise then every person must return to their natural occupation, even if it is not as lucrative as the one in an emergency. Krsna pronounces this principle of *krcchran mukto na garhyena vrttim lipseta karmana*, "All members of society who have adopted inferior occupations in emergency situations must give up those substitute occupations when the difficulties have passed."

Unfortunately a characteristic of people in Kali-yuga is that even in the absence of an emergency, they will consider any degraded occupation quite acceptable.

When devotees keep to their prescribed duties and worship Krsna in regulated devotion, their hearts become purified and they gradually realise their constitutional position as eternal servants of the Lord. The process of daiva-varṇāśrama is to dedicate one's duties to Krsna in loving devotion. Krsna concludes His instructions on varṇāśrama to Uddhava saying, "Those who are followers of this varṇāśrama system accept religious principles according to authorised traditions of proper conduct. When such varṇāśrama duties are dedicated to Me in loving service, they award the supreme perfection of life."

**Sudras**

In commenting on Lord Kapila's instruction that devotees remain faithful to both their prescribed duties and to a regulated sadhana, Srila Prabhupada lists the duties of the four orders. Of the sudras he says, "The sudras, or labourer class, serve the higher classes because they themselves are not very intelligent." The context of Srila Prabhupada's phrase "because they themselves are not very intelligent," is similar to the way he sometimes used it in relation to women. The meaning is: "Their nature is not to be independent, therefore they should be under superior guidance and protection."

The inclination of a modern man, even of a devotee, will most likely be to rebel against this classification: not very intelligent. Such reaction, however, clouds devotees' ability to properly situate themselves, impels them to adopt an artificial status, and impedes Srila Prabhupada's varṇāśrama mission. To recognise one's status as a sudra requires humility and an unmotivated appreciation of the institution of varṇāśrama. These are two qualities of a sudra: amaya, being without duplicity, and go-vipra-raksana, a protector of brahminical culture.

Like the other three social orders, the order of sudra is exalted by the standards of today's misguided humanity, which mistakenly thinks itself at the apex of human civilisation. Conditioned thus, even we devotees may think that taking the role of a sudra is beneath us. Of course that is not the case, and such a reservation is based on a material, instead of a spiritual, conception on life.

The material concept, the thesis, is to identify with the material body and claim that everyone can be materially equal. The spiritual concept, the antithesis, is to recognise that we are not this body but spirit soul, and while materially different we are spiritually equal. Varṇāśrama establishes a synthesis of these two trains of thought for those conditioned by bodily identification so that they may be elevated to spiritual realisation. This is done by organising society according to humans' varying material abilities while giving them equal access to spiritual practice. It is therefore childish to resent varṇāśrama for facilitating our liberation from a mistaken conception of life.

Our bodies are different because we have different karma and different natures! Therefore we look different and have different abilities. Varṇāśrama merely organises people of different abilities into different social roles. A sudra has the meritorious quality of being free of duplicity, meaning the tendency to pretend he is someone, or something, he is not. And a sudra also values the principles of varṇāśrama enough to know that he best serves himself and Krsna by performing duties according to his own nature. Indeed it is said that the conduct of a sudra is to offer obeisances to the higher sections of society, meaning that he is respectful of the greater social responsibility entrusted to others.

The overriding nature of a sudra is to be dependent on others for his occupation and maintenance. This lifelong guidance and shelter is best come by when acting in the capacity of a servant to one of the other three orders. Narada Muni thus says,

**"For the sudras the only duty is to accept a master from a higher social order and engage in his service."**

During the 2015 varṇāśrama seminars in Hungary, the general understanding of devotees was that being an employee was to be a sudra. And while it is true that a sudra is retained in the service of another, the sudras of varṇāśrama do not receive or work for a fixed salary that they then spend at whim. Real sudras are more self-restrained.

The duty of a sudra is to be satisfied with whatever remuneration he and his family receive for services rendered. Remuneration means food, garments, accommodation, and general care. It does not mean money. Why? Srila Prabhupada explains that sudras should not have the independence to bank or to use money because their tendency will be to squander wealth. While devotees may not spend their income on intoxication and sinful habits, they will tend to spend it on luxury items like cars and clothes, which may then lead to bad habits.

The lifestyle of a worker is to depend on the well-wishing maintenance of his master in both good times and bad. Traditionally servant and master remain together throughout generations and thus a bond of affection, like that between family members, develops. The considerate master keeps the servant satisfied in all respects, especially by providing them with first class prasada and clothing.

The relationship between master and servant should be one of mutual respect and one in which workers are actually inspired to show respect to the higher classes. This show of respect becomes a duty of sudras and is called sannati, which literally means "obedience." It is a duty not just of sudras, but of all varnas. Everyone is obedient to brahmanas, and brahmanas also have to be obedient to ksatriyas. However it is a quality that is especially emphasised for sudras.

The other duties of a sudra are cleanliness, freedom from duplicity, neither stealing nor lying, and participating in sankirtana whenever possible.

Since sudras may have to serve brahmanas, their standard of cleanliness must be to that standard, brahminical. While those who are unclean in habit are sometimes referred to as sudras because they are at the lower end of the worker status, in a society of devotees who are at the higher end there is no room for an impure lifestyle. That is daiva-varṇāśrama.

Freedom from duplicity and neither stealing nor lying are different facets of honesty. A servant must be trustworthy. For this reason workers highly value their reputation for trustworthiness. They treasure it as a distinguishing quality of their family tradition, a quality whose importance they pass on to every family member from generation to generation. In this way a family of workers becomes famous for their dependability, for their straightforwardness, and because they never steal even in time of need.

Obviously such exalted qualities have their root in spiritual advancement, which is possible only by chanting Hare Krsna. Devotees are sadhakas, and sadhana is based on both japa and sankirtana. Vaisnavas therefore chant sixteen rounds and regularly attend arati and other functions in which the maha-mantra is chanted congregationally. Although in ancient times sudras could not chant Vedic mantras, there is no such restriction with chanting Krsna's names, tatrarpita niyamitah smarane na kdlah.Indeed the basis of daiva-varṇāśrama is that all members, of all varnas and asramas chant Krsna's names at least out of duty, if not out of love.

Needless to say, sudras, like the other classes of society, are concerned for the welfare of cows and brahmanas, knowing that it is the very root of their own welfare.

The actual engagements of sudras are myriad. Because they assist the other sections of society with manual labour they can be engaged in work like collecting firewood for a brahmana's sacrifice, fighting on the battlefield with ksatriyas, or manufacturing clothing or ornaments for vaisyas. In these duties sudras have their specific field of work that assists, but does not take on, the duties of the higher orders.

In today's world the majority of people are employees and many work in the technology sector. And while both kinds of workers are sudras, their work does not promote the goals of daiva-varṇāśrama.

As already mentioned, the system in which an employer's only responsibility to his employees is to provide a salary, is not dharmic. It leaves the employees at loose ends when it comes to utilising their salary, and that false freedom may well be the cause of their degradation.

Technology, like the communication industry, is not an industry that serves the necessities of life, but falls in the category of ugra-karma, work that degrades humanity. While daiva-varṇāśrama is agrarian based, it facilitates the kind of cottage and light industry that helps the sectors of society fulfil their duties. However, the manufacture of consumer products and the development of industries that promote sinful acts and waste time do not benefit anyone except Kali. For that reason devotees should avoid these lines of work and engage in beneficial work. That approach to work is revolutionary in itself and requires devotees to be dedicated to establishing the principles of varṇāśrama of which the religious minded sudras are the work force and the basis.

Although not a duty related to work but to their spiritual status, it is to be understood that all sudras should marry and be well situated in the grhastha-asrama. Under the care of their masters for life, when they are no longer able to work, sudras may retire to concentrate on spiritual practices, but they should continue to stay in their place of domicile as vanaprasthas, concentrating on deity worship and sankirtana.

**Vaisyas**

Srimad-Bhagavatam describes Kali-yuga as a time in which the acquisition of wealth is the prime impetus driving society. Sukadeva Gosvami says,

*vittam eva kalau nrnam*  
*janmacara-gunodayah*  
*dharma-nyaya-vyavasthayam*  
*karanam balam eva hi*

**"In Kali-yuga, wealth alone will be considered the sign of a man's good birth, proper behaviour and fine qualities. And law and justice will be applied only on the basis of one's power."**

The current global culture of capitalism trains its workers as low-class sudras, and industrialists as exploitative vaisyas. While both consider making money their primary duty, almost all wealth falls exclusively to the capitalist. Oxfam predicts that by 2016,1% of the world's population will have more wealth than the remaining 99%.

Srimad-Bhagavatam goes on to describe the Kali-yuga merchant as one who engages in petty commerce with the aim of cheating the rest of the world.This refers to the manufacture and marketing of consumer products, non-essential items that foolish people value more than the quality of the food they eat or the water they drink. This cheating business results in an artificial economy that eventually implodes upon itself, resulting in global chaos and suffering. That dangerous situation is now experienced worldwide.

Additionally, since today's merchants have no scruples regarding how to make money, they engage in and promote any degraded occupation as acceptable as long as it turns a profit. Krsna calls merchants who engage in unbeneficial works with which they contaminate the earth, demons. Pollution of all sorts, global warming, and raping the earth's resources result from the ugra-karma with which unscrupulous vaisyas enrich themselves. And these same capitalists lobby the world's leaders to gain the political and military clout that they need to attain their ends in the name of raising the standard of living.

Set against the backdrop of a capitalist system in which vaisyas have degraded into demons, let us review the duties of real vaisyas in daiva-varṇāśrama.

Maitreya Muni defines the economic duty of vaisyas in this way:

**"The means of livelihood of all persons, namely production of grains and their distribution to the prajas, was generated from the thighs of the Lord's gigantic form. The mercantile men who take charge of such execution are called vaisyas."**

This description makes clear that the primary duty of vaisyas is to ensure the livelihood of society—to provide the essential commodities needed to live. The most essential commodity is food, and so the vaisya s duty begins with agriculture and cow protection, and is followed by the steps needed for every section of society to receive the produce of the earth and cows. These steps would include transport, banking, and trade. Srila Prabhupada gives examples of different kind of vaisyas:

**"The vaisyas are divided into many subsections: some of them are called ksetri, or landowners, some are called krsana, or land tillers, some of them are called tila-vanik, or grain raisers, some are called gandha-vanik, or merchants in spices, and some are called suvarna-vanik, or merchants in gold and banking."**

Scripture generally emphasises vaisyas duties to be those that are the most intimately related with food production. In this vein Krsna also names ploughing, cow protection, and trade as the duties of vaisyas.Because their nature is to further the cause of religious sense gratification and economic development, vaisyas are creative and can think of many novel ways to make money. However, if their creativity takes them too far from the necessities of life then vaisyas can also create an unsustainable economy. For this reason the vaisyas are guided by their gurus, the brahmanas, and ksatriyas. They are not free to start enterprises that are artificial, that increase people's greed, and that damage the environment. The global production of consumer items is one such enterprise that has "run amok" and with which today's merchants wreak chaos.

How can those who are always endeavouring to make money be self-controlled in economic pursuits and faithful to religious principles?

By quality training in the ideals and practices of varṇāśrama.

Like brahmanas and ksatriyas, vaisyas are twice-born, which means that they are reborn through the reformatory system of studying the Vedas and being initiated with appropriate Vedic mantras. As twice-born they must reside in a gurukula during childhood and youth, where they study the Vedas according to their ability. The life of a disciple is dedicated to serving the spiritual master, from whom he learns to cultivate the twenty-one qualities and how to follow the limbs of regulated devotional service.

Up to twenty years of scripture and character training anchor the qualified vaisyas to the proper execution of religious principles, and in that way they enrich their social milieu and society at large. Student life under the guidance of a spiritual master further prepares vaisyas to continue their personal and business dealings under the soft-touch guidance of brahmanas.In this way the mercantile community prospers by protecting dharma.

For example, even though vaisyas bargain with customers and exaggerate the quality of their wares, they do not cheat. By not cheating we mean that vaisyas neither manufacture substandard merchandise nor sell inedible produce all to turn a profit. Instead they see themselves as mediums between the world's resources and those in need of them, and they charge an appropriate profit—maximum twenty-five percent—for their services. Moreover, vaisyas never create or enter into a black market of forbidden items just because there is such a demand. This self-restraint is the result of proper training and of adhering to religious principles.

Srila Prabhupada said,

**"You require some money to maintain yourself. So they will never be dishonest. Dishonest. In India still there are merchants, they would not take profit more than twenty-five percent, highest. There is no question of black market. 'Now, I purchased this for one dollar. Oh, I am getting demand. I must charge five hundred times.ẏ No. That is irreligious. There are... Everything there is prescribed, that you cannot take more than this profit. So there was no black market, because people were religious."**

Along with fulfilling their own ambitions for economic development, vaisyas remain concerned for the welfare of others and thus they consider that giving charity is their religious duty. To those who are in need, or to the brahmanas who dedicate their lives to self-realisation, the vaisyas donate profusely and establish rest houses, temples, and food distribution centres. Often merchant families or communities will take responsibility for the upkeep of such projects throughout generations as part of their family tradition.

But no matter how much they have given, vaisyas cannot accept or live by charity, which is the exclusive privilege of highly qualified brahmanas. That is forbidden according to the principle of sve sve 'dhikare ya nistha, which states that everyone must adhere to the prescribed duty lest they incur sinful reaction.

Since their livelihood is either directly or indirectly based on the service offered by cows and bulls, vaisyas consider cow protection their primary duty. More than just a religious duty, vaisyas see cow protection as an essential part of their spiritual life. So much so that even the most humble merchant would personally keep ten cows.All members of daiva-varṇāśrama are Vaisnavas and so they know how dear cows are to Krsna. Even if they have an occupation other than cow protection they still take personal responsibility for a small herd.

The final and most important duty of vaisyas is sadhana, which is based on deity worship, study of scripture, and nama-sankirtana. Many merchant families build a temple in their neighborhood so that they can benefit others with their deity worship. These devotees also arrange for regular sankirtana, the recitation of Srimad-Bhagavatam, observing festivals, and hosting Vaisnavas as a part of their spiritual practices.

Srila Prabhupada's own family were suvarna-vaniks, gold merchants, who came from the town of Saptagrama to settle in Calcutta, where his father took up the occupation of a cloth merchant. Srila Prabhupada often spoke fondly of his pure devotee father who worshipped deities, fed sadhus daily, and celebrated Ratha-yatra along with his little son. The wealthy Mulliks—from whom Srila Prabhupada descended—constructed the Harrison Road Radha-Govinda temple, and those Deities are being worshipped there to this day.

Srila Prabhupada credited his father with his entire Vaisnava upbringing, with the exception of writing books, which he learned from his spiritual master. Gaura-mohan De had his son learn to play the harmonium and mrdanga, gave him Radha-Krsna Deities to worship, taught him Srimad-Bhagavatam, and engaged him in festivals like Janmastami. This spiritual training was symptomatic of the general conduct of Vaisnava merchants one hundred years back, a conduct that His Divine Grace wanted his followers to emulate. By doing so devotees could change the world, as exemplified by the Mullik vaisyas' great contribution: Srila Prabhupada, Krsna's empowered ambassador.

## Chapter Twenty-Seven. Two Pillars of Society

The relationship between brahmanas and ksatriyas is a close knit one in as much as they are together responsible for giving society direction. The brahmanas give guidance and the ksatriyas give protection. The brahmanas are the head or mouth of the social body and the ksatriyas its arms. Although Krsna is the creator and guardian of the varṇāśrama social order He is not involved in its direct management and therefore He "creates brahmanas for the protection of the ksatriyas, and ksatriyas for the protection of the brahmanas."

In one sense administrators are almost as qualified as brahmanas, whose duties are studying the Vedas, worshipping the Deity, and giving charity, as well as teaching the Vedas, teaching deity worship, and accepting charity. The first three are compulsory, the second three optional. Narada Muni says that in the absence of any other means of livelihood, a qualified ksatriyas can engage in the first five activities although they are forbidden to live by or collect charity.

Additionally brahmanas and ksatriyas excel the rest of society in the depth of their common personal qualities, which are nonviolence, truthfulness, honesty, the desire for others' welfare and freedom from lust, anger, and greed.

Ksatriyas and brahmanas are both twice-born, or dvija, since, like vaisyas, they receive second birth through a sequence of purificatory rites culminating in initiation. Both spend the first phase of their lives in gurukula to learn the Vedas and both are trained in their corresponding varṇāśrama duties. However, because the brahmanas are more adept at learning scriptures they may receive initiation at an earlier age than their ksatriya counterparts—twelve to the latter's fourteen.

Moreover, while both learn the arts of performing sacrifice and both learn begging on behalf of the guru, when they leave gurukula, only the brahmanas will actually perform public fire sacrifice and live by charity, although ksatriyas continue to perform sacrifices for themselves, but only in their home. Of course the sacrifice of chanting Krsna's names knows no such distinction, and so devotees of all varnas may lead kirtana, what to speak of participate in it.

Participation by all sections of society in sankirtana not only unites the varnas but also protects them from the contamination of material casteism. In supporting the sankirtana movement, which includes kirtana, festivals, food distribution, temple construction and bhagavata-katha, everyone has a specific role corresponding to their inclination for service.

The brahmanas give direction on the spiritual standards laid down by the acaryas. Administrators provide both the facilities and organisation to realise sankirtana projects. The merchants provide the necessary ingredients and articles, and the sudras offer manual labour, artistic skill, and handicrafts. This cooperation by the four social classes benefits all participants equally, and creates an atmosphere of auspiciousness for the people of the world.

While ksatriyas and brahmanas must all attend gurukula as brahmacaris, because they are intricately involved in the affairs of society and because of their passionate nature, ksatriyas must enter the grhastha-asrama at the completion of their training. On the other hand, brahmanas, who are in goodness and who do not feel the need to marry, may remain brahmacaris or adopt the vanaprastha- or sannyasa-asramas if deemed so qualified by their guru. But when they reach the age of fifty, both grhastha-brahmanas and ksatriyas must adopt the vanaprastha-asrama according to the rule of pancasordhvam vanam vrajet.Additionally, brahmanas may also take sannyasa at any time when their superiors so concur.

Before studying the individual duties of the brahmanas and ksatriyas, let us examine their relationship, which includes their all-important duties to each other and to society.

Co-operation between the two leading sections of society is key for collective peace, prosperity, and spirituality. Thus the ksatriyas and brahmanas are the pillars of happiness for human society and have been created by the Lord to protect each other as well as to protect themselves. Srila Prabhupada gives Maharaja Asoka and Lord Buddha as examples of such co-operation. Because the king supported the sage, Buddhism spread all over India and from there to neighbouring countries of Asia.

The protection afforded by administrators means ensuring the overall welfare of the intellectual class who in turn offer protection in the form of their enlightened guidance for governance. This constructive interaction between brahmanas and ksatriyas provides an atmosphere in which the vaisyas and sudras also automatically flourish.

It should be remembered that not only leading heads of state are ksatriyas, or in the case of ISKCON and MKTHK, not only its leading managers. Ksatriyas are present at all levels of society's administration, and where they are present, they should take guidance from learned brahmanas. Thus the classical organisation of varṇāśrama suggests that all levels of ISKCON's administration should be guided by appropriate brahminical councils that are made up of impartial and learned brahmana-Vaisnavas.

In Vedic times the brahmanas were society's ultimate guardians, for they would wisely and selflessly guide kings in righteous governance so as to create and maintain a perfect welfare state. Srimad-Bhagavatam describes how kings would receive guidance from a council of brahmanas, which ensured that governance was neither blind nor autocratic. And the brahmanas' guidance was based on scriptures like Manu-samhita, and so there was, in Srila Prabhupada's words, "no need for less intelligent persons to manufacture a code of law in the name of democracy."

Even committed devotees may find this governance of varṇāśrama to be controversial by modern democratic standards. Nonetheless it was an ideal that Srila Prabhupada wanted his followers to intelligently and carefully pursue. In summarising the governance that results between the co-operation of qualified ksatriyas and brahmanas Srila Prabhupada's words are most apt. His Divine Grace writes,

**The less intelligent mass of people have very little knowledge of their own welfare, as a child has very little knowledge of its future well-being. The experienced father guides the innocent child towards the path of progress, and the childlike mass of people need similar guidance. The standard welfare codes are already there in the Manu-samhita and other Vedic literatures. The learned brahmanas would advise the king in terms of those standard books of knowledge and with reference to the particular situation of time and place. Such brahmanas were not paid servants of the king, and therefore they had the strength to dictate to the king on the principles of scriptures. This system continued even up to the time of Maharaja Candragupta, and the brahmana Canakya was his unpaid prime minister."**

What happens when leaders do not listen to their council of selfless brahmanas?

The extreme answer to that question is told in the history of Maharaja Vena. When he disobeyed the brahmanas and created chaos in the land, they cursed him to die, and he did. Today's brahmanas do not have the same mystic powers as brahmanas of Vena's time, however the principles of dharma and the consequence of their neglect remain valid. Therefore ksatriyas know that if they disobey the guidance of their advisors, their duration of life, reputation, righteousness, and opulence will be lost. The principles of dharma, as already cited in this book, are infallible.

*dharma eva hato hanti*  
*dharmo raksati raksitah*

**"Dharma destroys those who disobey dharma and in so doing strive to destroy it, but dharma protects those who follow dharma and strive by all means to protect it."**

In conclusion, the leaders of society would not dare to disobey the guidance of brahmanas, fearing the consequences of not following religious principles.

For brahmanas to remain impartial and bold in advising ksatriyas, they cannot be dependent or employed by anyone. Therefore the tradition of varṇāśrama is for ksatriyas to give charity freely to brahmanas, charity that would include cows, bedding, land, and even wives if needed. In that way brahmanas' maintenance was provided for by the state on the understanding that brahmanas remained fully dedicated to the material and spiritual welfare of society and had no time to make a living for themselves. Moreover, while ksatriyas collected taxes from subordinate ksatriyas, vaisyas, and sudras, they would not tax the brahmanas who lived only by the bare necessities of life and distributed whatever excess charity they received.

The degradation of the brahminical order took place, and can again take place, when brahmanas simply enjoy the tax-free charity they receive without performing their own religious duties. Such freeloaders are called brahma-bandhus, or disqualified brahmanas, and are the cause of the subsequent degradation of the brahminical order and of society at large.

When the governing order observe that brahmanas are becoming corrupt, it is their duty to either correct fallen intellectuals or banish them from society. This is the exalted position of ksatriyas: they are empowered to supervise the conduct of even brahmanas. That is why, as stated earlier, ksatriyas must be almost as qualified as their intellectual counterparts.

Overall the exchange between ksatriyas and brahmanas is a very harmonious one in which brahmanas would visit the king's court and the ksatriyas would visit the sages' hermitage. At these times administrators would place issues of state before brahmanas for their guidance along with requests for spiritual instruction. And if the brahmanas were disturbed in the execution of their duties or lacking in basic necessities, they would request the administrators to resolve their problems and provide their needs. Srila Prabhupada cites this principle of protecting each others' mutual self interest as ksatram dvijatvam ca parasparartham.

If conditions become dire for either ksatriyas and brahmanas and they cannot live by their prescribed duties, then it is permissible to accept alternate work. Brahmanas can adopt the occupation of merchants, or if that is not possible, then that of a ksatriya as in the case of Dronacarya. However, brahmanas were forbidden to enter into the employment of another and adopt the profession of a sudra, also known as the occupation of a dog, sva-vrtti.Similarly, under conditions of emergency, a ksatriya may take on the profession of a merchant, a hunter, or a brahmana, but not that of a sudra.The twice born must select a livelihood that does not compromise their independence, which is completely absent in the work of a sudra. Moreover, being subordinate to another obstructs ksatriyas and brahmanas from returning to their natural duties when the state of emergency has passed.

The problems of Kali-yuga have been exasperated because intellectuals and administrators have become dependent employees who are subject to a process of democracy in which the selfish merchants and bewildered sudras determine the direction of society by majority vote.

Thus over the ages the guidance for and leadership of society fell into the hands of the brahma-bandhus and ksatra-bandhus, who are blind to the purpose of life and the duties of their profession. As a result, society has no direction, and by acting contrary to the principles of varṇāśrama the world is becoming depleted of its resources and people's lives made miserable by sinful habits, over-taxation, senseless wars, and economic chaos.

It is in this atmosphere of hopelessness in which Srila Prabhupada was eager to introduce the perfect means to attain social stability and general happiness by introducing varṇāśrama. But to do so Srila Prabhupada first wanted some of his followers to become qualified as brahmana advisors and ksatriya administrators by being suitably trained in a varṇāśrama college. Such a college is very much the need of the day.

## Chapter Twenty-Eight. Duties of Ksatriyas

While talking with Svayambhuva Manu, the brahmana Kardama Muni described the varṇāśrama social system as being designed by the Supreme Lord. The actual words the sage uses are bhagavad-racita. The import of these words is identical with Krsna's own instruction to Arjuna, *tasya kartaram api mam*, meaning "I am the father and creator of the varṇāśrama system."

In his commentary on the phrase bhagavad-racita, Srila Prabhupada points out that because Krsna creates varṇāśrama, it is eternal and cannot be destroyed by anyone. That means that varṇāśrama always exists either in its original form or a distorted one. Both the caste system and modern materialism are but different distortions of varṇāśrama, because the four varnas and asramas exist in some guise in both. Although differently named, intellectuals, leaders, merchants and workers are to be found in every society, whether they are advanced or primitive.

The same can be said of the Krsna consciousness movement: the social and spiritual orders also exist here. Establishing varṇāśrama means that the rights and obligations that correspond to both the varnas and asramas are clearly spelled out and then followed. Establishing varṇāśrama is an unfolding of what is already there, rather than introducing something new.

As explained in the previous chapter, the brahmanas and ksatriyas are the leaders of society. The brahmanas may be likened to the caretakers of Krsna's social constitution and ksatriyas the executors. Vaisyas and sudras follow the direction of the two higher sections of society, so they are followers.

Because leaders and followers are differently qualified and have different duties, they are not materially equal and their social roles are spoken of in terms of "higher" and "lower." However, such convention is solely meant to maintain clarity in the functioning of the social body. No such distinction is made spiritually: whether male or female, householder or renunciate, worker or intellectual, everyone is spiritually equal in Krsna's eyes, and their social roles do not restrict or effect their devotion to the Lord in any way.

*mam hi partha vyapasritya*  
*ye 'pi syuh papa-yonayah*  
*striyo vaisyas tatha sudras*  
*te 'pi ..anti param gatim*

**"O son of Prtha, those who take shelter in Me, though they be of lower birth—women, vaisyas \[merchants\] and sudras \[workers\]—can attain the supreme destination."**

The Purusa-sukta of the Rg-Veda describes the appearance of the brahmanas and ksatriyas from the Lord's universal form with the prayer, brahmano 'sya mukham asid bahu rajanyah krtah. The verse says,

**"The brahmanas emerged from the face of the Lord and the ksatriyas from His arms."**

The power of protection, ksatra, is the innate potency of the Lord's arms and it manifests itself in the righteous kings who are then known as ksatriyas.

The primary purpose of the ksatra principle is protecting the brahmanas, although such protection is also afforded to all other members of society, which includes animals. In the way that the arms are naturally inclined to protect the head of a body, the ksatriyas are naturally inclined to protect the brahmanas who are the head of civilisation. And while the ksatriya order is generally identified with kings, the administrative order is not restricted only to heads of state. All executives who are responsible for the welfare of others must be qualified ksatriyas, even if they are not trained in martial arts or military tactics.

Because ksatriyas are a manifestation of Krsna's potency of protection, Vedic culture reveres them as representatives of the Lord. This is especially the case with kings and even more so with the emperors of the world like Yudhisthira Maharaja.

To do justice to the respect given them, administrators, like brahmanas, must be a transparent medium to the Lord. In short, ksatriyas had to be qualified as representatives of Krsna. Qualification was acquired through purificatory rites like garbhadhana-samskara, through the transmission of family traditions, and through the gurukula training compulsory for all twice born.

Although ksatriyas are said to be under the mode of passion, Vaisnava administrators always strive for the mode of goodness; in that sense some may be in the mixed mode of passion and goodness, while others more advanced may be in the mode of goodness. And perfected beings like the Pandavas are situated in the mode of transcendental goodness. Krsna calls such exemplary leaders rajarsi, or saintly kings.

In gurukula, the administrative class should be taught Vaisnava philosophy from Srimad-Bhagavatam along with devotional arts like deity worship. And in order to be able to fulfil the duties unique to ksatriyas, they must learn the principles of law, governance, and defense. Being trained as both pure Vaisnavas and wise administrators, ksatriyas would be true to the honorific title of rajarsi.

As an incarnation of the Lord's governing potency, Prthu Maharaja was an example of a rajarsi, one that devotee administrators would do well to study carefully.The king's dedication to the Lord, to the Vaisnavas, and to the process of devotional service was immovable, even while he faithfully fulfilled his other administrative responsibilities. This spiritual foundation actually enabled Prthu to execute his kingly duties as if they were his devotional service, while also keeping in mind his ability and finances, as well as time, place, and circumstance.

Prthu Maharaja also studied and understood the mentality of his subjects and was able to satisfy them by his pleasing words, attitude, works, and gentle behaviour. He was not a tyrant, but a gentleman. And yet the king was so powerful that none dared disobey him, knowing well that he was completely intolerant of injustice as much as he was of irreligion. Of Prthu Maharaja the Bhagavatam says that in thoughtfulness he was like a lion, in affection for dependents like Svayambhuva Manu, and in his ability to control like Lord Brahma.

In summary, Prthu Maharaja was an example of the Vedic maxim yasmin vijnate sarvam evam vijnatam bhavati: if one makes Krsna consciousness their first priority then they will become adept in all other requisite duties.

**The Duties of Ksatriyas**

The previous chapter described the close-knit relationship between ksatriyas and brahmanas, and the mutual co-operation that must exist between them to afford the rest of society the peace, prosperity, and spiritual guidance they deserve. From this it is obvious that ksatriyas prime duty is to conduct their affairs in co-operation with brahmanas, and while doing so to always defer to brahminical opinion if it conflicts with their own.

The spiritual duties of ksatriyas are akin to that of brahmanas, and yet, unlike brahmanas and except for times of emergency, ksatriyas are not meant to make their livelihood from those duties. In other words, while ksatriyas should perform sacrifice, including deity worship and study of scriptures, they should not maintain themselves by teaching these skills. And under no circumstance may they live by accepting charity. Ksatriyas are meant to rule kingdoms, govern villages, and manage ISKCON projects as their prescribed duty and means of livelihood.

Additional spiritual duties of leaders are practising devotional service, regulating the mind and senses, and serving Vaisnavas and brahmanas. Because ksatriyas are the leaders of society, their leadership must exemplify progressive Krsna consciousness. Without being Krsna conscious, administrative heads will invariably deviate from the path of virtue and mislead their wards. Krsna describes the results of both performing and not performing one's duty:

*yad yad acarati sresthas*  
*tat tad evetaro janah*  
*sa yat pramanam kurute*  
*lokas tad anuvartate*

**"Whatever action a great man performs, common men follow. And whatever standards he sets by exemplary acts, all the world pursues."**

*yadi hy aham na varteyam*  
*jatu karmany atandritah*  
*mama vartmanuvartante*  
*manusyah partha sarvasah*

**"For if I ever failed to engage in carefully performing prescribed duties, Î Partha, certainly all men would follow My path."**

Srimad-Bhagavatam narrates Krsna's glories by describing the saintly kings of the world. Devotees who study the conduct and character of great kings like Yudhisthira Maharaja, Pariksit Maharaja, and Prthu Maharaja will learn how to become exemplary leaders for the rest of society.

Having touched on the spiritual duties of ksatriyas, let us more thoroughly investigate their occupational duty—their leadership.
