It should be noted that the classic example of ksatriyas described by scripture are monarchs—from emperors to small vassal kings. At present, however, the Krsna consciousness movement in Hungary is far from being a state. It is a religious society in which, following Srila Prabhupada's will, leaders are the ecclesiastic GBC secretary, temple presidents, and department heads. For these leaders to embody the qualities and duties of true ksatriyas, they must carefully adopt the essential principles that apply to leaders of varṇāśrama states. In this way we can incorporate the appropriate religious duties within the administrative structure of the Krsna consciousness movement.

The primary duty of a ruler or administrator is to give protection to his dependents. Protection takes many forms, including giving shelter from physical harm, ensuring access to an unadulterated judicial system, safeguarding from poverty, disease, and famine, and assuring that the varnas and asramas of society are free of irreligious practices.

Krsna gives special encouragement to the protection of Vaisnavas and brahmanas in the following wonderful words:

*samuddharanti ye vipram*  
*sidantam mat-parayanam*  
*tan uddharisye na cirad*  
*apadbhyo naur ivarnavat*

**"Just as a ship rescues those who have fallen into the ocean, similarly, I very quickly rescue from all calamities those persons who uplift brahmanas and devotees suffering in a poverty-stricken condition."**

Obviously similar protection is extended to all parts of society. An administrator is like a father figure in that he is disposed to all of his dependents in the same way that he is disposed to his children. Krsna gives special emphasis to the importance of kings protecting themselves.Since a leader is the key to the welfare of others, he cannot neglect his responsibility to himself, which includes being secure politically, spiritually, militarily, and domestically.

A leader who properly executes his duty to his dependents gains such religious merit as to be elevated to the heavenly kingdom to enjoy alongside the king of heaven, Indra. While such rewards do not hold much incentive for Vaisnavas, they do show the benefit of a leader who fulfils his occupational duties.

A ksatriya's duties—which are misunderstood and wrongly practised in the world today—do not extend solely to the human species, but rather to every living entity, from animals, to trees, to the earth planet. A ksatriya is responsible for all living entities in his area of influence. Maharaja Pariksit was ready to kill the personality of Kali for abusing a cow and a bull, while Prthu Maharaja was ready to punish the personified earth for not providing grains to his subjects.

Thus the duty of a ksatriya extends beyond being expert in martial arts and the use of weapons. Ksatriyas must be learned in the duties of each varna and asrama so that he can ensure that all subjects, including brahmanas, are living according to the Lord's order. Proficient in the dharma-sastras like Manu-samhita, ksatriyas must also know legal procedure and the laws guiding mankind, as well as the corresponding redress and punishment that accrues from irreligious conduct.

Finally, as landowners or landholders, ksatriyas are meant to parcel their holdings to vaisyas in order to facilitate their respective occupations like agriculture, cow protection, or trade. And when brahmanas are in want of living facilities, ksatriyas are careful to ensure that they are provided with sufficient land and accommodation.

**Activities Forbidden for Ksatriyas**

Since the infrastructure of administration or government requires maintenance, ksatriyas are authorised to collect tax, which Srila Prabhupada earmarks at twenty-five percent of production and no more.In addition, a ksatriya has a right to levy customs duties and fines as appropriate. However, excess monies accrued above administrative costs should not be squandered for personal enjoyment, but should be used to host religious events like sankirtana festivals and public food distribution in addition to abundant charity—a mainstay for certain parts of society.

Brahmanas and brahmana-Vaisnavas are the most worthy recipients of charity. And because charity may be their means of sustenance, because they are the spiritual head of society and because they live only by the basic necessities, they are never to be taxed. This principle is so inviolable that it exempts even a materialistic brahmana living in opulence. Srila Prabhupada says,

**"The members of the Krsna consciousness movement should therefore be exempt from government taxation."**

In the way that a king cannot live by charity, the same can be said of all subordinate ksatriyas. Aside from charity not being their subsistence, charity can easily become bribery and a way of winning political influence, as corporations do today by donating to political causes. Thus, even in times of emergency ksatriyas should not beg for a living or accept employment and so become degraded to the status of a dog, sva-vrtti. A ksatriya is never meant to accept the position of a vaisya or sudra.

It should be noted that leaders in ISKCON and MKTHK cannot emulate the lifestyle of Vedic kings who, because of the great responsibility they bore, were allowed to live a luxurious life. Ours is a missionary movement and our leaders are also sadhus. And while they may require additional resources to facilitate their service, those resources are not meant to reflect palatial luxury but the principle of Vaisnava yukta-vairagya.

Other activities forbidden to ksatriyas are accepting the renounced order of life, animal sacrifices, and eating meat.

The renounced order of life is too exacting for those in the mode of passion and so generally it is reserved for brahmanas. Like Yudhisthira Maharaja, ksatriyas may accept vanaprastha, but not sannyasa.

There is a common misconception that ksatriyas are allowed to eat meat. However, Narada Muni clarifies that ksatriyas, especially Vaisnavas, should not do so. He says,

**"A person fully aware of religious principles should never offer anything like meat, eggs, or fish in the sraddha ceremony, and even if one is a ksatriya, he himself should not eat such things. When suitable food prepared with ghee is offered to saintly persons, the function is pleasing to the forefathers and the Supreme Lord, who are never pleased when animals are killed in the name of sacrifice."**

It may be noted that ksatriya culture allowed rulers to hone their use of weapons by hunting in the forest.Because scripture authorised such practice, the animals so killed were promoted to the human form of life and their flesh was given as food to the lower classes who ate meat.

History shows that the role of ksatriyas was sometimes taken up by qualified vaisyas and brahmanas.

Nanda Maharaja was a landholder on behalf of King Kamsa, and was highly qualified as the protector of the Vraja-vasis. However, he was a member of the agricultural community and kept a vast herd of cows. He is an example of a vaisya in the role of a ksatriya.

On the other hand Lord Parasurama was a brahmana, and because the ksatriyas of his time had become irreligious and corrupt, he single-handedly killed twenty-one consecutive generations of rulers. When the ksatriyas gave up the duties prescribed for them by varṇāśrama-dharma, they became brahmojjhita-pathas, in the words of the Bhagavatam, "persons going against the path of the Absolute Truth," and so they were candidates for punishment.Citing the Mahabharata, Srila Prabhupada says that the kings of Egypt originated in India, and they fled to the desert when Lord Parasurama took it upon himself to exterminate their king.

Scriptures also describe other administrative leaders who abandoned the duties of ksatriyas, such as the Pulindas of Greece and the Abhiras of Afghanistan, both of whose descendants later ruled India.Leaders who deviate from the duties of ksatriyas are known as ksatra-bandhu. Today's unqualified leaders, who have gained their posts by the wrongful voting system of democracy, are also examples of ksatra-bandhus.

The system of making decisions or electing an administrative head by majority vote is not inherently wrong. The defect of today's voting system is that the voters neither know nor follow the principles of religion and so they vote on the basis of speculation and sense gratification. In a body of Vaisnavas who are educated in the ways of varṇāśrama, majority vote may be an acceptable decision making process. That was how Srila Prabhupada set up the GBC.

Srimad-Bhagavatam predicts that leadership in Kali-yuga will be acquired by a show of strength, be that strength majority votes, atheistic education, military power, or sheer cunning. Whatever the case, the result is directionless leadership and a lost humanity. Sukadeva Gosvami says,

*evam prajabhir dustabhir*  
*akirne ksiti-mandale*  
*brahma-vit-ksatra-sudranam*  
*yo bali bhavita nrpah*

**"As the earth thus becomes crowded with a corrupt population, whoever among any of the social classes shows himself to be the strongest will gain political power."**

Srimad-Bhagavatam continues to describe the unfortunate plight of citizens under such irreligious rule: they will be harassed by excessive tax, drought, famine, war, and disease. To add to such distresses, the weather cycle will become irregular with excessive heat, cold, rain, and wind being the norm. Under such conditions, people's ability and will to live will wane and the duration of their miserable lives decrease.

To reverse both the misfortune of the world's population and the devastation of the environment, the Krsna consciousness movement must train qualified ksatriyas who can usher in a world order that echoes the prosperity and peace that can prevail under religious leadership, like that of Maharaja Yudhisthira.

In a Vaisnava state, ruled ideally by a monarch who oversees other qualified administrators, the climatic conditions become favourable and regulated, the earth produces man s necessities grains, vegetables, minerals and water in profusion, animals like cows are joyful because they are protected, and the human population is free of anxiety and disease.In short, the condition of the world is ideal for practising Krsna consciousness and going back to Godhead.

## Chapter Twenty-Nine. The Duties of Brahmanas

The hierarchy of the members of varṇāśrama is based on how much living entities dedicate their life to knowing and serving Krsna, samsiddhir hari-tosanam.Therefore, because the brahmanas give their lives to search after Krsna, they are considered the best of all other social orders. Obviously this evaluation is a generalisation and does not consider individuals' actual levels of spiritual advancement. For example, Narada Muni admits that the Vraja-vasis, as vaisyas, and the Pandavas, as ksatriyas, were more elevated than he, a brahmana. Nonetheless, as social considerations go, based on the spiritual merit of their occupation, brahmanas are deemed the best.

In describing the qualitative hierarchy of living entities ascending from animals to pure devotees, Lord Kapila discloses to his mother a further comparative analysis of different kinds of brahmanas:

**"Among human beings, the society which is divided according to quality and work is best, and in that society, the intelligent men, who are designated as brahmanas, are best. Among the brahmanas, one who has studied the Vedas is the best, and among the brahmanas who have studied the Vedas, one who knows the actual purport of Veda is the best.**

**"Better than the brahmana who knows the purpose of the Vedas is he who can dissipate all doubts, and better than him is one who strictly follows the brahminical principles. Better than him is one who is liberated from all material contamination, and better than him is a pure devotee, who executes devotional service without expectation of reward.**

**"Therefore I do not find a greater person than he who has no interest outside of Mine and who therefore engages and dedicates all his activities and all his life—everything—unto Me without cessation."**

Because of their dedication to realising the absolute truth and to helping others do the same, brahmanas—especially Vaisnava brahmanas—become worshipable for even Lord Krsna. Indeed, by their austerities, Vedic studies, and satisfaction, the bodies of brahmanas, the best of the twice born, become the counterpart of Lord Krsnaẏs body.Thus the brahmanas, who are said to manifest from the Lord's universal form, are the Lord's heart, and along with the cows are the special object of Krsna's affection. It is said,

*namo brahmanya-devaya*  
*go-brahmana-hitaya ca*  
*jagad-dhitaya krsnaya*  
*govindaya namo namah*

**"My Lord, You are the well-wisher of the cows and the brahmanas and You are the well-wisher of the entire human society and world.”**

Of the five kinds of defenseless creatures, the cows and brahmanas receive special consideration because of the Lord's special concern for them.Because of their full absorption in Krsna consciousness, which leaves brahmanas no time to make a living, the ksatriyas and vaisyas are meant to provide brahmanas with all the necessities of life. Moreover, because it is understood that being averse to sense gratification they live by the bare necessities of life and are not after any profit from their occupation, brahmanas are always exempt from any kind of taxation or customs duty.

As can be seen, every social order of varṇāśrama embraces the principle of respect and care for the brahmanas, which can be exemplified by how food is distributed at sankirtana festivals. Srila Prabhupada explains that the prasada of the Deity should be offered first to the brahmans and then to everyone else.

**Different Kinds of Brahmanas**

Historically Brahmanas did not all follow the same religious practice: according to their faith they would be attracted to different parts of Vedic texts and the different paths those texts prescribed. Narada Muni indicates that these religious paths corresponded to the follower's asrama.For example, householders were often attached to fruitive rituals, vanaprasthas to austerities, brahmacaris to studying the Vedas, and sannyasis to practising and teaching yoga—sometimes bhakti-yoga.

Followers of Srila Prabhupada are all Vaisnavas, and although their spiritual path is the same, bhakti, the nature of their devotion may partially reflect the practices of Vedic brahmanas. For example, grhasthas must invariably perform samskaras related to family life and also remain conscious of financial obligations, thus giving the appearance of being worldly minded. When those same householders retire, they become free of their earlier obligations and live a more austere lifestyle. And if the husband takes sannyasa, he will leave home to fully immerse himself in devotional service and become a preacher.

Similar variety in brahminical duty is seen when devotees' faith draws them towards a certain kind of activity, or when the needs of the society requires them to attend to a certain kind of service. Finally, in the course of his life and as a response to the dictates of circumstance, a brahmana may display many or all of those lifestyles independent of his asrama. Thus, since the duties of a brahmana may change or since different brahmanas have different duties, even daiva-varṇāśrama has different kinds of brahmanas.

Returning to the broader varṇāśrama system where everyone is not a Vaisnava, brahmanas' conditioned nature, svabhava, will draw them to different duties—from fruitive ritualistic ceremonies to pure bhakti. By association with devotees, transcendental knowledge will awaken in fruitive brahmanas and they will gradually give up their ritualistic ways and rise to the status of pure Vaisnava. Thus it will be seen that in varṇāśrama not everyone will adhere to the same type of religion, although they will be governed by a common all-encompassing code of conduct, which will also prohibit them from indulging in irreligious practices.The administration of that code of conduct will be the responsibility of the sage-like leaders.

Vaisnava brahmanas who are householders will find that the needs of their asrama challenge their ability to strictly follow religious duties. Nonetheless, brahminical culture mandates that they should adhere to the principle of dharmartham api neheta yatrartham vadhano dhanam which states that a poor man should not unnecessarily endeavor for economic development.Instead, a brahmana's life becomes auspicious by dependence on the Lord, as was the case for Sudhama Vipra.

Srila Prabhupada warns householder brahmanas to be careful of excessive family affection and obligation, both of which may drag them down from their exalted status.They should remain faithful to a lifestyle of simplicity and keep in mind a future in the retired or the renounced order of life.

As long as they are in the grhastha-asrama, brahmanas should stay pure by chanting the maha-mantra, serving Srila Prabhupada's mission, and offering charity to sannyasi-brahmanas. And even if their destiny offers them abundance, Vaisnava brahmanas should live by the simple necessities of life, avoiding excess as they would dangerous animals. Remaining free of personal desire, brahmanas should serve Srila Prabhupadaẏs mission with their consciousness absorbed in the Lord. Such purity of life will be made possible by constant engagement in sahkirtana, by studying Srila Prabhupada's books, and by distributing in charity either material possessions or wisdom in the form of Srila Prabhupada's books.

While a grhastha-brahmana may maintain his family by accepting charity, doing so is not a given, as we shall discuss later. But however a brahmana maintains himself, it cannot be by becoming someone's employee—at least not if he wants to be known as a brahmana and not as a sudra. The alternative to accepting charity for his services, which in the service of the institution is to receive maintenance, is to take to a vaisya's occupation of cow protection, agriculture or trade.

**General Duties**

Having described different kinds of brahmanas and especially the grhastha-brahmanas, let us review brahmanas' general duties, how they may accept charity, and those activities forbidden to them.

Lord Krsna describes the six duties of brahmanas in this way:

*ijyadhyayana-danani*  
*sarvesam ca dvi-janmanam*  
*pratigraho 'dhyapanam ca*  
*brahmanasyaiva yajanam*

**"All twice-born men—brahmanas, ksatriyas and vaisyas— must perform sacrifice, study the Vedic literature and give charity. Only the brahmanas, however, accept charity, teach the Vedic knowledge and perform sacrifice on behalf of others."**

Srila Prabhupada would often summarise these duties:

**"The engagements of a brahmana are yajana, yajana, pathana, pathana, dana, and pratigraha."**

Studying scripture, performing sacrifices (including Deity worship), and giving charity are compulsory for brahmanas, and teaching scripture, teaching Deity worship, and accepting charity are permissible but not compulsory if a brahmana chooses some other means of maintenance, like cow protection. Still, for Vaisnavas, Lord Caitanya's order of óare dekha, tare kaha 'krsna'-upadesa makes preaching compulsory, even if one has some other means of livelihood.

As far as studying and teaching scripture are concerned, the former is the primary aspect of the gurukula curriculum compulsory for all twice born. Scriptural study for brahmanas is geared towards their responsibility as guides for the other social orders, especially for the ksatriyas. Therefore brahmanas' study is extensive and deep, more so than the study of ksatriyas or vaisyas.

The very essence of the Krsna consciousness movement is preaching, and Brahmanas are meant to both disseminate knowledge of Krsna to people in general, and systematically teach our philosophy to devotees. Ultimately it is the brahmanas that are the guardians of Gaudiya Vaisnava siddhanta.

As spiritual guardians, Vaisnava-brahmanas must be careful not to stray into the five kinds of irreligious practices. They must also be alert to ensure that the other orders of society, as well as the wards whom they regularly guide, do not adopt these five deviant practices: religious principles that obstruct one's own duties, religious principles for which one is unfit, concocted religious systems, speculative interpretation of religious principles, and neglect of one's own duties. Needless to say, it is the duty of the ksatriyas to mete out punishment to those who stray into the realm of irreligion by not heeding their brahmana advisors.

In short, brahmanas are meant to be "expert in the spiritual advancement of society." And since their spiritual acumen makes brahmanas the natural teachers and spiritual masters of all orders of society, these services are their primary social responsibility. When ksatriyas and vaisyas are under the guidance of such qualified brahmanas, they are able to fulfil their respective duties and in turn the entire social system functions smoothly and efficiently.

The personal worship of Deities is compulsory for brahmanas, who are also authorised to worship temple Deities, or Deities on othersẏ behalf. The other orders of society may worship their own Deities, but they will not take the role of full time pujaris as they are not authorised to maintain themselves thus. Srila Prabhupada encouraged all Vaisnavas to worship either the salagrama-sila or some other Deity in their homes. That instruction is imperative for brahmanas.

Much could be said about the unsavoury practice of current so-called brahmanas who make a living by worshipping deities in temples, shops, businesses, and other places. They often neglect their duty to maintain proper pancaratrika standards because their employers are not willing, or not able, to provide adequate finance for worship. As a result these so-called brahmanas compromise their own duties and implicate in offenses those who they should be guiding to freedom from sin. Obviously such pujaris are victims of Kali-yuga and they are able to continue with their degraded practices only due to a lack of qualified ksatriyas who would otherwise terminate their malpractices'.

And while Vedic sacrifices like the asvamedha or horse sacrifice, are beyond the scope of the most qualified of today s brahmanas, Vaisnava fire sacrifices and the all-important sankirtana-yajna are not. Purificatory rites such as the vivaha-yajna, the marriage ceremony are often accompanied by fire sacrifices in which pancaratrika-mantras, like the vaisnava-homa, are chanted and oblations of ghee are offered into the fire. While all Vaisnavas may learn and perform these sacrifices, it is the duty of brahmanas to be proficient in them and be able to conduct such ceremonies when and as necessary.

Sukadeva Gosvami suggests that brahmanas also be proficient in directing the members of society to sacrifice the products of their work, as well as a portion of their possessions to the Lord. To the verse concluding with *dravyatmakah karma vitana-yogah*, Srila Prabhupada adds that brahmanas should guide sudras in offering their work and vaisyas their produce in the sacrifice of chanting Krsna's holy names.Following the direction of our acaryas as represented by Srila Prabhupada's instructions for ISKCON, brahmanas should direct their wards in supporting and participating in ISKCON's preaching activities. That sankirtana-yajna is the most prominent and all-pervasive sacrifice for this age.

Krsna also prescribes this dravya-yajna or dravyamaya-yajna in the Bhagavad-gita.In today's world it is commonly known as charity. In India, rich vaisyas and pious ksatriyas provided facilities to benefit others materially and spiritually by constructing and maintaining hostels, free kitchens, schools, and temples. Elsewhere this dravya-yajna takes the form of hospitals, old age homes, and schools. Devotees are inclined to support a variety of activities including food distribution, festivals, book-distribution, and temple projects. Brahmanas should guide their wards as to what is most suitable for the donor and for the society. That is also how brahmanas teach and engage others in sacrifice.

**Accepting Charity**

In addition to guiding others how and when to give charity, brahmanas are also personally connected in receiving and distributing it. The general livelihood of a brahmana is to live by donations received for teaching and guiding others. These donations are known as daksina.

Should a brahmana request daksina if it is not offered? And is there a limit as to how much daksina a brahmana should accept?

Emphasis of the brahmanas' exclusive dependence on Krsna and their indifference to sense gratification should preface the answers to these questions. About brahmanas Krsna says,

*brahmanasya hi deho 'yam*  
*ksudra-kamaya nesyate*  
*krcchraya tapase ceha*  
*pretyananta-sukhaya ca*

**"The body of a brahmana is not intended to enjoy insignificant material sense gratification; rather, by accepting difficult austerities in his life, a brahmana will enjoy unlimited happiness after death."**

Krsna further describes the brahmana as one who keeps himself free of personal desire and follows noble religious principles. To remain in such an exalted state, brahmanas know well that they must abandon the two causes of material entanglement—the desire for prestige and money.

In answer to the two earlier questions, Vedic culture is unambiguous: a brahmana should never teach for a fee, and of the unsolicited daksina he receives for his services he should retain only as much as is needed to keep body and soul together. Excess should be distributed in charity to others. Those brahmanas who ask for remuneration have effectively adopted the role of a sudra and so disqualify themselves.

Srila Prabhupada sets a further criterion for a brahmana to accept charity: he must be "very pure."In other words, unless a devotee is fully qualified with qualities of austerity, satisfaction, and simplicity, he will become distracted by the apparent ease with which he can earn a charity-based livelihood. This distraction may well lead to deviation, and from deviation to sinful reaction. As will be explained later, this was how varṇāśrama began its degradation into the caste system.

And yet we see that Srila Prabhupada inspired his followers to support the Krsna consciousness movement by accepting charity in exchange for his books. Did that not compromise those devotees?

The answer is no! Srila Prabhupada applied the age-old tradition described by Narada Muni:

*sri-narada uvaca*  
*brahmacari guru-kule*  
*vasan danto guror hitam*  
*acaran dasavan nico*  
*gurau sudrdha-sauhrdah*

**"Narada Muni said: A student should practise completely controlling his senses. He should be submissive and should have an attitude of firm friendship for the spiritual master. With a great vow, the brahmacari should live at the guru-kula, only for the benefit of the guru.”**

This principle of brahmacari life, in which the student lives under shelter of the gurukula dedicating everything to the guru—including collected charity—, was applied by Srila Prabhupada to brahmacaris and brahmacarinis, as well as to grhasthas. In other words, Srila Prabhupada arranged for devotees to collect charity on behalf of the institution, in exchange for which they would be maintained with the minimum necessities of life. Thus the charity collected was the property of the institution, which also arranged for the devotees' livelihood. Devotees laid no claim to anything they personally received and in that way, whatever their level of brahminical purity, they were a transparent medium to Srila Prabhupada and to his ISKCON.

In summary, the most assured way for brahmanas to live by charity is to teach, preach, sell books, or collect donations on behalf of MKTHK without laying claim to or taking any of that charity. In turn a brahmana simply accepts whatever maintenance the Society has to offer them. That was the standard set by Srila Prabhupada. The alternative of living independent of the management and oversight of MKTHK requires devotees to be very pure, and is a lifestyle that had no precedent in Srila Prabhupada's time.

Śrī Krsna confirms that there is an inherent danger in brahmanas living by charity. Their austerity, spiritual influence, and fame may be compromised even by teaching and performing sacrifice, what to speak of by begging.Why? Because excessive dependence on others and on charity may compromise a brahmana-Vaisnava's full dependence on Krsna. Therefore, in the case where a brahmana does not consider himself qualified to live by charity and yet he lives independent of the institution, the Lord says that such a devotee may maintain himself by collecting rejected foodstuffs, or as already suggested, by trade, cow protection, or agriculture.

Since brahmanas accept only the basic necessities of life or even embrace a vow of poverty, the charity they receive may exceed their needs. In that case their duty is to give the excess in charity. Once again, such donation is best offered to the society, or its representative, an elevated brahmana-sannyasi. Following this conduct, brahmanas fulfil their responsibility of both accepting and giving charity in ways that both enhance their spiritual life and set an exemplary role model for other sections of society.

Other duties of brahmanas are to further cultivate those qualities that originally make them eligible for their varna. Control of the mind and senses, cleanliness, forgiveness, mercy, and truthfulness are matured through good association, scriptural study, and real-life situations. But the most effective cultivation of these qualities is by practising devotional service and surrender to the Lord.

**Brahmanas' Asramas and Means of Sustenance**

As far as which asrama to enter, Srila Prabhupada says that a brahmacari-brahmana "generally remains a brahmacari throughout his entire life."If he is pure hearted, if he understands the futility of family life, and if he has mastered his senses, a brahmacari, upon completion of his studies, could easily remain a brahmacari or, says Narada, he may accept the vanaprastha- or the sannyasa-asramas. And if a life of celibacy is too great a challenge for a brahmana, then he may marry and fulfil his religious obligations in the grhastha-asrama.However, once having accepted the grhastha-asrama he must plan for renunciation at some future time. A brahmana should not remain a householder until death.

As far as accepting the renounced order, only brahmanas are considered sufficiently qualified in terms of mind and sense control. After all, sannyasis are the spiritual masters of all varnas and all asramas. But if a brahmana accepts the sannyasa-asrama and then again returns to hearth and home, he is considered shameless and likened to one who eats his own vomit, a vantasi. In this regard it is said,

*yah pravrajya grhat purvam*  
*tri-vargavapanat punah*  
*yadi seveta tan bhiksuh*  
*sa vai vantasy apatrapah*

**"One who accepts the sannyasa order gives up the three principles of materialistic activities in which one indulges in the field of household life—namely religion, economic development, and sense gratification. One who first accepts sannyasa but then returns to such materialistic activities is to be called a vantasi, or one who eats his own vomit. He is indeed a shameless person."**

To avoid such falldown it is advised that brahmanas accept the renounced order only when they are no longer attracted to women. And when they have accepted the renounced order, sannyasis should carefully avoid female association. The latter recommendation also applies to brahmacaris. For this reason close mixing of the sexes is forbidden in varṇāśrama and that prohibition is one of its underlying principles.

Another cardinal principle of varṇāśrama is that higher social orders cannot accept employment as their means of livelihood. This injunction is especially important for brahmanas who are meant to be neutral in social issues and independently thoughtful. Neutrality and independence requires that brahmanas fully represent the truths of scriptures and can speak the truth without being influenced by others. However, a brahmana who is in another person's employ becomes compromised because his livelihood depends upon the good will of his employer. Thus his primary religious principle is undermined and he is said to have fallen from his caste, because he no longer represents its qualities and duties according to the principle of *yasya yal laksanam proktam*.

Rupa and Sanatana Gosvamis were of a brahmana family, but because they accepted employment, and that in the service of a Muslim ruler, they were ostracised from brahminical society. While their exalted Vaisnava status remained unchanged, their social status was lowered.

By contrast, Canakya Pandita was the state Prime Minister of Maharaja Candragupta, and yet he refused to accept any remuneration or benefit for his services. He lived outside the city in a hut and maintained himself by accepting charity as befitting a brahmana.

Sometimes devotees misconstrue a brahmana's freedom to speak the truth as independence from any social structure or social restriction. That is obviously a misunderstanding when we consider that brahmanas are part of varṇāśrama society, and that their status is dependent upon following prescribed duties. Independent thinking does not mean anarchy, just as it does not mean mental speculation. What it does mean is that brahmanas do not compromise their ability to speak the truth by being financially dependent on those whom they are meant to guide.

We should add that ideally the maintenance provided for brahmanas by the institution is not subject to the direct control of a manager, in the same way that the charity given to brahmanas in a kingdom is not under the direct control of the king. In that way brahmanas can enjoy the shelter of service to the society without prejudicing their social status.

Brahmana-Vaisnavas in Hungary may engage in the service of MKTHK and accept the subsistence that is offered to them by the institution. This was the standard set by Srila Prabhupada and is maintenance worthy of a brahmana. Preachers, teachers, pujaris, and cooks—those engaged in the service of brahmanas—should not work for a fixed salary, in the absence of which they would otherwise seek employment elsewhere. Such conditional service is not worthy of a brahmana. It compromises his dependence on the good will of Krsna and transforms him into a stipulatory merchant, instead of an unconditional benefactor

Scriptures forbid brahmanas from performing sacrifices like the horse and cow sacrifice in Kali-yuga. To these prohibited sacrifices Srila Prabhupada adds all Vedic sacrifices, and especially those of the karma-kanda category.These fruitive sacrifices require perfect intonation to bear a result, and because brahmanas in Kali-yuga are unable to meet the required standards, these sacrifices have not been performed for thousands of years. But devotees should not try to revive the practice: non-violence is a principle of varṇāśrama and thus brahmanas, especially Gaudiya Vaisnavas, would never consider indulging in sacrifices that cause suffering to others. Rather, the result of all sacrifices can be achieved by nama-sankirtana, and so brahmanas are recommended to perfect the pure chanting of Krsna's holy names and teach others the same perfection.

In every asrama, brahmanas must remain true to the conduct required of their varna. A brahmacari who abandons celibacy, a grhastha who compromises the regulative principles, a vanaprastha who participates in social activities, and a sannyasi who is addicted to sense gratification are all fallen. They should be reformed and encouraged to resume their original position, and if they are not thus amenable then they should be rejected, meaning they should be guided to a varna that corresponds to their conduct—if there is such a varna.

As already mentioned earlier in this book, the democratic system of governance—in which sudras, as a majority of the population, vote in leaders who then make laws to please their constituents—is a childish game at best, one that undoubtedly degrades into chaos and wickedness. Of the general mass of people Srila Prabhupada says, "The less intelligent mass of people have very little knowledge of their own welfare, as a child has very little knowledge of its future well-being."While Vaisnavas certainly know that their welfare is in serving Krsna, still sadhu, sastra, and guru illuminate that service. Thus devotees are also not independent of authority and this is where the leadership of ksatriyas and the guidance of brahmanas combine to assist devotees in perfecting their human form of life. In other words, the governance of a varṇāśrama society by ksatriyas needs to be guided by advice of brahminical councils.

The leadership structure of ISKCON—comprising the GBC body, its secretaries, temple presidents, department heads and nama-hatta leaders—is relatively well practised throughout the Society and throughout Hungary. However, that structure lacks the balance of a brahminical council, of which Srila Prabhupada wrote often. In one instance he says,

"According to the system of varṇāśrama-dharma, the pious and learned brahmanas were the natural guardians of society. The brahmanas, by their learned labour of love, would instruct the administrator-kings how to rule the country in complete righteousness."

The membership of the brahminical council was not made up of self-appointed brahmanas. Srila Prabhupada makes the qualifications of such members quite clear: they should be advanced devotees who are equally disposed to men and women, young and old, humans and animals. Such brahmanas are not representatives of any interest group, nor are they in the employ of any administrator. They dictate to leaders on the strength of scriptures because they receive no salary for their service. Ideally they should be self-realised souls.

Obviously such brahmanas would have to be educated in the religious duties of leaders and the principle of leadership that are enunciated by scriptures like Manu-samhita and Srimad-Bhagavatam, as well as by our acaryas and Srila Prabhupada. An advisory council composed of such highly qualified brahmanas can give sound guidance to our society's leaders who would welcome such assistance to their grave responsibility of managing and leading the devotee community.

Such brahmanas may be rare, yet Srila Prabhupada's vision was that ISKCON train devotees to this calibre.Varṇāśrama began its degradation into the caste system when brahmanas who were not properly trained and who lacked the prescribed qualifications accepted financial support from greedy administrators with no respect for brahminical culture. These disqualified brahmanas, brahma-bandhus, exchanged a life of serving the truth for one of enjoying comfort. They became sycophants of their masters, and manipulated the codes of dharma to suit unscrupulous politicians. Naturally the other orders of society quickly followed suit, and so today all varnas and asramas have abandoned the life of social and spiritual progress.

In Treta-yuga almost all people were brahmanas, and m Dvapara-yuga, ksatriyas and brahmanas combined were most numerous.But in this age even qualified sudras are hard to find, and therefore establishing varṇāśrama is dependent on training qualified brahmanas and re-establishing brahminical principles. While all parts of a body serve their unique purposes, the head, being indispensible, is the most important. Similarly, training clear-thinking and uncompromised brahmanas is a priority in establishing varṇāśrama. Srila Prabhupada says,

"Comparatively, the head is the most important part of the body, for it gives directions to the other parts of the body. Similarly, the ideal state functions under the directions of the brahmanas, who are not personally interested in political affairs or administration because they have a higher duty. At present this Krsna consciousness movement is training brahmanas. If the administrators take our advice and conduct the state in a Krsna conscious way, there will be an ideal society throughout the world."

## Chapter Thirty. Determining One's Varna

Introducing and elaborating on varṇāśrama as the social structure of His own design, Krsna, in the Bhagavad-gita, emphasises the importance of varna by not explicitly mentioning the asramas or the duties associated with them.That does not mean that asramas are not important: it means that for the understanding and implementation of varṇāśrama, varna comes first and asrama second. Social stability means that devotees are well situated in their varna and asrama, and that they diligently stick to the duties characteristic of both. Srila Prabhupada said,

**"First of all varna. And asrama, then, when the varna is perfectly in order, then asrama. Asrama is specially meant for spiritual advancement, and varna is general division. It must be there in the human society, or they're on the animals. If varna is not there, then this is a society of animal. And when the varna is working perfectly, then we give them asrama. Varṇāśrama. That is later on."**

Readers may take note that the principle of "varna first, asrama later" also means that as individuals, devotees should not make plans to determine or to establish their asrama until their varna is clear. One reason is that varṇāśrama dictates that a man not marry a woman of a higher varna, lest compatibility conflicts compromise the stability of the marriage. Another is that only brahmanas are recommended to stay celibates—brahmacaris or sannyasis.

For these reasons, establishing and remaining steady in one's varna is so important. However, Srila Prabhupada often complained that his American disciples were restless and that they did not stay at one duty for long.But adherence to duty is a quality of a Vaisnava. Indeed, the Visnu Purana defines a Vaisnava by their adherence to varna: "Please know that a Vaisnava is one who does not abandon the duties of his varna, who is equal to all, who is a friend even to his enemies, who never steals anything or harms anyone, and who is peaceful at heart."

Narada warns that while one may think that there is some economic or social gain in adopting another occupation, such irreligious practice will become the cause of fall down.

*pandita bahavo rajan*  
*bahu-jnah samsaya-cchidah*  
*sadasas patayo 'py eke*  
*asantosat patanty adhah*

**"O King Yudhisthira, many persons with varied experience, many legal advisers, many learned scholars and many persons eligible to become presidents of learned assemblies fall down into hellish life because of not being satisfied with their positions."**

But how is a devotee's natural varna determined? How does a devotee know his religious duty so that he does not transgress into an occupation for which he is not fit?

This chapter gives the traditional principles by which individuals and their seniors were able to answer that question in ages past and which will help them do the same today.

**Purification of Individuals' Nature**

At the outset readers must keep in mind that determining varna in this age, like everything in Kali-yuga, is not a black and white affair. The first handicap is expressed in the words janmana jayate sudrah, "by birth everyone is a sudra,"and Srimad-Bhagavatam clarifies that this is not because at birth we are babies, but because in Kali-yuga "people \[by nature\] are almost all sudras and barbarians," sudra-dasottarah prajah. Not a rosy beginning.

Practical observation shows, however, that there is some inclination towards an occupation in even the most materialistic person. Today's educational system does not teach in accord with students' inclinations, although such inclinations are there. While education is often based on the latest technological craze, we see that some students are gifted with an inclination towards, for example, music, mathematics, art, or learning. And we also see that other students have no interest in acquiring any skill and quickly leave school to work or to do nothing.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura says that people's nature needs to be purified in order to answer the question what is my varna?"This purification takes place through three agencies: samskaras, training, and devotional service.

The janmana jayate verse continues to say *samskarad dhi bhaved dvijah*, "by purificatory activities one becomes twice-born," veda-pathad bhaved viprah, "by study of the Vedas one becomes learned," and brahma janatiti brahmanah, "by devotion to the Supreme Lord one becomes a brahmana." Here again we see the three kinds of purification: samskaras, education, and bhakti, as we do in Narada's introduction to varṇāśrama in Srimad-Bhagavatam.

In short, the purification by which devotees can ascertain their varna and asrama, along with developing attachment to devotional service, is called brahminical training. In his conclusion to Śrī Krsna-samhita, Thakura Bhaktivinoda lists seven kinds of cultivation that fall into the category of educational and devotional purification. They are:

1\. Spiritual cultivation: meaning education in the relationship, practice, and goal of bhakti.

2\. Mental cultivation: practising the five kinds of meditation as well as repentance, fearlessness, and the corresponding mental aspects of the twenty-one qualities.

3\. Bodily cultivation: including service to others, engaging the senses in devotional service, chanting japa, going on pilgrimage, and the bodily aspects of the twenty-one qualities.

4\. Cultivation of speech: most notably kirtana, praying, and preaching.

5\. Cultivation of relationships with devotees and ultimately with the Lord-which obviously begins with perfecting servitude.

6\. Social cultivation: which begins by observing inclinations toward both varna and asrama as they become more manifest, and when clear then education in the relevant social and spiritual roles. Added to this is training on how to conduct oneself in an assembly of devotees, at festivals, and during sacrifices.

7\. Cultivation of sense objects: the eyes, ears, nose, tongue, and sense of touch should be engaged in pure items, such as for the sense of touch, the company of a chaste woman to create a God-centred family. This also includes auspicious times and places.

While the above list may appear demanding, most of its aspects are included in a very purposeful and conscientious approach to the sadhana and lifestyle that Srila Prabhupada gave his followers.

In response to the question, "How long should devotees wait to determine their varna and what should be the benchmarks of their purification?", our suggestion is that the three kinds of purification should be realised in these ways:

Samskaras: Devotees should have at least first (but if possible second) initiation, and should have taken a vow of brahmacarya or of marriage.

Education: Bhakti-sastri degree and counselor supervision.

Devotion: Regular temple attendance and/or home morning program for two years, some steady service under MKTHK guidance, and cultural practices like wearing devotional clothes at home and in the temple.

**Determining Varna**

The method for evaluating an individual's varna is a collective one: the individual should not conclude their evaluation by self-analysis alone. Bhaktivinoda Thakura describes the age-old tradition in this way,

**"The ancient custom was that when a boy attained maturity, the elderly family members, the family priest, the family acarya, the landlord, and the leaders of the village would consider his nature and then ascertain his varna. The main consideration in ascertaining a boy's varna was to analyse whether he was qualified to accept the same varna as his father."**

The essence to be gleaned from Bhaktivinoda Thakura's instruction is that a devotee's varna should be determined with the help of senior Vaisnavas who are well conversant with the individual. The consideration in determining varna is nature, meaning the qualities that dominate an individual's personality and conduct, in addition to his inclination towards a certain kind of occupation.

As an aside, the Thakura's dosing statement dearly indicates that in a righteous family that strictly adheres to samskaras, like garbhadana, the varna of the child will generally be the same as that of the father. In this regard Krsna says,

*varnanam asramanam ca*  
*janma-bhumy-anusarinih*  
*asan prakrtayo nrnam*  
*nicair mcottamottamah*

**"The various occupational and social divisions of human society appeared according to inferior and superior natures manifest in the situation of the individual's birth."**

Nonetheless, the rule of yasya yal laksanam proktam necessitates that the son of a brahmana, for example, must display the actual qualities of a brahmana. If he does not, then the subordinate principle enunciated by Krsna is superseded, and the son of a brahmana must adopt the varna for which others deem him qualified.

Let us return to the means by which senior Vaisnavas will help one evaluate one's varna.

The third part of this book describes both the qualities and duties of the varnas. Based on the outstanding qualities of each varna, we have created an online varna-finder as a tool to that end. The finder, and an explanation of how it works and what its method of assessment is, can be found at the following link: vacompendium.com.

Other traditional indicators to help confirm varna were horoscope, and in the case of a baby, the grain-giving ceremony.Both of these may be applied today.

We hope that given suitable purification, the varna- finder and the guidance of this book, devotees' social occupation can be determined, knowing which they may be provided with further training in their chosen vocation.

To ensure further vocational training is the duty of the leaders of society and Srila Prabhupada's explicit desire. His Divine Grace writes,

**"A leader should train the people as brahmanas, ksatriyas, vaisyas, and sudras and engage them in various occupational duties, thus helping them progress toward Krsna consciousness."**

**Varṇāśrama College**

Srila Prabhupada did not just establish the principle for varna training, but he gave the vehicle as well. It is called the Varṇāśrama College. The Varṇāśrama College is meant to be open to all, devotee or non-devotee, provided they follow the regulative principles and chant Hare Krsna.

As the gurukula is for children, so the college is for teens and adults. College applicants may have a varna inclination which would be assessed and cultivated. In the case where such inclination has not manifest, it is determined at the College and the individual trained accordingly. And of course the training is not just in the skills of the vocation within the four varnas, but also in the corresponding natural qualities, religious duties, social rights, and devotional practices. Interestingly, Srila Prabhupada envisaged that the Varṇāśrama College would only be for men, and that women's varṇāśrama training would take place in the home. All of the above should be borne in mind by the administrator's of MKTHK's Bhakti College, which is the natural vehicle for fulfilling Srila Prabhupada's ideal.

Srila Prabhupada s expectation—which shows the scope of his vision for varṇāśrama and its relationship with ISKCON—was that a Varṇāśrama College be established wherever ISKCON had a centre.

**“The varṇāśrama college has to be established immediately. Everywhere, wherever we have got our centre, a Varṇāśrama College should be established to train four divisions: one class, brahmana; one class, ksatriya; one class, vaisya; and one class, sudra. But everyone will be elevated to the spiritual platform by the spiritual activities which we have prescribed."**

The concept of the Varṇāśrama College is deeply entrenched in Vedic culture, and its education is prescribed in scripture as a facility that is unique to the Earth planet:

*asminn eva varse purusair labdha-janmabhih*  
*sukla-lohita-krsna-varnena svarabdhena karmana*  
*divya-manusa-naraka-gatayo bahvya atmana anupurvyena*  
*sarva hy eva sarvesam vidhiyante yatha-varna-vidhanam*  
*apavargas capi bhavati.*

**"The people who take birth in this tract of land are divided according to the qualities of material nature the modes of goodness \[sattva-guna\], passion \[rajo-guna\], and ignorance \[tamo-guna\]. Some of them are born as exalted personalities, some are ordinary human beings, and some are extremely abominable, for in Bharata-varsa one takes birth exactly according to one's past karma. If one's position is ascertained by a bona fide spiritual master and one is properly trained to engage in the service of Lord Visnu according to the four social divisions \[brahmana, ksatriya, vaisya and sudra\] and the four spiritual divisions \[brahmacarya, grhas- tha, vanaprastha and sannyasa\], one's life becomes perfect."**

**Conclusion**

To conclude on the topic of determining devotees' varna, we should recognise at least three groups of candidates: children, devotees who have done years of voluntary service, and devotees who have not gone through temple training, the donors.

For children, especially those born to devotees according to Vaisnava samskaras, determining varna and training them will be the most straightforward of the three. And since they have minimal bad habits and no financial or social obligations, children will find it easiest to follow their varna—provided they are suitably protected from unfavourable materialistic influences. It should be noted that children's varna will be determined after their sixteenth year.

Devotees who were volunteers could choose to be either missionaries or donors. Past experience shows that although these devotees are handicapped with a non-devotional background, they have little if any financial or social obligations. Once their varna is established they are relatively unencumbered in following their occupational duty.

Donors who have already committed themselves to an occupation and possibly to family life may discover with the help of senior Vaisnavas that their varna is actually different from that of their vocation. If this is the case, devotees should neither rashly give up their current occupation nor reject the findings of their varna-advisors and thus create confusion in their spiritual lives. Rather, with the guidance of advisors they should try to conform to the other duties and qualities of their inherent varna, while maintaining strong spiritual practices. In Srila Prabhupa- da's words,

**"One cannot suddenly give up a profession to which he is accustomed, and therefore it is recommended that one gradually be awakened."**

Let us summarise on how the different kinds of devotees can put to practice the knowledge of their varna:

**For devotees who are raised in the Society from childhood and for those youth who became volunteers, living according to their varna will be quite natural. The determining factor in their choice is whether the principle of following their varna supersedes the example and pressures of the confused world around them. Will they be satisfied to be what they are, and with the lifestyle that comes from being what they are? It will depend a lot on their training.**

For devotees who have joined Krsna consciousness and have family commitments and an occupation, living according to their varna may or may not be what they are already doing. If not, changing occupation may or may not be possible, necessary, or even desirable. However, no situation obstructs devotees from embracing their varna by following the appropriate samskaras, purification, cultivation, and duties. Introducing varṇāśrama starts by devotees accepting its principles, even if implementation poses practical difficulties. And if their occupation cannot be in line with their varna, devotees who accept the principles of the varṇāśrama social system and do their best to adhere to them will be advocates and facilitators of their children doing so.

In any case, sincere devotees who try their best to fulfil Srila Prabhupada's varṇāśrama vision, and yet who cannot fully do so practically, will never incur sin or impiety. They will be protected by the strength of their devotion, which acts as a spiritual bridge to help them cross the gap of their material shortfall. Krsna says,

*ananyas cintayanto mam*  
*ye janah paryupasate*  
*tesam nityabhiyuktanam*  
*yoga-ksemam vahamy aham*

**"But those who always worship Me with exclusive devotion, meditating on My transcendental form—to them I carry what they lack, and I preserve what they have."**

## Chapter Thirty-One. Mixed Varnas

As an introduction to daiva-varṇāśrama, this book focuses on the four occupational duties of devotees. Scriptures detail these varnas in terms of the three modes of nature: goodness, passion, passion mixed with ignorance, and ignorance.

When living entities come in contact with the modes of nature, they become conditioned by those modes and, according to the various choices they make in life, that conditioning becomes varied. In this way the inherent nature, svabhava, of a living entity evolves and is remoulded birth after birth while exhibiting corresponding psychological qualities, or gunas, that result in behavioural characteristics, karma. The natural social structure of varṇāśrama then places living entities into one of the four varnas, based upon their qualities and inclinations for work. There are common as well as unique duties and rights for the four varnas, and these duties and rights comprise the laws underlying varṇāśrama.

The special feature of daiva-varṇāśrama, the varṇāśrama for Vaisnavas, is the power of devotional service practised by its members. Sadhana-bhakti invokes the mode of goodness: thus, while devotees' residue karma may still impel them to different occupations, they are simultaneously qualified to perform the activities of brahmanas. This is the brahminical lifestyle of Vaisnavas, which, if strictly followed, enables them to actually rise to the mode of goodness and, by full purification of the heart, to the mode of transcendental goodness. Evolution to transcendence through varṇāśrama is the essence of this book.

**Mixed Castes**

Because residual karma for devotees is a reality, the modes of nature that dominates them are accepted as a relevant consideration in the life-long services or occupations that Vaisnavas adopt. However, experience has shown that determining the varna of devotees is not a black and white affair.

Why?

One obvious reason is that little is clear in the murky waters of Kali-yuga. But more to the point: because devotees do not have clearly formed natures.

In the traditional varṇāśrama culture, where purity of lifestyle was the social norm, peoples' natures were clearly defined by the three modes—goodness, passion, passion and ignorance, and ignorance. The result was four distinct varnas, and the corresponding occupational duties and religious codes were equally lucid.

However—and we should note—, Hungarian devotees do not come from a time-honoured varṇāśrama culture. Instead we come from a society that varṇāśrama would and did consider untouchable—cow killers and worse. As a result of a hedonistic background, devotees' natures are far from self-evident. Consequently, devotees are fashioned by a countless combination of the modes, with the result being anything but clarity of varna. Is it any surprise, then, that devotees would experience uncertainty in understanding their social status.

Not at all! During the 2015 introductory varṇāśrama seminars in Hungary, this ambivalence was very much on display. One exercise in the seminars grouped devotees together to determine each other's varna. The experiment was designed to evaluate how much devotees understood the qualities that determined varnas and how willing they were to apply that knowledge. Among the lessons learned, one was that many devotees found difficulty in assessing their social status according to varṇāśrama principles. Devotees found that their natures and inclinations indicated the traits of multiple varnas—two, three, or even four.

That would have been expected from new devotees who had little by way of self-purification. However, serious devotees of many years standing expressed a similar confusion. And considering our background, that observation would also be expected. With the rare exception, we are of mixed castes.

Let us stop for a moment to address the question of whether mixed castes exist in a varṇāśrama society.

The answer is "Yes!"

When there is intermarriage between the varnas and hence a mixing of the mother's and father's natures, the resultant offspring act under mixed modes, and thus they are known as mixed castes. Of them Narada says,

*vrttih sankara-jatinam*  
*tat-tat-kula-krta bhavet*  
*acauranam apapanam*  
*antyajantevasayinam*

**"Among the mixed classes known as sankara, those who are not thieves are known as antevasdyi or candalas \[dog-eaters\], and they also have their hereditary customs."**

The mixed classes are of two kinds: anuloma, where the man marries a woman of lower class, and pratiloma, where he marries a woman of a higher class.Strictly speaking, only the anuloma marriages were considered part of a varṇāśrama society, while the pratiloma would generally result in such sinful offspring and so were unacceptable to varṇāśrama—parents and children would be outcasts.

Readers no doubt know that a person's qualities are more significant in recognising varna than is birth. And history bears witness to examples of highly qualified souls born of both kinds of mixed marriages, like Suta Gosvami. However, exceptions aside, in a proper varṇāśrama system the general rule is that birth is an indicator of qualities and of varna. That is why Krsna says, varnanam asramanam ca janma-bhumy-anusarinih..., "The various occupational and social divisions of human society appeared according to inferior and superior natures manifest in the situation of the individual's birth."From this we may recognise the importance of the marriage institution and its underlying principles in establishing the varṇāśrama system. The subject matter of asrama will be the content of the second volume of the Varṇāśrama Compendium.

Having glanced at mixed castes arising from mixed marriages, let us return to how devotees resolve their varna identity with a past legacy that is characterised by a jumble of modes Let us conclude on the status of devotees who feel that they are of a mixed caste.

The simple answer to a not-so-simple question is as follows: first devotees should assess whether they have undertaken adequate purification. Once they have done so, senior Vaisnavas who know them well should help determine their dominant mode and subsequently their corresponding varna—one of the four varnas. And finally devotees may then adopt the occupational duties that accompany their varna, being assured that they have done their best to contribute to a new social revival.

We should remember that the introduction of varṇāśrama is not just about us, but also about generations of devotees to follow. As varṇāśrama is gradually introduced, its effects will become more pronounced and recognition of the varnas more obvious. In the meantime devotees must make the best of current realities and, in Srila Prabhupada's words, their identities should, "... gradually be awakened."

Srila Prabhupada wrote of mixed castes by the name of kayasthas, who are also mentioned in the texts of Śrī Caitanya-caritamrta.His elaboration on the kayas- tha community give us a hint of how the remnants of varṇāśrama—from Lord Caitanya's time to today—deal with mixed castes. History shows that kayasthas comprised many mixed castes, some honoured almost as brahmanas, others as ksatriyas, still others as vaisyas, and yet still others as sudras. Ramananda Raya and Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami are just two of the great devotees who came from kayastha families that were regarded as sudras.

We in Hungary should follow the same principle: whatever mixed caste we are in, we should find the nearest varna and identify with that. That enables us to establish varnas among devotees, something that was Srila Prabhupada's explicit desire and order. Whatever awkwardness devotees feel from what they perceive as a compromise will be offset by resolute devotional practices and Krsna's blessings, *yoga-ksemam vahamy aham*.

Srimad-Bhdgavatam emphasises the power of devotional service, especially of devotee association, in elevating people from mixed classes to an unmixed class:

*suta uvaca*  
*aho vayam janma-bhrto 'dya hasma*  
*vrddhanuvrttyapi viloma-jatah*  
*dauskulyam adhim vidhunoti sighram*  
*mahattamanam abhidhana-yogah*

**"Śrī Suta Gosvami said: 'O God, although we are born in a mixed caste, we are still promoted in birthright simply by serving and following the great who are advanced in knowledge. Even by conversing with such great souls, one can without delay cleanse oneself of all disqualifications resulting from lower births.'"**

A final question to be answered at this point is "Who is not part of varṇāśrama?"

Lord Krsna gives the answer to Uddhava:

*asaucam anrtam steyam*  
*nastikyam suska-vigrahah*  
*kamah krodhas ca tarsas ca*  
*sa bhavo 'ntyavasayinam*

**"Dirtiness, dishonesty, thievery, faithlessness, useless quarrel, lust, anger, and hankering constitute the nature of those in the lowest position outside the varṇāśrama system."**

In short, devotees who decline to follow the duties of their varna and asrama, mixed or unmixed, are classified as being outside the varṇāśrama social system. And they may not expect the same rights as those who follow varṇāśrama, for they are unwilling to make the social contribution characteristic of their natures.

But devotees outside varṇāśrama can be reinstated if they again undertake the process of purification. Acts of purification begin by adopting proper conduct and cultivating good qualities, followed by observing social duties, engaging in services like harinama-sankirtana, book distribution, and giving charity, and finally by keeping good Vaisnava association.

Purification is also essential for devotees within the scope of varṇāśrama. Following samskaras, keeping strict sadhana, serving Krsna and His society, and regular scriptural study will ensure that devotees realise their material and spiritual goals. Of these acts of purification, the most important is chanting Krsna's names. Manu says,

**"In this age the chanting of the Lord's holy names can enable one to take full advantage of the pancaratrika and vaidika purificatory systems."**

In conclusion, devotees who take shelter of Lord Krsna in the form of His holy names while trying their best to fulfil their prescribed varṇāśrama duties are free of sin and beyond reproach. Dedicated to fulfilling Srila Prabhupada's desire to re-establish varṇāśrama, these devotees are respected by the demigods and, as told by Yamaraja, they never suffer the pangs of death or rebirth.

*jihva na vakti bhagavad-guna-namadheyam*  
*cetas ca na smarati tac-caranaravindam*  
*krsnaya no namati yac-chira ekaddpi*  
*tan anayadhvam asato 'krta-visnu-krtyan*

**"My dear servants, please bring to me only those sinful persons who do not use their tongues to chant the holy name and qualities of Krsna, whose hearts do not remember the lotus feet of Krsna even once, and whose heads do not bow down even once before Lord Krsna. Send me those who do not perform their duties toward Visnu \[the duties prescribed by the varṇāśrama system\], which are the only duties in human life. Please bring me all such fools and rascals."**

## Part Four. Concluding Words

## Chapter Thirty-Two. Asrama, Economy, and Family Tradition

This, the fourth part of this book, is its summary and conclusion. Here we shall briefly detail some important points mentioned earlier and re-emphasise the goal of inaugurating daiva-varṇāśrama amongst devotees: to show the world the perfect social structure and to facilitate devotees' journey to loving devotion.

This introductory volume elaborates on twenty-one of the thirty qualities that Narada Muni sets out as character requirements for followers of varṇāśrama. It is also dedicated to a study of the four social divisions by defining them, by describing their qualities and duties, and by providing guidelines as to how devotees can identify their social class.

The purpose of this volume, and the volumes that will follow, is to provide members and leaders of MKTHK with a constitution for establishing varṇāśrama-dharma for the devotee community in Hungary. Putting into practice the knowledge in this volume will be the first step on the journey to varṇāśrama.

The contents of this book are just part of the framework of varṇāśrama, and as in any framework, each part is connected to the other. So the qualities of humans and the duties of the varnas are related to other aspects of varṇāśrama. Some of these aspects are the asramas, economy, and family tradition. While each of these topics will be the subject matter of further volumes, they will be mentioned here briefly as a reminder to devotees that establishing varṇāśrama-dharma will not be achieved in one attempt, and solely with the knowledge in this book. Rather the research into, publications on, and application of varṇāśrama will most likely be a lengthy process. Completion will span generations. It has taken two generations just to get from Srila Prabhupada's order to this book. Social engineering, and that based on eternal spiritual principles, is no easy feat.

Indeed, the Varṇāśrama Compendium and the educational material that stems from it are the extract of varṇāśrama principles made relevant to Hungary today. This means that current application of the principles may differ somewhat from the way those same principles were applied five thousand years ago, what to speak of in Treta-yuga. Narada explains the time and place adjustment of varṇāśrama's application,

*prayah sva-bhava-vihito*  
*nrnam dharmo yuge yuge*  
*veda-drgbhih smrto rajan*  
*pretya ceha ca sarma-krt*

**"My dear King, brahmanas well conversant in Vedic knowledge have given their verdict that in every age \[yuga\] the conduct of different sections of people according to their material modes of nature is auspicious both in this life and after death.”**

Because current application of varṇāśrama is in uncharted waters, devotees can expect some adjustments in practical implementation of those principles over time Those adjustments will be made by the collective wisdom of the yatra's leaders, in response to the needs of the time and circumstance. Of course such adjustment will not change the fundamental principles of varṇāśrama, just as the laws of Hungary are regularly amended without contravening constitutional principles. Although he was immensely learned in the principles of varṇāśrama, as mentioned earlier, Srila Prabhupada wanted to spend time thinking about how he would practically implement it. The guidance to do so would have come to Srila Prabhupada from Krsna as it will also come to those who sincerely try to serve the desire of His Divine Grace. That is Krsna's promise to us as enshrined in the tesam satata-yuktanam verse.Thus we, the Kozgyules of Hungary, pray for the blessings of the Vaisnavas to be an instrument in fulfilling Srila Prabhupada's desire and to be a suitable receptacle for Radha-Syama's grace.

**Asrama**

The asrama half of varṇāśrama has been present in ISKCON almost since its inception. Of the four asramas, Srila Prabhupada introduced three into practice in his fledgling movement—brahmacarya, grhastha, and sannyasa. And while he wrote extensively on vanaprasthas, forty years after his disappearance only a few devotees identify with that order of life. No doubt it will manifest.

The Bhagavatam description of brahmacarya and of vanaprastha are a glaring contrast to those same asramas in ISKCON today.That contrast reflects the earlier mentioned difference in the time, place, and circumstance application of eternal varṇāśrama principles. And yet it would not be fair to say that the asramas of today live up to what Srila Prabhupada's expectations were of them, what to speak of what they would be in a fully functional varṇāśrama society. In other words, as in ISKCON, MKTHK's asrama standards fall short.

For example, in Srila Prabhupada's time all devotees received training in the brahmacarya-asrama. That is a principle of varṇāśrama enumerated by Krsna Himself,

*dvitiyam prapyanupurvyaj*  
*janmopanayanam dvijah*  
*vasan guru-kule danto*  
*brahmadhiyita cahutah*

**"The twice-born member of society achieves second birth through the sequence of purificatory ceremonies culminating in Gayatri initiation. Being summoned by the spiritual master, he should reside within the guru's asrama and with a self-controlled mind carefully study the Vedic literature."**

However gurukula, or temple training, is no longer a practice followed by all members of the devotee community. As a result the youth of ISKCON are not well trained in qualities like austerity, celibacy, and simplicity. They do not systematically study scripture, nor are they experienced in book distribution. Thus the principle ing only for the benefit of the guru or institotion for one s personal benefit, is often absent, the overall result being that the missionary spirit of our society is waning.

The de-emphasis of the brahmacarya-asrama-the foundation upon which devotees build their lives-is a drawback that must be recognised and rectified if we are to successfully establish varṇāśrama. Even if today's youth do not spend twenty years of training therein, they can dedicate five. That will give them the kind of substance upon which both a righteous and mission-dedicated grhas- tha-asrama can be built.

The most influential and common asrama in society is that of the grhasthas. It is open to all varnas, it is the economic motor of varṇāśrama, and it is the future hope of varṇāśrama in the form of children—the most significant legacy of the grhastha-asrama and one that deserves special consideration.

The success of varṇāśrama is intimately connected to the quality of its practitioners, a quality that was assured in the past by adherence to prescribed purificatory rites and samskaras. Today's society will also only be as good as are its members, notably those members who are born in the society. One factor that will contribute to the elevated quality of our offspring—meaning to their being both free of past bad karma and of a clearly defined nature—is for a marriage to be based on both the man and the woman being of the same varna. As Krsna says,

*grharthi sadrsim bharyam*  
*udvahed ajugupsitam*  
*yaviyasim tu vayasa*  
*yam sa-varnam anu kramat*

**"One who desires to establish family life should marry a wife of his own caste, who is beyond reproach and younger in age..."**

Since devotees' varna has not been a consideration in our society, this practice is not current. But because it is a fundamental principle of varṇāśrama it should gradually be introduced, and so will be discussed at length in the next volume of this series.

The vanaprastha-asrama has been mentioned as one that is still somewhat overlooked, but is important for all grhasthas who do not take to the renounced order of life. Varṇāśrama life and spiritual life cannot end amidst the trappings of family and home. There must be some movement of older grhasthas towards a higher degree of austerity and to dedicating quality time to Lord Caitanya's mission.

Narada Muni says that all human beings must be introspective enough to gradually take leave of unnecessary engagements.With regard to grhasthas this quality can be cultivated at the outset of household life. Devotees may plan to live in a way that facilitates retirement at the scriptural age of fifty, pahcasordhvam vanam vrajet, and again dedicate themselves full-time to deep spiritual practices and to serving Srila Prabhupada's movement.That is the application of gradually taking leave of unnecessary engagements.

This type of household planning will be essential for the success of varṇāśrama because it helps establish grhasthas lifestyle. In order to retire at the age of fifty with assets that will support them, grhasthas-without abandoning their own varna—may carefully determine their living standards and recognise, and be satisfied with, that which the Lord has allotted as their "set apart quota," tyakta.As this kind of forethought becomes the tradition, especially for aspiring and new grhasthas, family life becomes principled and increasingly harmonious with the values of varṇāśrama.

Mention has already been made of the vanaprastha-asrama and the need to adjust its role to ethical modernity. The word vanaprastha means "one who has gone, prastha, to the forest, vana." It is no surprise that few devotees gravitate to this order of life as very few are inclined, or would be able, to live in the forest—what to speak of in the wintry forests of the northern hemisphere. But the essence of leaving family life and dedicating oneself fully to spiritual pursuits should not be neglected. Srila Prabhupada set that example at the age of fifty-six when he travelled to Jhansi to dedicate himself to fulfilling the mission of his spiritual master.

Similarly, if following their plan as grhasthas, devotees dedicate themselves to Srila Prabhupada's mission as His Divine Grace did to his guru's, the varṇāśrama principles of their asrama will be more than honoured.

The last stage of life is the renounced order, sannyasa. Traditionally this order is reserved for brahmanas, but it can be taken up from any asrama provided that the suitable qualities of detachment, magnanimity and freedom from sexual attraction are present in a candidate. Srila Prabhupada emphasised that sannyasa was for preaching. Yet history has shown that being an enthusiastic preacher was not sufficient qualification to judge whether a devotee would remain in the renounced order. Many sannyasis who Srila

Prabhupada initiated fell down. However, over the last thirty years the GBC has mandated that candidates for sannyasa exhibit corresponding qualities, as a result of which no new sannyasis have fallen victim to illusion.

There remain, however, adjustments to be made in how the traditionally independent sannyasis who are the diksa and siksa spiritual masters of society, work within an institutional framework.One adjustment is the subordination of sannyasa and guru authority to leadership authority, and another is the transparent accountability of finances received by sannyasis. These adjustments to varṇāśrama tradition are much needed in order to make varṇāśrama harmonious with the leadership hierarchy established by Srila Prabhupada for ISKCON, and to prevent sannyasis and gurus, like the other asramas, from being victimised by the influence of Kali. Srimad-Bhdgavatam warns of the risks of degradation that the asramas face:

*avratd batavo 'sauca*  
*bhiksavas ca kutumbinah*  
*tapasvino grama-vasa*  
*nyasino 'tyartha-lolupah*
